and slowly varying function
L(\cdot)
, with
(a,b)
replaced by
(\alpha^{*}a,b^{{}^{\alpha^{*}}})
in Assumption
4
(c). Let
U\sim\pi,
and Stable-
\alpha
process
\mathcal{\bf{S}}_{\alpha,\beta_{j}}
be all mutually independent for each
j\in S.
As
t\to\infty,

(i)

if
E_{\pi}a(\cdot)<0,

\displaystyle\frac{\log|X_{t}|+tE_{\pi}a(\cdot)}{t^{\frac{1}{\alpha}}L_{0}(t)}\quad\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{\to}}\,\,\sum_{j\in S}\delta_{U}\big(\{j\}\big)\Big(\frac{1}{E|\mathfrak{I}^{j}_{1}|}\Big)^{\frac{1}{\alpha}}\sigma_{(j,\alpha)}\mathcal{S}_{\alpha}(1,\beta_{j},0).

(5.21)

(ii)

if
E_{\pi}a(\cdot)=0,b\neq 0
and Assumption
4
(c) is replaced by

\displaystyle E\Big[\Big(\log\int_{\tau_{0}^{j}}^{\tau_{1}^{j}}b^{{}^{\alpha^{*}}}(Y_{s})e^{-\int_{s}^{\tau_{1}^{j}}\alpha^{*}a(Y_{r})dr}ds\Big)^{\alpha+\epsilon}\Big]<\infty\quad\text{for some }\,\,\epsilon>0.

(5.22)

Then for a slowly varying function
L_{0}(\cdot)
as
t\to\infty,

\displaystyle\frac{\log|X_{t}|}{t^{\frac{1}{\alpha}}L_{0}(t)}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{\to}}\sum_{j\in S}\delta_{U}(\{j\})\Big(\frac{1}{E|\mathfrak{I}^{j}_{1}|}\Big)^{\frac{1}{\alpha}}\sigma_{(j,\alpha)}\sup_{0\leq u\leq 1}\mathcal{\bf{S}}_{\alpha,\beta_{j}}(u).

(D)

Supposse Assumption
1
holds and
a(\cdot)=a
(constant). Let
U\sim\pi,
and
\mathcal{S}_{\alpha^{*}}(1,0,0)
be a symmetric-
\alpha
stable random variable with scale
=1,
and they are independent. As
t\to\infty,
if

(i)

a<0
and
E\log^{+}\big[\int_{\tau_{0}^{j}}^{\tau_{1}^{j}}b^{\alpha^{*}}(Y_{s})e^{\alpha^{*}a(s-\tau_{0}^{j})}ds\big]<\infty,
then

e^{at}X_{t}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{\to}}X_{0}+\Big(\sum_{j\in S}\delta_{U}(\{j\})\big|\int_{0}^{\tau_{0}^{j}}b^{\alpha^{*}}(Y_{s})e^{\alpha^{*}as}ds+e^{\alpha^{*}a\tau_{0}^{j}}V_{j,\alpha^{*}}^{*}\big|^{\frac{1}{\alpha^{*}}}\Big)\mathcal{S}_{\alpha^{*}}(1,0,0),

where
V_{j,\alpha^{*}}^{*}
satisfies (
2.13
) with

(A,B)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{=}}\Big(e^{\alpha^{*}a(\tau_{1}^{j}-\tau_{0}^{j})},\int_{\tau_{0}^{j}}^{\tau_{1}^{j}}b^{\alpha^{*}}(Y_{s})e^{\alpha^{*}a(s-\tau_{0}^{j})}ds\Big).

(ii)

a=0,\,\,\&\quad E_{\pi}b^{\alpha^{*}}(\cdot)<\infty,
then
\quad\quad\frac{X_{t}}{t^{\frac{1}{\alpha^{*}}}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{\to}}\big|E_{\pi}b^{{}^{\alpha^{*}}}(\cdot)\big|^{\frac{1}{\alpha^{*}}}\mathcal{S}_{\alpha^{*}}(1,0,0).

Remark 5.3
.

(i)

Both Theorem
5.9
and Corollary
5.2
show that weak limits depend on the noise process
only
in the stable domain (i.e., when
E_{\pi}a(\cdot)>0
) and in the divergence domain when
\sigma_{j}^{2}=0
for all
j\in S
. However, in other divergent cases (B) & (C) (where
\sigma_{j}^{2}
is finite or infinite for all
j\in S
) the weak limits do not depend on the volatility term
b(\cdot)
. Consequently, the limit results in cases (B) and (C) are universal and remain unchanged regardless of choice of noise processes, provided the relevant integrability conditions (Assumptions
3
(c),
4
(c), or conditions (
5.19
), (
5.22
), with exponent
2
replaced by
\alpha^{*}
in all stable
\alpha^{*}
cases) hold. This phenomenon arising from the logarithmic transformation and associated scaling used in the divergent cases is quite surprising.

(ii)

The stable case of Corollary
5.2
(A) provides an explicit form of the limiting measure, clearly showing how its heavy tail structure arises from the stable distribution of noise process and the stochastic environments through the mixing measure. This explicit form can be used for parametric statistical inference to estimate
\alpha^{*}
when partial information about the latent dynamics is available.

6
An important Lemma

By notation
\mathcal{F}^{Y}_{\tau_{g^{j}_{t}}^{j}},
define the sigma field generated by the class of sets
\{A:A\cap\{g_{t}^{j}=n\}\in\mathcal{F}^{Y}_{\tau_{n}^{j}}\}.
Crucial elements of main proofs rely on the following lemma on asymptotic conditional independence of
t-\tau_{g_{t}^{j}}^{j}
and a
\Big\{\mathcal{F}^{Y}_{\tau_{g^{j}_{t}}^{j}}\Big\}_{t\geq 0}
measurable
\mathbb{R}^{d}
valued process
(H_{t}:t\geq 0)
conditioned on
\{Y_{t}=j\}.
We present Lemma
6.1
separately, It is useful in eliminating
Y_{t}=j
in the
t\to\infty
limit from the conditioning part of a probability, particularly when the main event is regarding some functional of
Y,
that exhibits a marginal weak limit with some properties (see (
6.1
)). By
{\mathbf{0}}_{d},I_{d}
we respectively denote a
d
-dimensional vector of zeros and a diagonal matrix of order
d.

Lemma 6.1
.

Fix
j\in S.
Suppose
(H_{t}:t\geq 0)
is a
\Big\{\mathcal{F}^{Y}_{\tau_{g^{j}_{t}}^{j}}\Big\}_{t\geq 0}
measurable
\mathbb{R}^{d}
valued process such that there exists a random variable
H_{\infty},
and an increasing deterministic function
\epsilon(\cdot):\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}\to\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
such that as
t\to\infty

\displaystyle H_{t}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{\to}}H_{\infty},\quad\frac{\epsilon(t)}{t}\to 0,\,\,\,\,\epsilon(t)\to\infty,\,\,\text{as}\,\,\,t\to\infty,\quad\text{and}\quad H_{t}-H_{t-\epsilon(t)}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle P}}{{\to}}{\mathbf{0}}_{d}

(6.1)

and
Y
is a semi-Markov process with Assumption
1
. Suppose further that
\big(L^{(1)}_{t},L^{(2)}_{t}\big)_{t\geq 0}
are arbitrary
d
-dimensional vector and matrix valued processes satisfying
\big(L^{(1)}_{t},L^{(2)}_{t}\big)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle P}}{{\to}}({\mathbf{0}}_{d},I_{d})
as
t\to\infty
. Then,
L^{(1)}_{t}+L^{(2)}_{t}H_{t}
and
(t-\tau_{g_{t}^{j}}^{j})
are asymptotically independent conditioned on
\{Y_{t}=j\};
i.e, for any
A\in\mathcal{B}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
such that
P[H_{\infty}\in\partial A]=0
,

\displaystyle\lim_{t\to\infty}P\Big[L^{(1)}_{t}+L^{(2)}_{t}H_{t}\in A,\,\,t-\tau_{g_{t}^{j}}^{j}>x\mid Y_{t}=j\Big]=P\Big[H_{\infty}\in A\Big]\,\,\,\frac{\sum_{k\in S}P_{jk}\int_{x}^{\infty}(1-F_{jk}(y))dy}{m_{j}},

for any
x\in\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}.

Remark 6.1
.

Both the process
(H_{t}:t\geq 0)
and the random variable
H_{\infty}
depend on
j\in S,
but for notational simplicity the subscript
j
is omitted.

Proof.

Since under necessary assumptions
\lim_{t\to\infty}P[Y_{t}=j]=\pi_{j}=\frac{\mu_{j}m_{j}}{\sum_{k\in S}\mu_{k}m_{k}},
the main assertion will follow if we prove that, for any
A\in\mathcal{B}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
with
P\big[H_{\infty}\in\partial A\big]=0
, as
t\to\infty,

\displaystyle\quad P\Big[H_{t}\in A,\,\,t-\tau_{g_{t}^{j}}^{j}>x,Y_{t}=j\Big]\to P\Big[H_{\infty}\in A\Big]\,\,\,\frac{\mu_{j}\sum_{k\in S}P_{jk}\int_{x}^{\infty}(1-F_{jk}(y))dy}{\sum_{k\in S}\mu_{k}m_{k}},

(6.2)

then, by Slutsky’s theorem, the assertion will hold as

\lim_{t\to\infty}P\Big[L_{t}^{(1)}+L_{t}^{(2)}H_{t}\in A,\,\,t-\tau_{g_{t}^{j}}^{j}>x,Y_{t}=j\Big]=\lim_{t\to\infty}P\Big[H_{t}\in A,\,\,t-\tau_{g_{t}^{j}}^{j}>x,Y_{t}=j\Big].

We prove (
6.2
) in following steps sequentially.

•

Step
1
:

\lim_{t\to\infty}P_{{}_{i}}\Big[t-\tau_{g_{t}^{j}}^{j}>x,Y_{t}=j\Big]=\frac{\mu_{j}\sum_{k\in S}P_{jk}\int_{x}^{\infty}(1-F_{jk}(y))dy}{\sum_{k\in S}\mu_{k}m_{k}}.

•

Step
2
:

\lim_{t\to\infty}P\Big[H_{t-\epsilon(t)}\in A\,\mid\,t-\tau_{g_{t}^{j}}^{j}>x,Y_{t}=j\Big]=P[H_{\infty}\in A].

•

Step
3
:

\lim_{t\to\infty}P\Big[H_{t}\in A\,\mid\,t-\tau_{g_{t}^{j}}^{j}>x,Y_{t}=j\Big]=\lim_{t\to\infty}P\Big[H_{t-\epsilon(t)}\in A\,\mid\,t-\tau_{g_{t}^{j}}^{j}>x,Y_{t}=j\Big]
for any
A
such that
P\big[H_{\infty}\in\partial A\big]=0.

Step 1
follows by similar arguments used in proving asymptotic time limit of forward residual time
\lim_{t\to\infty}P[\tau^{j}_{g_{t}^{j}+1}-t>x,Y_{t}=j]
at display (8.5) in
[
20
]
. For completeness we give a proof in the appendix as Lemma
12.1
.

Step 2:
Denote the event
\{Y_{t}=j,t-\tau^{j}_{g_{t}^{j}}>x\}
by
\widetilde{C}^{(j)}_{t,x}.
Observe that for any set
A\in\mathcal{B}(\mathbb{R}^{d}),

\displaystyle P\Big[H_{t-\epsilon(t)}\in A\,\mid\,\widetilde{C}^{(j)}_{t,x}\Big]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{j^{\prime}\in S}P\Big[H_{t-\epsilon(t)}\in A\,\mid\,Y_{t-\epsilon(t)}=j^{\prime},\widetilde{C}^{(j)}_{t,x}\Big]P\Big[Y_{t-\epsilon(t)}=j^{\prime}\mid\widetilde{C}^{(j)}_{t,x}\Big]

(6.3)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{j^{\prime}\in S}\frac{P\big[\widetilde{C}^{(j)}_{t,x}\mid Y_{t-\epsilon(t)}=j^{\prime},H_{t-\epsilon(t)}\in A\big]}{P\big[\widetilde{C}^{(j)}_{t,x}\big]}P\big[H_{t-\epsilon(t)}\in A,Y_{t-\epsilon(t)}=j^{\prime}\big]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle P[H_{t-\epsilon(t)}\in A]+\sum_{j^{\prime}\in S}P\Big[H_{t-\epsilon(t)}\in A,\,Y_{t-\epsilon(t)}=j^{\prime}\Big]

\displaystyle\quad\quad\times\Bigg(\frac{P[\widetilde{C}^{(j)}_{t,x}\mid H_{t-\epsilon(t)}\in A,Y_{t-\epsilon(t)}=j^{\prime}]}{P[\widetilde{C}^{(j)}_{t,x}]}-1\Bigg).

Denote the second term in RHS of (
6.3
) by
\mathcal{D}_{t}
and the quantity in first bracket by

M_{t}:=\frac{P[t-\tau_{g_{t}^{j}}^{j}>x,Y_{t}=j\mid H_{t-\epsilon(t)}\in A,Y_{t-\epsilon(t)}=j^{\prime}]}{P[t-\tau_{g_{t}^{j}}^{j}>x,Y_{t}=j]}.

In order to show
\mathcal{D}_{t}\to 0,
we first prove that
\lim_{t\to\infty}M_{t}\to 1.

Denote the set
\{\tau_{g_{t-\epsilon(t)}^{j}+2}^{j}\leq\tau_{g_{t}^{j}}^{j}\}
by
K_{t}.
Observe that

K_{t}=\Big\{B_{j}(t-\epsilon(t))+(\tau_{g^{j}_{t-\epsilon(t)}+2}^{j}-\tau_{g^{j}_{t-\epsilon(t)}+1}^{j})+A_{j}(t)\leq\epsilon(t)\Big\}.

Clearly
P[K_{t}]\to 1,
as
t\to\infty
(since all quantities
B_{j}(t-\epsilon(t)),\tau_{g^{j}_{t-\epsilon(t)}+2}^{j}-\tau_{g^{j}_{t-\epsilon(t)}+1}^{j}
and
A_{j}(t)
are
O_{{}_{P}}(1)
(using (
2.10
) and Proposition
2.2
(i)) and
t-\epsilon(t)\to\infty,\epsilon(t)\to\infty
as
t\to\infty
).

Define
\widetilde{a}_{t},\widetilde{b}_{t}
as

\displaystyle\widetilde{a}_{t}

\displaystyle:=

\displaystyle P\big[K_{t}\mid Y_{t-\epsilon(t)}=j^{\prime},H_{t-\epsilon(t)}\in A\big],

\displaystyle\widetilde{b}_{t}

\displaystyle:=

\displaystyle P\big[K_{t}^{c},(t-\tau_{g_{t}^{j}}^{j})>x,Y_{t}=j\mid H_{t-\epsilon(t)}\in A,Y_{t-\epsilon(t)}=j^{\prime}\big],

and it is easy to see that as
t\to\infty,

(\widetilde{a}_{t},\widetilde{b}_{t})\to(1,0).

Observe that the numerator of
M_{t}

\displaystyle P\big[t-\tau_{g_{t}^{j}}^{j}>x,Y_{t}=j\mid H_{t-\epsilon(t)}\in A,Y_{t-\epsilon(t)}=j^{\prime}\big]

\displaystyle\quad=\,\,\,\,\,\,\widetilde{a}_{t}\,\,P\big[t-\tau_{g_{t}^{j}}^{j}>x,Y_{t}=j\mid K_{t},H_{t-\epsilon(t)}\in A,Y_{t-\epsilon(t)}=j^{\prime}\big]+\widetilde{b}_{t}

\displaystyle\quad\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(a)}}{{=}}\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\widetilde{a}_{t}\,\,P\big[t-\tau_{g_{t}^{j}}^{j}>x,Y_{t}=j\mid K_{t}\big]+\widetilde{b}_{t}\,\,,

(6.4)

where equality in
(a)
follows by observing that conditioned on
K_{t},\{Y_{t-\epsilon(t)}=j^{\prime}\}
the event
\{t-\tau^{j}_{g_{t}^{j}}>x,Y_{t}=j\}
is
\sigma\{\mathcal{H}_{i}:i\geq g_{t-\epsilon(t)}^{j}+2\}
measurable where
H_{t-\epsilon(t)}
is
\sigma\{\mathcal{H}_{i}:i\leq g_{t-\epsilon(t)}^{j}\}
measurable so using Proposition
2.2
(iii) one can remove
\{H_{t-\epsilon(t)}\in A\}
from the conditioning event. Furthermore,
\{Y_{t-\epsilon(t)}=j^{\prime}\}
can be removed from the conditional part as well, since it is
\mathcal{F}^{Y}_{\tau_{g^{j}_{t-\epsilon(t)}+1}^{j}}
measurable which is independent of
\sigma\{\mathcal{H}_{i}:i\geq g_{t-\epsilon(t)}^{j}+2\}
under which
\{t-\tau^{j}_{g_{t}^{j}}>x,Y_{t}=j\}
is measurable conditioned on
K_{t}.

Since
(\widetilde{a}_{t},\widetilde{b}_{t})\to(1,0),
both the numerator and denominator of
M_{t}
will have identical time limit, it follows that
\lim_{t\to\infty}M_{t}\to 1.
Now we prove
D_{t}\to 0
as
t\to\infty.

Denote
\frac{\mu_{j}\sum_{k\in S}P_{jk}\int_{x}^{\infty}(1-F_{jk}(y))dy}{\sum_{k\in S}\mu_{k}m_{k}}
by
\pi_{j,x}.
For a fixed
j\in S,x\geq 0,
and any
\delta_{x}\in(0,\pi_{j,x})
there exists
t_{\delta_{x}}>0
such that

\displaystyle\Big|P\big[t-\tau_{g_{t}^{j}}^{j}>x,Y_{t}=j\big]-\pi_{j,x}\Big|\leq\delta_{x},

(6.5)

for
t\geq t_{\delta_{x}}
.

Observe that each summand in the second term of the RHS of (
6.3
) converges to
0
as
t\to\infty
. Also note that, for any
x>0,t\geq t_{\delta_{x}}
, the
j^{\prime}
-th term has the following upper bound

\displaystyle P\big[H_{t-\varepsilon(t)}\in A,Y_{t-\varepsilon(t)}=j^{\prime}\big]\big|M_{t}-1\big|

\displaystyle\quad\quad\leq P\big[Y_{t-\varepsilon(t)}=j^{\prime}\big]\frac{2}{P\big[t-\tau_{g_{t}^{j}}^{j}>x,Y_{t}=j\big]}

\displaystyle\quad\quad\leq P\big[Y_{t-\varepsilon(t)}=j^{\prime}\big]\frac{2}{\pi_{j,x}-\delta_{x}}

and the upper bound is summable over
j^{\prime}\in S
and has a limit as
t\to\infty
which is also summable over
j^{\prime}\in S
. Hence, by Pratt’s lemma (p. 101 in Schilling
[
54
]
), the sum in the RHS of (
6.3
) converges to
D_{t}\to 0
as
t\to\infty
.

From assumption (
\frac{\epsilon(t)}{t}\to 0
) the assertion
H_{t-\epsilon(t)}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{\to}}H_{\infty}
follows, showing the first term in (
6.3
) converging to
P[H_{\infty}\in A]
as asserted.

Step 3:
Take any
\delta>0
and define
A^{\delta}:=\{x:d(x,A)<\delta\},A^{-\delta}:=\{x\in A:d(x,A^{c})>\delta\}
and note that
A^{-\delta}\subseteq A\subseteq A^{\delta}
. Denoting
B_{t}:=H_{t}-H_{t-\epsilon(t)}
one has

\displaystyle P\big[H_{t}\in A\,\mid\,t-\tau_{g_{t}^{j}}^{j}>x,Y_{t}=j\big]=P\big[H_{t-\epsilon(t)}+B_{t}\in A,|B_{t}|\leq\delta\,\mid\,t-\tau_{g_{t}^{j}}^{j}>x,Y_{t}=j\big]+C_{t}

where

\limsup_{t\to\infty}C_{t}\leq\limsup_{t\to\infty}\frac{P[|B_{t}|>\delta]}{P\big[t-\tau_{g_{t}^{j}}^{j}>x,Y_{t}=j\big]}=0.

Observe that

\displaystyle P\big[H_{t-\epsilon(t)}\in A^{-\delta}\mid\,\,t-\tau_{g_{t}^{j}}^{j}>x,Y_{t}=j\big]

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle P\big[H_{t-\epsilon(t)}+B_{t}\in A,|B_{t}|\leq\delta\mid\,\,t-\tau_{g_{t}^{j}}^{j}>x,Y_{t}=j\big]

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle P\big[H_{t-\epsilon(t)}\in A^{\delta}\mid\,\,t-\tau_{g_{t}^{j}}^{j}>x,Y_{t}=j\big].

Step
3
follows by letting
\delta\to 0
after taking
t\to\infty
to both sides of aforementioned step and applying result obtained from Step 2 (using continuity set assumption of
A
i.e

P[H_{\infty}\in\partial A]=0
).
∎

7
Conclusion and future directions

(i)

The regime-switching framework of CTMC in
[
46
]
is extended to a semi-Markovian regime switching while preserving the renewal regenerative structure. This extension is crucial for the modeling point of view as well as for making long-term prediction of observed stochastic processes via finding the scaled limits. Exact long-time behaviors of several classes of stochastic models, admitting a function of a specific integral
\int_{0}^{t}b(Y_{s})e^{-\int_{s}^{t}a(Y_{r})dr}ds,
become explicit in terms of the parameters that determine the dynamics of the underlying regime process. We applied it further in two examples (Pitchfork bifurcation and regime switching diffusions) and got explicit long-time behaviors in different cases with several interesting observations (Remark
5.1
and Remark
5.3
). We are curious whether, for regime-switching diffusions that are more general than (
5.9
) and (
5.16
), phenomena similar to those in Remark
5.3
still occur or not. Specifically, we wonder if the universal weak limit results emerge in the divergent cases, that do not depend on the noise process (as in cases (B) and (C)) in the right hand side weak limit, while strong dependence on the noise process is observed in the stable case (A) and the special divergent case (D) in both Theorem
6
and Corollary
5.2
.

Can this framework be extended to more general regime processes while maintaining the renewal regenerative structure? To address this partially, several works on long-memory (see
[
11
]
) and infinite-memory processes (see
[
33
]
) suggest that under certain restrictive conditions, the regenerative renewal property may still hold. However, integrating such formulation with our technique requires the distributional estimate of the asymptotic residual time

\mathcal{L}\Big(t-\tau^{j}_{g_{t}^{j}}\mid Y_{t}=j\Big)

as well as developing an analogous version of Lemma
6.1
. Such derivations may not be as explicit as those for the semi-Markov regime process that we have here, which will be addressed later.

As mentioned in Remark
2.1
the renewal regenerative structure of
Y
will be absent if Assumption
1
(c) does not hold. Can we have any alternative methodology to circumvent this or at least have some closed-form limit results in such non-regular cases? We conjecture that in the divergent cases, one may still get similar limit theorems for
\frac{\log|I_{t}^{(a,b)}|}{a^{\prime}(t)}-b^{\prime}(t)
for suitable choices of
a^{\prime}(\cdot),b^{\prime}(\cdot)
depending on different cases. Such results for non-regenerative
Y,
will be explored further in future.

(ii)

In contrast to
[
46
]
, the varying tail structures of sojourn time distributions play a crucial role in shaping the different behaviors of the scaling limits. In the stable region that change is not abrupt as it only affects the common law of
\{(L_{i}^{j},Q_{i}^{j}):i\geq 1\}
as well as the law of the residual time
(t-\tau^{j}_{g_{t}^{j}}),
but in the transient region it changes the scaling factor of
\log|I_{t}|
as it controls the rate on how it will diverge in a long time. In a way, this work illustrates universal results on long-time behaviour of some semi-markovian regime-switching stochastic processes with different tail structures of the sojourn times.

As hinted in Remark
4.3
, tails of
|\widetilde{A}_{1}^{j}\cap\mathbb{S}^{c}_{\alpha}|
and
|\widetilde{A}_{1}^{j}\cap\mathbb{S}_{\alpha}|
are governed by the underlying chain
J,
and the assumptions in (
4.7
) and
E|\widetilde{A}_{1}^{j}\cap\mathbb{S}^{c}_{\alpha}|^{1+\alpha}<\infty
are quite restrictive in a sense that they force
|\widetilde{A}_{1}^{j}\cap\mathbb{S}^{c}_{\alpha}|,|\widetilde{A}_{1}^{j}\cap\mathbb{S}_{\alpha}|
to have tails lighter than
\alpha,
which is the heaviest tails of the sojourn times. In pathological cases when the tail index of
|\widetilde{A}_{1}^{j}\cap\mathbb{S}_{\alpha}|
is equal to
\alpha
or less than that, it will drive the aggregate tail index of
\int_{\tau_{0}^{j}}^{\tau_{1}^{j}}\big(a(Y_{s})-E_{\pi}a(\cdot)\big)ds
in line of Lemma 3.7 (part (2) and other parts) of
[
41
]
and that will change the statement of Theorem
3
as well (which will be explored with details in future).

(iii)

As a standing condition Assumption
1
(b) ensures that all elements of
\mathcal{G}
should have finite first moment. If that is violated, several problems will arise such as
\int_{\tau_{0}^{j}}^{\tau_{1}^{j}}(a(Y_{s})-E_{\pi}a(\cdot))ds
can be regularly varying with some
\alpha\in(0,1),
and as a consequence almost surely/ in probability convergence of
\frac{g_{t}^{j}}{t}
will not hold anymore, instead for an increasing function
\alpha(\cdot)
one has
\frac{g_{t}^{j}}{\alpha(t)}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{\to}}X
for some strictly positive random variable
X.
As a consequence, Anscome’s law via verifying the contiguity condition (
2.11
) will not be useful as it requires in probability convergence of the stopped random time to a constant, such as
\frac{g_{t}^{j}}{\alpha(t)}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle p}}{{\to}}a
for some
a>0
. There should be a way to circumvent this issue, and it will be addressed in the future.

(iv)

Lemma
6.1
can be seen as a conditional version of the Anscombe’s law when the stopped random time (here the renewal time
g_{t}^{j}
) appears from the semi-Markovian contexts and it can be used in more general directions such as in finding the weak limit of
H_{t}:=\sup_{t\geq 0}I_{t}
. It would be interesting to use similar methods in the context of extremal processes under general regime-switching structures as well.

8
Set-up of proof-ideas & notations

We proceed with the convention
\sum_{i=j}^{k}a_{i}=0
and
\prod_{i=j}^{k}a_{i}=1
if
j>k
for any
a_{i}
.
For any functions
c,d:S\to\mathbb{R}
and
j\in S
, define

\displaystyle\,\,\,\,\,G^{c,d}_{j}(x):=\int_{0}^{x}d(j)e^{-c(j)(x-s)}ds=xd(j)1_{\{c(j)=0\}}+\frac{d(j)}{c(j)}\Big(1-e^{-xc(j)}\Big)1_{\{c(j)\neq 0\}}.

(8.1)

Let
e^{-\int_{s}^{t}a(Y_{r})dr}
be denoted by
\Phi(s,t)
and by this notation
\Phi_{t}=\Phi(0,t).
Given two functions
a(\cdot),b(\cdot):S\to\mathbb{R}
, we define some random variables
\big\{\big(L_{i}^{j},Q_{i}^{j}\big):i\geq 1\big\},\big(L_{0}^{j},Q_{0}^{j}\big)
as

\displaystyle(L_{i}^{j},Q_{i}^{j})

\displaystyle:=

\displaystyle\bigg(e^{-\int_{\tau_{i-1}^{j}}^{\tau_{i}^{j}}a(Y_{s})ds},\int_{\tau_{i-1}^{j}}^{\tau_{i}^{j}}b(Y_{s})e^{-\int_{s}^{\tau_{i}^{j}}a(Y_{r})dr}ds\bigg)\,\,\,\,\,\forall i\geq 1,\quad\text{and}

(8.2)

\displaystyle(L_{0}^{j},Q_{0}^{j})

\displaystyle:=

\displaystyle\bigg(e^{-\int_{0}^{\tau_{0}^{j}}a(Y_{s})ds},\int_{0}^{\tau_{0}^{j}}b(Y_{s})e^{-\int_{s}^{\tau_{0}^{j}}a(Y_{r})dr}ds\bigg).

(8.3)

Observe that if
Y_{0}=j
, then
\tau_{0}^{j}=0
and
(L^{j}_{0},Q^{j}_{0})=(1,0)
. The sequence of intervals
(\mathfrak{I}^{j}_{k})_{k\geq 1}
of renewal cycles (recall
\mathfrak{I}^{j}_{k}:=[\tau_{k-1}^{j},\tau_{k}^{j})
) is an i.i.d. sequence and therefore also
(L^{j}_{i},Q^{j}_{i})_{i=1}^{\infty}
is an i.i.d. sequence under Assumption
1
. However, for fixed
t>0,

(L^{j}_{i},Q^{j}_{i})_{i=1}^{g^{j}_{t}}
is not an i.i.d. sequence since
g^{j}_{t}
, defined in (
2.7
), is a renewal time which depends on the sum of all renewal cycle lengths before time
t
.

Given an arbitrary sequence of iid random variables
\{(A_{i},B_{i}):i\geq 1\}
having common law
\mathcal{L}(A,B),
define the random variables
\big\{\big(P_{n}(A,B),\widetilde{P}_{n}(A,B)\big):n\geq 1\big\}
as following

\displaystyle P_{n}(A,B):=\sum_{k=1}^{n}\bigg(\prod_{i=1}^{k-1}A_{i}\bigg)B_{k},\quad\quad\widetilde{P}_{n}(A,B):=\sum_{k=1}^{n}\bigg(\prod_{i=k+1}^{n}A_{i}\bigg)B_{k}.

(8.4)

Further note that in general
\{\widetilde{P}_{n}(A,B):n\geq 1\}
is Markovian and have a random coefficient autoregressive process type evolution

\widetilde{P}_{n+1}(A,B)=A_{n+1}\widetilde{P}_{n}(A,B)+B_{n+1},\quad\text{ with }\widetilde{P}_{n}(A,B)\perp\!\!\!\!\perp\big(A_{n+1},B_{n+1}\big),

while the sequence
\{P_{n}(A,B):n\geq 1\}
is not Markovian but admits the following representation

P_{n+1}(A,B)=P_{n}(A,B)+\Big(\prod_{i=1}^{n}A_{i}\Big)B_{n+1}.

Observe that
\{(L_{i}^{j},Q_{i}^{j}):i\geq 1\}
are iid under Assumption
1
. For notational simplicity, by
P_{g_{t}^{j}}^{j},\widetilde{P}_{g_{t}^{j}}^{j}
we denote
P_{g_{t}^{j}}(L_{i}^{j},Q_{i}^{j}),\widetilde{P}_{g_{t}^{j}}(L_{i}^{j},Q_{i}^{j})
respectively. Define

\displaystyle R_{t}:=\big(R_{t}^{(1)},R_{t}^{(2)}\big):=\bigg(\bigg(\prod_{k=1}^{g_{t}^{j}}L^{j}_{k}\bigg)L^{j}_{0},\quad\bigg(\prod_{k=1}^{g_{t}^{j}}L^{j}_{k}\bigg)Q^{j}_{0}+P^{j}_{g_{t}^{j}}\bigg).

(8.5)

From the aforementioned definitions,
R_{t}
depends on
j\in S
but for notational simplicity
j
is omitted.

9
Proof of Theorem
1

We use the following two lemmas to prove the theorem.

Lemma 9.1
.

Under Assumption
1
, for any
t>0
and
A=(A_{1},A_{2})\in\mathcal{B}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
,

\displaystyle P\big[(\Phi^{(a)}_{t},I^{(a,b)}_{t})\in A\mid Y_{0}=i,Y_{t}=j\big]=P\big[\big(Q^{(1)}_{t},Q^{(2)}_{t}\big)\in(A_{1},A_{2})\mid Y_{0}=i,Y_{t}=j\big],

where
(Q^{(1)}_{t},Q^{(2)}_{t})_{t\geq 0}
is an
(\mathcal{F}_{t}^{Y})_{t\geq 0}
-adapted process defined as

\displaystyle(Q^{(1)}_{t},Q_{t}^{(2)})

\displaystyle:=

\displaystyle\bigg(e^{-a(j)(t-\tau_{g_{t}^{j}}^{j})}\bigg(\prod_{k=1}^{g^{j}_{t}}L^{j}_{k}\bigg)L^{j}_{0}\quad,\quad

(9.1)

\displaystyle\quad\quad G_{j}^{a,b}\big(t-\tau_{g_{t}^{j}}^{j}\big)+e^{-a(j)(t-\tau_{g_{t}^{j}}^{j})}\bigg[\bigg(\prod_{k=1}^{g_{t}^{j}}L^{j}_{k}\bigg)Q^{j}_{0}+P^{j}_{g_{t}^{j}}\bigg]\bigg).

Lemma 9.2
.

Suppose Assumptions
1
and
2
hold.

(a)

For each
j\in S,
as
t\to\infty,
the process
R:=(R_{t}:t\geq 0)
defined in (
8.5
) marginally satisfies

R_{t}=\big(R_{t}^{(1)},R_{t}^{(2)}\big)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{\to}}(0,V^{*}_{j})

where the random variable
V^{*}_{j}
is distributed identically as in (
3.3
).

(b)

The process
\big(R_{t}^{(2)}:t\geq 0\big)
satisfies all properties of
(H_{t})_{t\geq 0}
in (
6.1
) of Lemma
6.1
.

We sketch how aforementioned lemmas will help us proving the assertion in (
3.2
). For any
A_{3}\in\mathcal{B}(\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}\times\mathbb{R}\times S)
taking
t\to\infty
limit on both sides of

P_{i}[(\Phi^{(a)}_{t},I^{(a,b)}_{t},Y_{t})\in A_{3}]=\sum_{j\in S}P[Y_{t}=j\mid Y_{0}=i]P\big[(\Phi^{(a)}_{t},I^{(a,b)}_{t},j)\in A_{3}\mid Y_{0}=i,Y_{t}=j\big],

and using
P[Y_{t}=j\mid Y_{0}=i]\to\pi_{j}
(which holds under Assumption
1
), main assertion will be a consequence of Dominated Convergence Theorem if we show that as
t\to\infty,

P\big[(\Phi^{(a)}_{t},I^{(a,b)}_{t},Y_{t})\in A_{3}\mid Y_{0}=i,Y_{t}=j\big]\to P[(0,Z_{j},j)\in A_{3}]\quad

where for each
j\in S,
the random variable
Z_{j}
is defined in (
3.2
). Since on conditioning with respect to
\{Y_{t}=j\}
last co-ordinate of
(\Phi^{(a)}_{t},I^{(a,b)}_{t},Y_{t})
becomes deterministic, we proceed with
P\big[(\Phi^{(a)}_{t},I^{(a,b)}_{t})\in A\mid Y_{0}=i,Y_{t}=j\big]
which has a distributional characterization from Lemma
9.1
through
(Q^{(1)}_{t},Q_{t}^{(2)})
. The assertion holds if we show that as
t\to\infty,

\displaystyle P\Big[(Q^{(1)}_{t},Q^{(2)}_{t})\in A\mid Y_{0}=i,Y_{t}=j\Big]\to P\Big[(0,Z_{j})\in A\Big]\quad A\in\mathcal{B}(\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}\times\mathbb{R}).

(9.2)

Observe that

\displaystyle(Q^{(1)}_{t},Q_{t}^{(2)})=\Big(e^{-a(j)(t-\tau_{g_{t}^{j}}^{j})}R_{t}^{(1)},G_{j}^{a,b}\big(t-\tau_{g_{t}^{j}}^{j}\big)+e^{-a(j)(t-\tau_{g_{t}^{j}}^{j})}R_{t}^{(2)}\Big).

(9.3)

The limit in (
9.2
) holds if one can find
t\to\infty
limit of
\mathcal{L}\Big((t-\tau_{g_{t}^{j}}^{j}),R_{t}\mid Y_{0}=i,Y_{t}=j\Big).
From Lemma
9.2
(a) one has the unconditional
t\to\infty
weak limit for
R_{t}.
As
R_{t}
is a
\mathcal{F}^{Y}_{\tau_{g^{j}_{t}}^{j}}
measurable process with
R_{t}^{(1)}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{\to}}0
established in Lemma
9.2
(a), It suffices to find the limit of
\mathcal{L}\Big((t-\tau_{g_{t}^{j}}^{j}),R^{(2)}_{t}\mid Y_{0}=i,Y_{t}=j\Big).

With the help of Lemma
9.2
(b), Lemma
6.1
is applied by setting
H_{t}:=R_{t}^{(2)},(L_{t}^{(1)},L_{t}^{(2)})=(0,1),
and we yield for any
A_{1}\in\mathcal{B}(\mathbb{R}),A_{2}\in\mathcal{B}(\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0})
as
t\to\infty

P_{i}\big[\big(R_{t}^{(2)},(t-\tau_{g_{t}^{j}}^{j})\big)\in A_{1}\times A_{2}\mid Y_{t}=j\big]\to P[V^{*}_{j}\in A_{1}]\pi_{j}^{*}(A_{2})

where
V^{*}_{j}
is defined as the unconditional limit of
R^{(2)}_{t}
in Lemma
9.2
(a) and the measure
\pi_{j}^{*}(\cdot)
is defined in (
3.1
). This way (
9.2
) is established by setting the random variable
T^{j}
(that is independent of rest of the random variables) such that
\mathcal{L}(T^{j})=\pi^{*}_{j},
and observing

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}\Big(e^{-a(j)(t-\tau_{g_{t}^{j}}^{j})}R_{t}^{(1)},G_{j}^{a,b}\big(t-\tau_{g_{t}^{j}}^{j}\big)+e^{-a(j)(t-\tau_{g_{t}^{j}}^{j})}R_{t}^{(2)}\mid Y_{0}=i,Y_{t}=j\Big)

\displaystyle\quad\quad\quad\quad\stackrel{{\scriptstyle w}}{{\to}}\mathcal{L}\big(0,G_{j}^{a,b}(T^{j})+e^{-a(j)T^{j}}V^{*}_{j}\big)

\displaystyle\quad\quad\quad\quad=\mathcal{L}(0,Z_{j})

as
t\to\infty
(where
Z_{j}
is defined in (
3.3
)) proving the assertion in (
9.2
).

\square

9.1
Proof of Lemma
9.1

Proof.

Define
\Phi(s,t)
as
e^{-\int_{s}^{t}a(Y_{r})dr}
for any
0<s<t.
On set
\{Y_{0}=i,Y_{t}=j\}=\{\omega\in\Omega:Y_{0}(\omega)=i,Y_{t}(\omega)=j\}
we decompose the integral type expressions of
(\Phi^{(a)}_{t},I^{(a,b)}_{t})
through partitioning the interval
[0,t)
into disjoint intervals as
[0,t)=[0,\tau_{0}^{j})\cup_{k=1}^{g_{t}^{j}}[\tau_{k-1}^{j},\tau_{k}^{j})\cup[\tau^{j}_{g_{t}^{j}},t).
One has the following representation using the notations in (
8.2
),(
8.3
) and (
8.5
)

\displaystyle\begin{pmatrix}\Phi^{(a)}_{t}\\
I^{(a,b)}_{t}\end{pmatrix}=\begin{pmatrix}\Phi(0,\tau_{0}^{j})\Big(\prod_{i=1}^{g_{t}^{j}}\Phi\big(\tau_{i-1}^{j},\tau_{i}^{j}\big)\Big)\Phi(\tau_{g_{t}^{j}}^{j},t)\\
\int_{0}^{\tau_{0}^{j}}b(Y_{s})\Phi(s,t)ds+\sum_{k=1}^{g_{t}^{j}}\int_{\tau_{k-1}^{j}}^{\tau_{k}^{j}}b(Y_{s})\Phi(s,t)ds\quad+\int_{\tau_{g_{t}^{j}}^{j}}^{t}b(Y_{s})\Phi(s,t)ds.\end{pmatrix}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{=}}\begin{pmatrix}L^{j}_{0}\bigg(\prod_{k=1}^{g^{j}_{t}}L^{j}_{k}\bigg)\Phi(\tau_{g_{t}^{j}}^{j},t)\\
\Phi(\tau_{g_{t}^{j}}^{j},t)\Big[Q_{0}^{j}\prod_{k=1}^{g_{t}^{j}}L_{k}^{j}+\sum_{k=1}^{g_{t}^{j}}\Big(\prod_{i=k}^{g_{t}^{j}-1}L_{i+1}^{j}\Big)Q_{k}^{j}\Big]+\int_{\tau_{g_{t}^{j}}^{j}}^{t}b(Y_{s})\Phi(s,t)ds\end{pmatrix}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\{Y_{0}=i,Y_{t}=j\}}}{{=}}\begin{pmatrix}L^{j}_{0}\bigg(\prod_{k=1}^{g^{j}_{t}}L^{j}_{k}\bigg)e^{-a(j)(t-\tau_{g_{t}^{j}}^{j})}\\
e^{-a(j)(t-\tau_{g_{t}^{j}}^{j})}\bigg[\bigg(\prod_{k=1}^{g_{t}^{j}}L^{j}_{k}\bigg)Q^{j}_{0}+\widetilde{P}^{j}_{g_{t}^{j}}\bigg]+G_{j}^{a,b}\big(t-\tau_{g_{t}^{j}}^{j}\big)\end{pmatrix}

(9.4)

where the last equality is a consequence of the fact that on
\{Y_{0}=i,Y_{t}=j\}
the latent process
Y
will always be at state
j
in interval
[\tau_{g_{t}^{j}}^{j},t)
and that implies

\int_{\tau_{g_{t}^{j}}^{j}}^{t}b(Y_{s})\Phi(s,t)ds=\int_{\tau_{g_{t}^{j}}^{j}}^{t}b(j)e^{-a(j)(t-s)}ds=\int_{0}^{t-\tau_{g_{t}^{j}}^{j}}b(j)e^{-a(j)(t-r)}dr=G_{j}^{a,b}\big(t-\tau_{g_{t}^{j}}^{j}\big).

Note that in (
9.1
) we have
P_{g_{t}^{j}}^{j}
which is not same as
\widetilde{P}_{g_{t}^{j}}^{j}
in (
9.4
). Observe that

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}\bigg((L_{0}^{j},Q_{0}^{j}),(L_{1}^{j},Q_{1}^{j}),(L_{2}^{j},Q_{2}^{j}),\ldots,(L_{g_{t}^{j}}^{j},Q_{g_{t}^{j}}^{j}),(t-\tau_{g_{t}^{j}}^{j})\mid Y_{0}=i,Y_{t}=j\bigg)

\displaystyle\,\,\,\quad\quad\quad\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{=}}\mathcal{L}\bigg((L_{0}^{j},Q_{0}^{j}),(L_{g_{t}^{j}}^{j},Q_{g_{t}^{j}}^{j}),(L_{g_{t}^{j}-1}^{j},Q_{g_{t}^{j}-1}^{j}),\ldots,(L_{1}^{j},Q_{1}^{j}),(t-\tau_{g_{t}^{j}}^{j})\mid Y_{0}=i,Y_{t}=j\bigg).

(9.5)

This follows from the fact that
g_{t}^{j}
represents the renewal time, which depends solely on the total sum of the interval lengths
[0,\tau_{0}^{j})\cup_{k=1}^{g_{t}^{j}}[\tau_{k-1}^{j},\tau_{k}^{j})
. This sum remains unchanged if we reverse the order of the regenerating renewal intervals in
\cup_{k=1}^{g_{t}^{j}}[\tau_{k-1}^{j},\tau_{k}^{j})
by replacing each
k
with
g_{t}^{j}-k+1
for
k=1,\ldots,g_{t}^{j}
. As a result, the equality in distribution holds for both sides of (
9.5
).

Since
P_{g_{t}^{j}}^{j}
and
\widetilde{P}_{g_{t}^{j}}^{j}
are outputs of identical function of the random variables in LHS and RHS of (
9.5
). This implies that

\displaystyle\Big((L_{0}^{j},Q_{0}^{j}),P_{g_{t}^{j}}^{j},(t-\tau_{g_{t}^{j}}^{j})\Big)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathcal{L}(\cdot\mid Y_{0}=i,Y_{t}=j)}}{{=}}\Big((L_{0}^{j},Q_{0}^{j}),\widetilde{P}_{g_{t}^{j}}^{j},(t-\tau_{g_{t}^{j}}^{j})\Big)

(9.6)

are weakly identical. Hence,

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}\bigg(L^{j}_{0}\bigg(\prod_{k=1}^{g^{j}_{t}}L^{j}_{k}\bigg)e^{-a(j)(t-\tau_{g_{t}^{j}}^{j})}\quad,\quad e^{-a(j)(t-\tau_{g_{t}^{j}}^{j})}\bigg[\bigg(\prod_{k=1}^{g_{t}^{j}}L^{j}_{k}\bigg)Q^{j}_{0}+\widetilde{P}^{j}_{g_{t}^{j}}\bigg]

\displaystyle\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad+\,\,G_{j}^{a,b}\big(t-\tau_{g_{t}^{j}}^{j}\big)\mid Y_{0}=i,Y_{t}=j\bigg)

\displaystyle\quad=\mathcal{L}\Big(Q_{t}^{(1)},Q_{t}^{(2)}\mid Y_{0}=i,Y_{t}=j\Big),

holds, proving the assertion of Lemma
9.1
.
∎

Proof of Lemma
9.2
is given in the Supplementary Material. Since the proof of Theorem
2
closely follows the approach of Theorem
3
(and Theorem 2 of
[
46
]
), it is added in the Supplementary material.

10
Proof of Theorem
3

Denote
t^{1/\alpha}L_{0}(t),n^{1/\alpha}L_{0}(n)
by
c_{t},c_{n}
respectively. We begin by stating the following Lemma and prove the theorem in the following subsections.

Lemma 10.1
.

Suppose Assumptions
1
and
4
(a,b) hold. Then for each
j\in S,
there exist
\alpha\in(1,2)
and a slowly varying function
L(\cdot)
(specified in Assumption
4
(b)) such that

\displaystyle\lim_{x\to\infty}\frac{P\Big[\int_{\tau_{0}^{j}}^{\tau_{1}^{j}}\big(a(Y_{s})-E_{\pi}a(\cdot)\big)ds>x\Big]}{x^{-\alpha}L(x)}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\widetilde{\alpha}_{j}^{(+)}=\frac{1-\beta_{j}}{2}\sigma^{\alpha}_{(j,\alpha)},

(10.1)

\displaystyle\lim_{x\to\infty}\frac{P\Big[\int_{\tau_{0}^{j}}^{\tau_{1}^{j}}\big(a(Y_{s})-E_{\pi}a(\cdot)\big)ds<-x\Big]}{x^{-\alpha}L(x)}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\widetilde{\alpha}_{j}^{(-)}=\frac{1+\beta_{j}}{2}\sigma^{\alpha}_{(j,\alpha)}.

(10.2)

10.1
Proof of Theorem
3
(a)

Proof.

We begin with
I_{t}^{(a,b)}=\Phi_{t}^{(a)}\widetilde{I}_{t}^{(a,b)},
where
\widetilde{I}^{(a,b)}_{t}=\int_{0}^{t}b(Y_{s})e^{\int_{0}^{s}a(Y_{r})dr}ds.
Fix arbitrary
i,j\in S
. Observe that on
\{Y_{0}=i,Y_{t}=j\}
using renewal regenerative structure of
Y
(as a consequence of Assumption 1)

\displaystyle\Phi_{t}^{(a)}=L_{0}^{j}\Big(\prod_{i=1}^{g_{t}^{j}}L_{i}^{j}\Big)e^{-a(j)(t-\tau_{g_{t}^{j}}^{j})},\,\,\,\widetilde{I}^{(a,b)}_{t}=\bigg[\frac{Q_{0}^{j}+S^{**}_{g_{t}^{j}}}{L_{0}^{j}}+\frac{G_{j}^{(a,b)}(t-\tau_{g_{t}^{j}})}{\Phi_{t}^{(a)}}\bigg]

where for any integer
n\geq 1,\quad

S^{**}_{n}:=P_{n}\big(\frac{1}{L_{1}^{j}},\frac{Q_{1}^{j}}{L_{1}^{j}}\big)=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\frac{Q_{i}^{j}}{L_{i}^{j}}\prod_{k=1}^{i-1}\Big(\frac{1}{L_{k}^{j}}\Big).

It follows that

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}\bigg[\bigg(\frac{\log\Phi_{t}^{(a)}+tE_{\pi}a(\cdot)}{t^{\frac{1}{\alpha}}L_{0}(t)},\frac{\log|I_{t}^{(a,b)}|+tE_{\pi}a(\cdot)}{t^{\frac{1}{\alpha}}L_{0}(t)}\bigg)|Y_{0}=i,Y_{t}=j\bigg]

\displaystyle\quad=\mathcal{L}\bigg[\Big(\widetilde{G}^{*(1)}_{t}+\widetilde{G}^{(2)}_{t},\widetilde{G}^{*(1)}_{t}+\widetilde{G}^{(2)}_{t}+\widetilde{G}^{(3)}_{t}\Big)|Y_{0}=i,Y_{t}=j\bigg],

(10.3)

where

\displaystyle\widetilde{G}^{*(1)}_{t}=\frac{\sum_{i=1}^{g_{t}^{j}}\log L_{i}^{j}+tE_{\pi}a(\cdot)}{t^{\frac{1}{\alpha}}L_{0}(t)},\,\,\quad\widetilde{G}^{(2)}_{t}=\frac{\int_{0}^{\tau_{0}^{j}}a(Y_{s})ds+a(j)(t-\tau_{g_{t}^{j}}^{j})}{t^{\frac{1}{\alpha}}L_{0}(t)},

\displaystyle\widetilde{G}^{(3)}_{t}=\frac{\log\Big|\frac{Q_{0}^{j}+S^{**}_{g_{t}^{j}}}{L_{0}^{j}}+\frac{G_{j}^{(a,b)}(t-\tau_{g_{t}^{j}})}{\Phi_{t}^{(a)}}\Big|}{t^{\frac{1}{\alpha}}L_{0}(t)}.

Hence for any
A_{1},A_{2}\in\mathcal{B}(\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}),

\displaystyle P\bigg[\bigg(\frac{\log\Phi_{t}^{(a)}+tE_{\pi}a(\cdot)}{t^{\frac{1}{\alpha}}L_{0}(t)},\frac{\log|I_{t}^{(a,b)}|+tE_{\pi}a(\cdot)}{t^{\frac{1}{\alpha}}L_{0}(t)}\bigg)\in\cdot|Y_{0}=i\bigg]

(10.4)

\displaystyle\quad=\sum_{j\in S}P[Y_{t}=j|Y_{0}=i]P\bigg[\big(\widetilde{G}^{*(1)}_{t}+\widetilde{G}^{(2)}_{t},\widetilde{G}^{*(1)}_{t}+\widetilde{G}^{(2)}_{t}+\widetilde{G}^{(3)}_{t}\big)\in\cdot|Y_{t}=j,Y_{0}=i\bigg].

Observe that

\displaystyle\widetilde{G}^{*(1)}_{t}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{\sum_{i=1}^{g_{t}^{j}}(\log L_{i}^{j}+E_{\pi}a(\cdot)|\mathfrak{I}_{i}^{j}|)}{t^{\frac{1}{\alpha}}L_{0}(t)}+\frac{\big[\tau_{0}^{j}+(t-\tau_{g_{t}^{j}}^{j})\big]E_{\pi}a(\cdot)}{t^{\frac{1}{\alpha}}L_{0}(t)}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\frac{c_{g_{t}^{j}}}{c_{t}}\frac{\sum_{i=1}^{g_{t}^{j}}\int_{\tau_{i-1}^{j}}^{\tau_{i}^{j}}\big(a(Y_{s})-E_{\pi}a(\cdot)\big)ds}{(g_{t}^{j})^{\frac{1}{\alpha}}L_{0}(g_{t}^{j})}+\frac{\big[\tau_{0}^{j}+(t-\tau_{g_{t}^{j}}^{j})\big]E_{\pi}a(\cdot)}{t^{\frac{1}{\alpha}}L_{0}(t)}.

Now clearly
\Big\{-\int_{\tau_{i-1}^{j}}^{\tau_{i}^{j}}(a(Y_{s})-E_{\pi}a(\cdot))ds:i\geq 1\Big\}
are iid regularly varying with
1<\alpha<2
, mean
=0,
with right asymptotic tail-index
\widetilde{\alpha}_{j}^{(-)}=\frac{1+\beta_{j}}{2}\sigma^{\alpha}_{(j,\alpha)}
, left asymptotic tail-index
\widetilde{\alpha}_{j}^{(+)}=\frac{1-\beta_{j}}{2}\sigma^{\alpha}_{(j,\alpha)}
, after scaling by
x^{-\alpha}L(x)
(from Lemma
10.1
). Furthermore,
\lim_{t\to\infty}\frac{c_{g_{t}^{j}}}{c_{t}}=\Big(\frac{1}{E|\mathfrak{I}^{j}_{1}|}\Big)^{\frac{1}{\alpha}}
(see Lemma
12.3
(a)).

Using for each
j\in S
the quantity
\beta_{j}
in (
4.12
), applying the stable central limit Theorem 4.5.1 of
[
62
]
, as sample size
n\to\infty

\displaystyle-c^{-1}_{n}\Big(\sum_{i=1}^{n}\int_{\tau_{i-1}^{j}}^{\tau_{i}^{j}}\big(a(Y_{s})-E_{\pi}a(\cdot)\big)ds\Big)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{\to}}\sigma_{(j,\alpha)}\mathcal{S}_{\alpha}(1,\beta_{j},0).

(10.5)

Using (
10.5
) and Theorem 3.2 of
[
34
]
for the stopped random time
g_{t}^{j}
as
t\to\infty,

-\frac{\sum_{i=1}^{g_{t}^{j}}\int_{\tau_{i-1}^{j}}^{\tau_{i}^{j}}\big(a(Y_{s})-E_{\pi}a(\cdot)\big)ds}{(g_{t}^{j})^{\frac{1}{\alpha}}L_{0}(g_{t}^{j})}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{\to}}\sigma_{(j,\alpha)}\mathcal{S}_{\alpha}(1,\beta_{j},0).

Furthermore since
\tau_{0}^{j}+(t-\tau_{g_{t}^{j}}^{j})
is
O_{p}(1)
and hence

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}\Big(\widetilde{G}_{t}^{*(1)}|Y_{0}=i\Big)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle w}}{{\to}}\mathcal{L}\bigg(\big(E|\mathfrak{I}^{j}_{1}|\big)^{-\frac{1}{\alpha}}\sigma_{(j,\alpha)}\mathcal{S}_{\alpha}(1,\beta_{j},0)\bigg).

(10.6)

Since both
\log L_{0}^{j},(t-\tau_{g_{t}^{j}}^{j})
are
O_{P}(1)
, hence

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}(\widetilde{G}_{t}^{(2)}|Y_{0}=i)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle w}}{{\to}}\delta_{\{0\}}

(10.7)

Lastly using similar arguments used in proof of Theorem
2
(a), as
t\to\infty,
one has
S^{**}_{g_{t}^{j}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{\to}}S^{**}
where
S^{**}
satisfies the stochastic recurrence equation

\displaystyle S^{**}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{=}}\frac{1}{L_{i}^{j}}S^{**}+\frac{Q_{i}^{j}}{L_{i}^{j}},\quad S^{**}\perp\Big(\frac{1}{L_{i}^{j}},\frac{Q_{i}^{j}}{L_{i}^{j}}\Big).

(10.8)

Also
\Phi_{t}^{(a)}\to\infty
as
t\to\infty,
we have
\log\big|\frac{Q_{0}^{j}+S^{**}_{g_{t}^{j}}}{L_{0}^{j}}+\frac{G_{j}^{(a,b)}(t-\tau_{g_{t}^{j}})}{\Phi_{t}^{(a)}}\big|
is
O_{P}(1).

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}(\widetilde{G}_{t}^{(3)}|Y_{0}=i)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle w}}{{\to}}\delta_{\{0\}}

(10.9)

Combining (
10.6
),(
10.7
),(
10.9
) and taking

L_{t}^{(1)}=(\widetilde{G}^{(2)}_{t},\widetilde{G}^{(2)}_{t}+\widetilde{G}^{(3)}_{t})^{\prime},\quad L_{t}^{(2)}=\begin{pmatrix}1&0\\
0&1\end{pmatrix},\quad H_{t}=\begin{pmatrix}\widetilde{G}^{*(1)}_{t}\\
\widetilde{G}^{*(1)}_{t}\end{pmatrix}.

in Lemma
6.1
as
t\to\infty

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}\Big(\big(\widetilde{G}^{*(1)}_{t}+\widetilde{G}^{(2)}_{t},\widetilde{G}^{*(1)}_{t}+\widetilde{G}^{(2)}_{t}+\widetilde{G}^{(3)}_{t}\big)|Y_{t}=j,Y_{0}=i\Big)

\displaystyle\quad\quad\stackrel{{\scriptstyle w}}{{\to}}\big(E|\mathfrak{I}^{j}_{1}|\big)^{-\frac{1}{\alpha}}\sigma_{(j,\alpha)}\big(\mathcal{S}_{\alpha}(1,\beta_{j},0),\mathcal{S}_{\alpha}(1,\beta_{j},0)\big)

(10.10)

where
\widetilde{G}^{*(1)}_{t}
satisfies (
6.1
), i.e,

\displaystyle\widetilde{G}^{*(1)}_{t}-\widetilde{G}^{*(1)}_{t-\varepsilon(t)}\to 0,\quad\text{as}\quad t\to\infty,

(10.11)

for some
\varepsilon(t)\to\infty,\&\frac{\varepsilon(t)}{t}\to 0,
which follows from Lemma
10.2
. Using (
10.10
) and
P[Y_{t}=j|Y_{0}=i]\to\pi_{j}
in (
10.4
) as
t\to\infty
the assertion will follow.
∎

Lemma 10.2
.

There exists an increasing function
t\to\varepsilon(t)
such that
\varepsilon(t)\to\infty
and
\frac{\varepsilon(t)}{t}\to 0
as
t\to\infty
for which (
10.11
) holds.

Proofs of Lemma
10.1
and Lemma
10.2
are given in the supplementary material.

10.2
Proof of Theorem
3
(b)

Proof.

We will expand
I_{t}^{(a,b)}
in a different manner than in Theorem
3
(a). Fix
i,j\in S
. Given
E_{\pi}a(\cdot)=0,
the random walk generated by the process
\big(\sum_{k=1}^{n}\log L_{k}^{j}:n\geq 1\big)
has zero drift as the
k
-th increment
\log L_{k}^{j}=-\int_{\tau_{i-1}^{j}}^{\tau_{i}^{j}}a(Y_{s})ds
has mean
0,
but Var
(\log L_{k}^{j})
doesn’t exist unlike Theorem
2
(b). Let

S_{n}^{\otimes j}:=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\log L_{i}^{j},\quad M_{n}^{\otimes j}:=\max_{1\leq k\leq n}\Big\{\sum_{i=1}^{k}\log L_{i}^{j}\Big\},\quad\text{ and }\quad Z_{n}^{\otimes j}:=\max_{1\leq k\leq n}\Big\{\big(\prod_{i=1}^{k-1}L_{i}^{j}\big)|Q_{k}^{j}|\Big\}.

Observe that on
\{Y_{0}=i,Y_{t}=j\},
using (
9.1
)

\displaystyle\big(\log\Phi_{t}^{(a)},\log|I_{t}^{(a,b)}|\big)

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathcal{L}(\cdot|Y_{0}=i,Y_{t}=j)}}{{=}}

\displaystyle\bigg(\log L^{j}_{0}+\log\bigg(\prod_{k=1}^{g^{j}_{t}}L^{j}_{k}\bigg)+\log(e^{-a(j)(t-\tau_{g_{t}^{j}}^{j})}),

(10.12)

\displaystyle\log\bigg|e^{-a(j)(t-\tau_{g_{t}^{j}}^{j})}\bigg[\bigg(\prod_{k=1}^{g_{t}^{j}}L^{j}_{k}\bigg)Q^{j}_{0}+\widetilde{P}^{j}_{g_{t}^{j}}\bigg]+G_{j}^{a,b}\big(t-\tau_{g_{t}^{j}}^{j}\big)\bigg|\bigg)

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{=}}

\displaystyle\bigg(S_{g_{t}^{j}}^{\otimes j}+[\log L^{j}_{0}-a(j)(t-\tau_{g_{t}^{j}}^{j})],

\displaystyle\quad\quad\log Z_{g_{t}^{j}}^{\otimes j}+\log\bigg|B_{t}^{(1)}+\frac{B_{t}^{(2)}}{Z_{g_{t}^{j}}^{\otimes j}}P_{g_{t}^{j}}^{j}\bigg|\bigg)

where

B_{t}^{(1)}=\frac{e^{-a(j)(t-\tau_{g_{t}^{j}}^{j})}\bigg(\prod_{k=1}^{g_{t}^{j}}L^{j}_{k}\bigg)Q^{j}_{0}+G_{j}^{a,b}\big(t-\tau_{g_{t}^{j}}^{j}\big)}{Z_{g_{t}^{j}}^{\otimes j}},\quad\quad B_{t}^{(2)}=e^{-a(j)(t-\tau_{g_{t}^{j}}^{j})},

and denote
\bigg(B_{t}^{(1)}+\frac{B_{t}^{(2)}}{Z_{g_{t}^{j}}^{\otimes j}}P_{g_{t}^{j}}^{j}\bigg)
by
B^{(3)}_{t}.
Consider the probability

\displaystyle P\Big[\Big(\frac{\log\Phi_{t}^{(a)}}{t^{\frac{1}{\alpha}}L_{0}(t)},\frac{\log|I_{t}^{(a,b)}|}{t^{\frac{1}{\alpha}}L_{0}(t)}\Big)^{\prime}\in\cdot|Y_{0}=i\Big]

\displaystyle\quad=\sum_{j\in S}P[Y_{t}=j|Y_{0}=i]P\Big[\Big(\frac{\log\Phi_{t}^{(a)}}{t^{\frac{1}{\alpha}}L_{0}(t)},\frac{\log|I_{t}^{(a,b)}|}{t^{\frac{1}{\alpha}}L_{0}(t)}\Big)^{\prime}\in\cdot|Y_{0}=i,Y_{t}=j\Big]

\displaystyle\quad=\sum_{j\in S}P[Y_{t}=j|Y_{0}=i]P\Big[\widetilde{L}_{t}^{(1)}+L_{t}^{(2)}\widetilde{H}_{t}\in\cdot|Y_{0}=i,Y_{t}=j\Big]

(10.13)

where the identity (
17.2
) is used in (
10.13
) and

\widetilde{L}_{t}^{(1)}=\Big(\frac{\log L_{0}^{j}-a(j)(t-\tau_{g_{t}^{j}}^{j})}{t^{\frac{1}{\alpha}}L_{0}(t)},\frac{\log|B_{t}^{(3)}|}{t^{\frac{1}{\alpha}}L_{0}(t)}\Big)^{\prime},\,\,L_{t}^{(2)}=\begin{pmatrix}1&0\\
0&1\end{pmatrix},\,\widetilde{H}_{t}=\Big(\frac{S^{\otimes}_{g_{t}^{j}}}{t^{\frac{1}{\alpha}}L_{0}(t)},\frac{\log Z_{g_{t}^{j}}^{\otimes}}{t^{\frac{1}{\alpha}}L_{0}(t)}\Big)^{\prime}.

We will apply Lemma
6.1
with the above specifications. Observe that both
B_{t}^{(1)}
and
B_{t}^{(2)}
are
O_{p}(1)
. For each
1\leq k\leq n
, we have

-Z_{n}^{\otimes j}\leq Q_{k}^{j}\left(\prod_{i=1}^{k-1}L_{i}^{j}\right)\leq Z_{n}^{\otimes j}.

This implies that
-nZ_{n}^{\otimes j}\leq P_{n}^{j}\leq nZ_{n}^{\otimes j}
for all
n\geq 1
, which further implies that

\left|\frac{P_{g_{t}^{j}}^{j}}{Z_{g_{t}^{j}}^{\otimes j}}\right|\leq g_{t}^{j}.
Hence,
|B_{t}^{(3)}|=O_{p}(t)
and is strictly positive.

Since
L_{0}^{j},(t-\tau_{g_{t}^{j}}^{j})
are both
O_{P}(1),
hence
\widetilde{L}_{t}^{(1)}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle P}}{{\to}}(0,0)^{\prime}
and by Lemma
10.3
(c)
\widetilde{H}^{\prime}_{t}
verifies the condition
\widetilde{H}^{\prime}_{t}-\widetilde{H}^{\prime}_{t-\varepsilon(t)}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle P}}{{\to}}(0,0)
as
t\to\infty.
The proof will be done if we show as
t\to\infty

\widetilde{H}_{t}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{\to}}\bigg(\frac{1}{E|\mathfrak{I}^{j}_{1}|}\bigg)^{\frac{1}{\alpha}}\sigma_{(j,\alpha)}\big(\mathcal{\bf{S}}_{\alpha,\beta_{j}}(1),\sup_{0\leq u\leq 1}\mathcal{\bf{S}}_{\alpha,\beta_{j}}(u)\big),

since then using
P[Y_{t}=j|Y_{0}=i]\to\pi_{j},
after applying the Lemma
6.1
the LHS of (
10.13
) will lead to

\displaystyle\lim_{t\to\infty}P\Big[\Big(\frac{\log\Phi_{t}^{(a)}}{t^{\frac{1}{\alpha}}L_{0}(t)},\frac{\log|I_{t}^{(a,b)}|}{t^{\frac{1}{\alpha}}L_{0}(t)}\Big)\in\cdot|Y_{0}=i\Big]

\displaystyle\quad=\sum_{j\in S}\pi_{j}\lim_{t\to\infty}P\Big[\Big(\frac{S_{g_{t}^{j}}^{\otimes j}}{t^{\frac{1}{\alpha}}L_{0}(t)},\frac{\log Z_{g_{t}^{j}}^{\otimes j}}{t^{\frac{1}{\alpha}}L_{0}(t)}\Big)\in\cdot|Y_{0}=i\Big]

(10.14)

where we got rid of
\{Y_{t}=j\}
in the conditioning part of RHS of (
10.13
) by virtue of the Lemma
6.1
. From the definitions of
M_{n}^{\otimes j},Z_{n}^{\otimes j},
it follows that

\displaystyle M_{g_{t}^{j}}^{\otimes j}-\max_{1\leq k\leq g_{t}^{j}}\log|Q_{k}^{j}|\quad\leq\quad\log Z_{g_{t}^{j}}^{\otimes j}\quad\leq\quad M_{g_{t}^{j}}^{\otimes j}+\max_{1\leq k\leq g_{t}^{j}}\log|Q_{k}^{j}|

(10.15)

(see also (4.4) of
[
37
]
). Now under the condition (
4.14
) Lemma
10.3
(a) holds and Lemma
10.3
(b) below verifies the weak convergence result as
t\to\infty
. Combining these in (
10.14
) the assertion follows in
t\to\infty
limit.
∎

Lemma 10.3
.

Under assumptions of Theorem
3
(b) as
t\to\infty,

\displaystyle(a)\quad\text{ under \eqref{Theorem2bcond**}\hskip 10.22217pt}\frac{\max_{1\leq k\leq g_{t}^{j}}\log|Q_{k}^{j}|}{t^{\frac{1}{\alpha}}L_{0}(t)}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle a.s}}{{\to}}0,\quad

\displaystyle(b)\quad\bigg(\frac{S_{g_{t}^{j}}^{\otimes j}}{t^{\frac{1}{\alpha}}L_{0}(t)},\frac{M_{g_{t}^{j}}^{\otimes j}}{t^{\frac{1}{\alpha}}L_{0}(t)}\bigg)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{\to}}\bigg(\frac{1}{E|\mathfrak{I}^{j}_{1}|}\bigg)^{\frac{1}{\alpha}}\sigma_{(j,\alpha)}\big(\mathcal{\bf{S}}_{\alpha,\beta_{j}}(1),\sup_{0\leq u\leq 1}\mathcal{\bf{S}}_{\alpha,\beta_{j}}(u)\big)

\displaystyle(c)\quad\widetilde{H}^{\prime}_{t}=\bigg(\frac{S_{g_{t}^{j}}^{\otimes j}}{t^{\frac{1}{\alpha}}L_{0}(t)},\frac{\log Z_{g_{t}^{j}}^{\otimes j}}{t^{\frac{1}{\alpha}}L_{0}(t)}\bigg)\quad\text{satisfies the condition in \eqref{key}, i.e }

\displaystyle\quad\quad\quad\widetilde{H}_{t}-\widetilde{H}_{t-\varepsilon(t)}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle P}}{{\to}}(0,0)^{\prime}\quad\text{for some }\quad\varepsilon(\cdot)\quad\text{as specified in \eqref{key}}.

Proof of Lemma
10.3
, along with proofs of results in Remark
4.2
, Proposition
4.4
, Theorem
4
are all given in the supplementary material.

11
Corollary
4.5

11.1
Proof of Corollary
4.5

For some time-dependent deterministic functions
a_{2}(\cdot),b_{2}(\cdot)
(such that
a_{2}(t)\to\infty
as
t\to\infty
) limit results of
\log|I^{(a,b)}_{t}|
in Theorem
2
& Theorem
3
can be summarized as

\mathcal{L}\Big(\frac{\log|I^{(a,b)}_{t}|}{a_{2}(t)}-b_{2}(t)\Big)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle w}}{{\to}}\text{some mixture type law},

where the functions
a_{2}(\cdot)
and
b_{2}(\cdot)
can be figured out separately from the results of Theorems
2
and
3
. Notably,
a_{2}(t)
grows polynomially in
t
, specifically as
t^{\frac{1}{2}}
in Theorem
2
, and as
t^{\frac{1}{\alpha}}L_{0}(t)
for some slowly varying function
L_{0}(\cdot)
in Theorem
3
. The term
b_{2}(\cdot)
takes the form
(-\sqrt{t}E_{\pi}a(\cdot)\&0)
(respectively
(t^{1-\frac{1}{\alpha}}L^{-1}_{0}(t)\&0)
) in Theorem
2
(a) and
2
(b) (respectively in Theorem
3
(a) and
3
(b)).

We first prove part (a) for
|\widetilde{Z}^{(1)}_{t}|
in a general setting, while the proof for
|\widetilde{Z}^{(2)}_{t}|
requires additional work. Under the assumptions of Theorems
2
(a) and
2
(b), we establish the results, with analogous conclusions for Theorems
3
(a) and
3
(b), which we omit here. The proof of part (b) follows similar steps as in Theorem
4
, completing the assertion.

11.1.1
Proof of part (a)

Proof.

For simplicity of notation, we denote the scaling and shifting functions as
a_{2}(\cdot),b_{2}(\cdot).
We use the fact that for any two non-negative random variables
X,Y
one has
\log\max\{X,Y\}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{=}}\max\{\log X,\log Y\}
(See Lemma
12.4
).

Observe that
\widetilde{Z}^{(1)}_{t}=\widetilde{Z}^{*}_{t}c_{t}
where
\widetilde{Z}^{*}_{t}:=\max\{\Phi_{t}^{(a)},|I^{(a,b)}_{t}|\}
and
c_{t}:=a_{1}\frac{\Phi_{t}^{(a)}}{\widetilde{Z}^{*}_{t}}+b_{1}\frac{|I^{(a,b)}_{t}|}{\widetilde{Z}^{*}_{t}}.
Note that for all
t\geq 0,
almost surely

0\leq|c_{t}|\leq|a_{1}|+|b_{1}|,

showing that
c_{t}
is
O_{p}(1).

\displaystyle\frac{\log|\widetilde{Z}^{(1)}_{t}|}{a_{2}(t)}-b_{2}(t)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{\log|\widetilde{Z}^{*}_{t}c_{t}|}{a_{2}(t)}-b_{2}(t)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\bigg(\frac{\log|\widetilde{Z}^{*}_{t}|}{a_{2}(t)}-b_{2}(t)\bigg)+\frac{\log|c_{t}|}{a_{2}(t)}.

Since
\frac{\log|c_{t}|}{a_{2}(t)}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle P}}{{\to}}0,
it is enough to show that
t\to\infty
weak limit of
\frac{\log|\widetilde{Z}^{*}_{t}|}{a_{2}(t)}-b_{2}(t)
matches with
t\to\infty
weak limit of
\frac{\log|I^{(a,b)}_{t}|}{a_{2}(t)}-b_{2}(t).
Using Lemma
12.4
we observe that

\displaystyle\frac{\log|\widetilde{Z}^{*}_{t}|}{a_{2}(t)}-b_{2}(t)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{\log\max\{\Phi_{t}^{(a)},|I^{(a,b)}_{t}|\}}{a_{2}(t)}-b_{2}(t)

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{=}}

\displaystyle\max\Big\{\frac{\log\Phi_{t}^{(a)}}{a_{2}(t)}-b_{2}(t),\frac{\log|I^{(a,b)}_{t}|}{a_{2}(t)}-b_{2}(t)\Big\}.

Note that any two variables
x,y;
the function
\max\{x,y\}=\frac{(x+y)}{2}+\frac{|x-y|}{2},
is a continuous function in its arguments. Using the continuous mapping theorem it is easy to verify that the weak limit of
\max\Big\{\frac{\log\Phi_{t}^{(a)}}{a_{2}(t)}-b_{2}(t),\frac{\log|I^{(a,b)}_{t}|}{a_{2}(t)}-b_{2}(t)\Big\}
matches with the
t\to\infty
marginal weak limit of
\frac{\log|I^{(a,b)}_{t}|}{a_{2}(t)}-b_{2}(t)
in all cases of Theorem
2
and Theorem
3
, proving the assertion (1) of part (a).

For simplicity, let the bivariate
t\to\infty
weak limit of
\Big(\frac{\big(\log\Phi_{t}^{(a)},\log|I^{(a,b)}_{t}|\big)}{a_{2}(t)}-b_{2}(t)\Big)
be represented by the laws
\mathcal{W}^{*}_{2(a)},\mathcal{W}^{*}_{2(b)},\mathcal{W}^{*}_{3(a)},\mathcal{W}^{*}_{3(b)}
under the assumptions of Theorem
2
(a),
2
(b),
3
(a) and Theorem
3
(b) respectively, whose second marginals are laws
\mathcal{W}_{2(a)},\mathcal{W}_{2(b)},\mathcal{W}_{3(a)}
and
\mathcal{W}_{3(b)}
respectively as defined before Corollary
4.5
.

To prove the result (2) of part (a) for
\widetilde{Z}^{(2)}_{t},
we show that the time asymptotic behaviour of
\big(\log\Phi_{t}^{(a)},\frac{\log|I^{(ma,b)}_{t}|}{m}\big)
and
\big(\log\Phi_{t}^{(a)},\log|I^{(a,b)}_{t}|\big)
will be identical in distribution under same scaling and shifts, under conditions of Theorem
2
(a) and
2
(b) (with some modifications). We show that under conditions of Theorem
2
(a) with
(a,b)
replaced by
(ma,b)
in Assumption
3
(c)

\displaystyle\lim_{t\to\infty}\mathcal{L}\bigg(\frac{\big(\log\Phi_{t}^{(a)},\log|I^{(ma,b)}_{t}|^{\frac{1}{m}}\big)}{a_{2}(t)}-b_{2}(t)\bigg)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{=}}\mathcal{W}^{*}_{2(a)},

(11.1)

and under conditions of Theorem
2
(b) with
(a,b)
replaced by
(ma,b)
in (
4.3
)

\displaystyle\lim_{t\to\infty}\mathcal{L}\bigg(\frac{\big(\log\Phi_{t}^{(a)},\log|I^{(ma,b)}_{t}|^{\frac{1}{m}}\big)}{a_{2}(t)}\bigg)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{=}}\mathcal{W}^{*}_{2(b)}.

(11.2)

The verifications of (
11.1
) and (
11.2
) will be done in Lemma
11.1
. Now we finish the proof by proceeding similar to the proof of
|\widetilde{Z}^{(1)}_{t}|
and using (
11.1
) & (
11.2
). Replacing
|I^{(a,b)}_{t}|
by
|I^{(ma,b)}_{t}|^{\frac{1}{m}},
we define

\widetilde{Z}^{(2)}_{t}:=\widetilde{Z}^{*}_{m,t}c_{m,t}

where
\widetilde{Z}^{*}_{m,t}:=\max\{\Phi_{t}^{(a)},|I^{(ma,b)}_{t}|^{\frac{1}{m}}\}
and
c_{m,t}:=a_{2}\frac{\Phi_{t}^{(a)}}{\widetilde{Z}^{*}_{m,t}}+b_{2}\frac{|I^{(ma,b)}_{t}|^{1/m}}{\widetilde{Z}^{*}_{m,t}}.
Since
c_{m,t}
is asymptotically
O_{p}(1),
hence
\lim_{t\to\infty}\frac{|c_{m,t}|}{a_{2}(t)}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle P}}{{\to}}0.
Following along the lines of proof of
|\widetilde{Z}^{(1)}_{t}|,

\displaystyle\lim_{t\to\infty}\mathcal{L}\bigg(\frac{\log|\widetilde{Z}^{(2)}_{t}|}{a_{2}(t)}-b_{2}(t)\bigg)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\lim_{t\to\infty}\mathcal{L}\bigg(\frac{\log|\widetilde{Z}^{*}_{m,t}|}{a_{2}(t)}-b_{2}(t)\bigg)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\lim_{t\to\infty}\mathcal{L}\bigg(\frac{\log\max\{\Phi_{t}^{(a)},|I^{(ma,b)}_{t}|^{\frac{1}{m}}\}}{a_{2}(t)}-b_{2}(t)\bigg)

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{=}}

\displaystyle\lim_{t\to\infty}\mathcal{L}\bigg(\max\Big\{\frac{\log\Phi_{t}^{(a)}}{a_{2}(t)}-b_{2}(t),\frac{\log|I^{(ma,b)}_{t}|^{\frac{1}{m}}}{a_{2}(t)}-b_{2}(t)\Big\}\bigg).

Using Lemma
11.1
(a) & (b), the result for
|\widetilde{Z}^{(2)}_{t}|
will follow.

Analog versions (i.e the second part of part (a)(2)) under conditions of Theorem
3
(a) and
3
(b) (with
(a,b)
replaced by
(ma,b)
in Assumption
4
(c) and in (
4.14
) respectively) will be similar and we omit their proofs.

Lemma 11.1
.

(a)

(
11.1
) holds under assumptions of Theorem
2
(a) with
(a,b)
replaced by
(ma,b)
in Assumption
3
(c).

(b)

(
11.2
) holds under assumptions of Theorem
2
(b) with
(a,b)
replaced by
(ma,b)
in (
4.3
).

Assertions of part (b) of the Corollary
4.5
follow similarly by proceeding like proofs of Theorem
4
(hence omitted).
∎

Proofs of Lemma
11.1
(a),(b) are given in the supplementary material.

11.2
Proof of Theorem
5

Proof.

We begin with the equation at display (
1.2
) with initial value
\rho_{0}
. Dividing both sides by
\rho^{3}_{t}
will yield

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\rho^{3}_{t}}\frac{d\rho_{t}}{dt}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\rho^{-2}_{t}a(Y_{t})-b(Y_{t}),

\displaystyle-\frac{1}{2}\frac{d\big(\rho^{-2}_{t}\big)}{dt}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\big(\rho^{-2}_{t}\big)a(Y_{t})-b(Y_{t}).

Denoting
X_{t}:=\rho^{-2}_{t},
we get a linear ODE for with semi-Markov
Y
modulated coefficients
a(Y_{t}),b(Y_{t})
as

\displaystyle\frac{dX_{t}}{dt}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 2b(Y_{t})-2a(Y_{t})X_{t}.

(11.3)

\displaystyle\frac{d\big[e^{\int_{0}^{t}2a(Y_{s})ds}X_{t}\big]}{dt}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 2b(Y_{t})e^{\int_{0}^{t}2a(Y_{s})ds}.

\displaystyle X_{t}\Phi_{t}^{(-2a)}-X_{0}\Phi^{(-2a)}_{0}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{t}2b(Y_{s})\Phi^{(-2a)}_{s}ds,

(11.4)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 2I^{(-2a,b)}_{t},

where
\Phi^{(-2a)}_{0}=1,
and (
11.4
) is obtained by multiplying (
11.3
) by
\Phi_{t}^{(-2a)}
in both sides and integrating in
[0,t],
using the notations of integrals in (
1.1
). After simplifying we get that conditioned on
\mathcal{F}_{t}^{Y},

\displaystyle X_{t}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle X_{0}\Phi_{t}^{(2a)}+2I_{t}^{(2a,b)},

\displaystyle\rho^{-2}_{t}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\rho^{-2}_{0}\Phi_{t}^{(2a)}+2I_{t}^{(2a,b)},

(11.5)

\displaystyle\rho^{2}_{t}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\rho_{0}^{-2}\Phi_{t}^{(2a)}+2I_{t}^{(2a,b)}}.

(11.6)

We finish the proof of part (a) when
E_{\pi}a(\cdot)>0.
Observe that unconditionally under assumptions of Theorem
5
(a) the result of Theorem
1
translates to

(\Phi_{t}^{(2a)},I_{t}^{(2a,b)},Y_{t})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{\to}}\Big(0,\sum_{j\in S}\delta_{U}(\{j\})W^{(2a,b)}_{j},U\Big)\quad\text{as}\quad t\to\infty,

where
W^{(2a,b)}_{j}
is defined in (
3.3
) identically as
Z_{j}
where
(a,b)
is replaced by
(2a,b).

Hence using the continuous mapping theorem, one gets

\displaystyle\lim_{t\to\infty}\mathcal{L}(\rho^{2}_{t},Y_{t})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\lim_{t\to\infty}\mathcal{L}\bigg(\frac{1}{\rho_{0}^{-2}\Phi_{t}^{(2a)}+2I_{t}^{(2a,b)}},Y_{t}\bigg)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\Big(\sum_{j\in S}\delta_{U}(\{j\})\frac{1}{2W^{(2a,b)}_{j}},U\Big)

proving the assertion in (
5.2
) of the theorem.

Clearly in divergent cases (i.e,
E_{\pi}a(\cdot)\leq 0
) the state-space representation of the process
(\rho^{-2}_{t}:t\geq 0)
in (
11.5
) marginally evolves like
(\widetilde{Z}_{t}^{(1)}:t\geq 0)
in Corollary
4.5
with
a_{1}=\rho_{0}^{-2},b_{1}=2,
and
(a,b)
is replaced by
(2a,b).
Hence the results for
\log\rho_{t}^{-2}=-2\log|\rho_{t}|
will be identical with the results for
\log|\widetilde{Z}_{t}^{(1)}|
under the assumptions of Theorem
2
, Theorem
3
, and Theorem
4
with
a(\cdot)
replaced by
2a(\cdot)
as the only change. As a result in part (B), the quantity
\widetilde{\sigma}^{2}_{j}
will be identical with the term
\sigma_{j}^{2}
in Theorem
2
. Using Corollary
4.5
the rest parts of Theorem
5
will follow.
∎

11.3
Proof of Theorem
6

Proof.

Beginning with the set-up at (
5.9
) using differentiability of
\beta(\cdot)
we observe that
h^{\prime}(x)=\frac{1}{\beta(x)},h^{\prime\prime}(x)=-\frac{\beta^{\prime}(x)}{\beta^{2}(x)}
. Given the trajectory of
Y
in [0,t], the SDE of
X
is defined by

\displaystyle dX_{s}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\Big[-a(Y_{s})h(X_{s})\beta(X_{s})+\frac{b^{2}(Y_{s})}{2}\beta(X_{s})\beta^{\prime}(X_{s})\Big]dt+b(Y_{s})\beta(X_{s})dW_{s},\quad X_{0}=x_{0}.

Applying Ito’s lemma with the transformation
h(\cdot)
on
X
above yields

\displaystyle dh(X_{s})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\Big[0+h^{\prime}(X_{s})\Big(-a(Y_{s})h(X_{s})\beta(X_{s})+\frac{b^{2}(Y_{s})}{2}\beta(X_{s})\beta^{\prime}(X_{s})\Big)

\displaystyle+\frac{b^{2}(Y_{s})}{2}h^{\prime\prime}(X_{s})\beta^{2}(X_{s})\Big]ds+\Big(h^{\prime}(X_{s})b(Y_{s})\beta(X_{s})\Big)dW_{s}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-a(Y_{s})h(X_{s})ds+b(Y_{s})dW_{s}.

Now integrating both sides with
s
in the range
[0,t]
yields,

\displaystyle h(X_{t})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle h(x_{0})e^{-\int_{0}^{t}a(Y_{s})ds}+\int_{0}^{t}b(Y_{s})e^{-\int_{s}^{t}a(Y_{r})dr}dW_{s}

(11.7)

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{=}}

\displaystyle h(x_{0})\Phi^{(a)}_{t}+\sqrt{\int_{0}^{t}b^{2}(Y_{s})e^{-\int_{s}^{t}2a(Y_{r})dr}ds}N

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{=}}

\displaystyle h(x_{0})\Phi^{(a)}_{t}+\sqrt{I^{(2a,b^{2})}_{t}}N

for some
N\sim N(0,1)
independent of
Y,
hence independent of
(\Phi^{(a)}_{t},I^{(2a,b^{2})}_{t}).
Since
h
does not vanish in
\mathbb{R},
and it is monotone, hence the inverse
h^{-1}(\cdot)
exists. Using representation (
11.7
), under Assumptions
1
and
2
using the result of Theorem
1
and continuous mapping theorem following holds:

(h(X_{t}),Y_{t})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{\to}}\Big(\sum_{j\in S}\delta_{U}(\{j\})\sqrt{Z_{j}}N,U\Big)

where
U\sim\pi
independent of
Z_{j}
and
N\sim N(0,1),
where
Z_{j}
satisfies weak solution of (
3.3
) with
(a,b)
replaced by
(2a,b^{2}).
Now using the continuity of
h^{-1}(\cdot)
and continuous mapping theorem the result in part (A) follows.

For the rest of part (B), (C), (D) we observe that marginal evolution of the process

h(X_{t})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{=}}h(x_{0})\Phi^{(a)}_{t}+\sqrt{I^{(2a,b^{2})}_{t}}N,

resembles with
Z_{t}^{(2)}
in Corollary
4.5
with
a_{2}=h(x_{0}),b_{2}=N,m=2;
hence all the results will follow by replacing
(a,b)
by
(a,b^{2})
in Corollary
4.5
for
|Z_{t}^{(2)}|
.
∎

Proof of Corollary
5.2
is given in the supplementary material.

12
Appendix

Lemma 12.1
.

Under Assumption
1
,

\lim_{t\to\infty}P_{{}_{i}}\Big[t-\tau_{g_{t}^{j}}^{j}>x,Y_{t}=j\Big]=\frac{\mu_{j}\sum_{k\in S}P_{jk}\int_{x}^{\infty}(1-F_{jk}(y))dy}{\sum_{k\in S}\mu_{k}m_{k}}.

Lemma 12.2
.

Under Assumptions
1
and
2
the quantities
\log L_{0}^{j}
and
|Q_{0}^{j}|
are
O_{P}(1).

Lemma 12.3
.

(a)

Suppose Assumption
1
holds and
c_{\cdot}
is a function defined on
\mathbb{N}
as (
2.15
) and its definition is extended to the whole
\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
for any arbitrary slowly varying function
L(\cdot)
. Let
g^{j}_{t}
be defined as the number of total renewals to the state
j,
before time
t.
Then for any fixed
s>0,

\lim_{t\to\infty}c^{-1}_{g_{st}^{j}}/c^{-1}_{t}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle a.s}}{{\to}}\Big(\frac{s}{E|\mathfrak{I}^{j}_{1}|}\Big)^{-\frac{1}{\alpha}}.

(b)

For any function
L(\cdot)
slowly varying at
+\infty,
and any two functions
a_{1}(\cdot),b_{1}(\cdot):\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}\to\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
such that both
a_{1}(t),b_{1}(t)\to\infty
as
t\to\infty
and
a_{1}\leq b_{1};
for any
\epsilon>0
there exists
t_{0,\epsilon}\geq 0,
such that for all
t\geq t_{0,\epsilon},

\displaystyle(1-\epsilon)\Big(\frac{b_{1}(t)}{a_{1}(t)}\Big)^{-\epsilon}\leq\frac{L(b_{1}(t))}{L(a_{1}(t))}\leq(1+\epsilon)\Big(\frac{b_{1}(t)}{a_{1}(t)}\Big)^{\epsilon}

(12.1)

(c)

For any function
L(\cdot)
slowly varying at
t\to\infty,

\lim_{t\to\infty}\frac{\log L(t)}{\log t}\to 0
.

Lemma 12.4
.

For any two non-negative random variables
X,Y
one has

\log\max\{X,Y\}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{=}}\max\{\log X,\log Y\}.

Proofs of these lemmas are given in the
Supplementary material
.

14
Proof of supplementary results of Theorem 1

We prove Lemma 9.2 here.

14.1
Proof of
Lemma 9.2(a)

We prove this lemma by showing

•

Step
1
:

R_{t}^{(1)}
and absolute value of first term of
R_{t}^{(2)}
both will converge to
0
in probability as
t\to\infty,

•

Step
2
:

P_{g_{t}^{j}}^{j}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{\to}}V_{j}^{*}
where
V_{j}^{*}
is defined in (3.3), (3.4) (i.e second term of
R_{t}^{(2)}
will converge to the solution of SRE),

and as a consequence of Slutsky’s theorem, the assertion of this lemma holds.

To show
Step
1
, observe that
R_{t}^{(1)}
and first term of
R_{t}^{(2)}
are respectively

\displaystyle\bigg(\exp\Big\{t\Big(\frac{\sum_{i=1}^{g_{t}}\log L^{j}_{i}}{g_{t}}+\frac{\log L^{j}_{0}}{g_{t}}\Big)\frac{g_{t}}{t}\Big\},\exp\Big\{t\Big(\frac{\sum_{i=1}^{g_{t}}\log L^{j}_{i}}{g_{t}}+\frac{\log|Q^{j}_{0}|}{g_{t}}\Big)\frac{g_{t}}{t}\Big\}\bigg).

A consequence of renewal theorem, law of large number (validated under Assumption 1) will give that

\displaystyle\frac{\sum_{i=1}^{g_{t}}\log L^{j}_{i}}{g_{t}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle P}}{{\to}}E_{\pi}\log L^{j}_{i}=-E|\mathfrak{I}^{j}_{1}|E_{\pi}a(\cdot)<0\quad\text{and}\quad\frac{g_{t}}{t}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle a.s}}{{\to}}\frac{1}{E|\mathfrak{I}^{j}_{1}|}.

(14.1)

Since
\log L_{0}^{j},|Q_{0}^{j}|
are both
O_{{}_{P}}(1)
a consequence of Lemma 12.2, both of
\frac{\log L^{j}_{0}}{g_{t}},\frac{\log|Q^{j}_{0}|}{g_{t}}
will go to
0
in probability. This implies as
t\to\infty,

\bigg(\bigg(\prod_{k=1}^{g_{t}^{j}}L^{j}_{k}\bigg)L^{j}_{0},\bigg(\prod_{k=1}^{g_{t}^{j}}L^{j}_{k}\bigg)|Q^{j}_{0}|\bigg)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle P}}{{\to}}(0,0).

Lemma 14.1
.

Step
2
holds under assumptions of Theorem 1, i.e under Assumptions 1 and 2,
P_{g_{t}^{j}}^{j}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{\to}}V_{j}^{*}
as
t\to\infty
.

14.1.1
Proof of Lemma
14.1

Proof.

Define
P^{j}_{\infty}:=\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}\bigg(\prod_{i=1}^{k-1}L_{i}^{j}\bigg)Q_{k}^{j},
and we show that
P^{j}_{g_{t}^{j}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle P}}{{\to}}P^{j}_{\infty}
and
P^{j}_{\infty}
satisfies unique solution of SRE that is identical as
\mathcal{L}(V_{j}^{*})
(defined in (3.3), (3.4)). Note that

\displaystyle P^{j}_{\infty}=\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}\bigg(\prod_{i=1}^{k-1}L_{i}^{j}\bigg)Q_{k}^{j}=Q_{1}^{j}+L_{1}^{j}\Big(\sum_{k=2}^{\infty}\bigg(\prod_{i=2}^{k-1}L_{i}^{j}\bigg)Q_{k}^{j}\Big).

Now since
(L_{1}^{j},Q_{1}^{j})\perp\{(L_{i}^{j},Q_{i}^{j}):i\geq 2\},
it follows that the second term inside first bracket of RHS
\sum_{k=2}^{\infty}\bigg(\prod_{i=2}^{k-1}L_{i}^{j}\bigg)Q_{k}^{j}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{=}}P^{j}_{\infty}
and also
(L_{1}^{j},Q_{1}^{j})\perp\sum_{k=2}^{\infty}\bigg(\prod_{i=2}^{k-1}L_{i}^{j}\bigg)Q_{k}^{j},
it follows that
P^{j}_{\infty}
satisfies the SRE at (3.3). Now since under Assumption 2

E\log|L_{1}^{j}|=-E|\mathfrak{I}^{j}_{1}|E_{\pi}a(\cdot)<0,\quad\text{ and }\quad E\log^{+}|Q_{1}^{j}|<\infty,

it follows that the solution to the SRE
X\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{=}}Q_{1}^{j}+L_{1}^{j}X
is unique in distribution (Lemma 1.4(a) and Vervaat’s Theorem 1.5 in
[
60
]
). So it follows that
\mathcal{L}(P_{\infty}^{j})=\mathcal{L}(V_{j}^{*})
in (3.3), (3.4).

Observe that for any
t>0
,

\displaystyle P^{j}_{\infty}-P^{j}_{g_{t}^{j}}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{=}}

\displaystyle\Big(\prod_{i=1}^{g_{t}^{j}}L_{i}^{j}\Big)\bigg[\sum_{k=g_{t}^{j}+1}^{\infty}\Big(\prod_{i=g_{t}^{j}+1}^{k-1}L_{i}^{j}\Big)Q^{j}_{k}\bigg]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\exp\Big\{t\Big(\frac{\sum_{i=1}^{g_{t}}\log L^{j}_{i}}{g_{t}}\Big)\frac{g_{t}^{j}}{t}\Big\}\bigg[\sum_{k=g_{t}^{j}+1}^{\infty}\bigg(\prod_{i=g_{t}^{j}+1}^{k-1}L_{i}^{j}\Big)Q^{j}_{k}\bigg].

As
t\to\infty,
using (
14.1
) the first term
\prod_{i=1}^{g_{t}^{j}}L_{i}^{j}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle P}}{{\to}}0.
To prove the assertion, we prove that the second term (in the RHS above) is
O_{P}(1).
Observe that for any
t\geq 0,

\displaystyle\big((L^{j}_{g^{j}_{t}+1},Q^{j}_{g^{j}_{t}+1}),(L^{j}_{g^{j}_{t}+2},Q^{j}_{g^{j}_{t}+2}),\ldots\big)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{=}}\big((L^{j}_{1},Q^{j}_{1}),(L^{j}_{2},Q^{j}_{2}),\ldots\big)

(14.2)

which follows by conditioning w.r.t
g^{j}_{t}
and using argument similar to Proposition 2.2 (ii). A consequence of (
14.2
) gives

\displaystyle P^{j}_{\infty}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{=}}\sum_{k=g_{t}^{j}+1}^{\infty}\bigg(\prod_{i=g_{t}^{j}+1}^{k-1}L_{i}^{j}\Big)Q^{j}_{k}=Q^{j}_{g_{t}+1}+L^{j}_{g_{t}+1}\big(Q^{j}_{g_{t}+2}+L^{j}_{g_{t}+2}\big(\ldots\big)\big),

(14.3)

and since distribution of
P^{j}_{\infty}
uniquely exists under Assumption 2, the random quantity
\sum_{k=g_{t}^{j}+1}^{\infty}\bigg(\prod_{i=g_{t}^{j}+1}^{k-1}L_{i}^{j}\Big)Q^{j}_{k}
is
O_{{}_{P}}(1),
proving our assertion.
∎

14.2
Proof of
Lemma 9.2(b)

Since first term of
R^{(2)}_{t}
i.e
\big(\prod_{k=1}^{g_{t}^{j}}L^{j}_{k}\big)Q^{j}_{0}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle P}}{{\to}}0
as
t\to\infty.
The assertion holds if we show (6.1) of Lemma 6.1 with
P_{g_{t}^{j}}^{j}
as
H_{t}.
Note that from Lemma 9.2(a) it follows that unconditionally
P_{g_{t}^{j}}^{j}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{\to}}V^{*}_{j},
so we can take
V^{*}_{j}
as
H_{\infty}
in (6.1). We are only left to prove
P_{g_{t}^{j}}^{j}-P_{g_{t-\epsilon(t)}^{j}}^{j}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle P}}{{\to}}0
for some function
\epsilon(\cdot)
such that
\epsilon(t)\to\infty
and
\frac{\epsilon(t)}{t}\to 0
as
t\to\infty.

\displaystyle P_{g_{t}^{j}}^{j}-P_{g_{t-\epsilon(t)}^{j}}^{j}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{k=1}^{g_{t}^{j}}\Big(\prod_{i=1}^{k-1}L_{i}^{j}\Big)Q_{k}^{j}-\sum_{k=1}^{g_{t-\epsilon(t)}^{j}}\Big(\prod_{i=1}^{k-1}L_{i}^{j}\Big)Q_{k}^{j}

(14.4)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\bigg(\prod_{i=1}^{g_{t-\epsilon(t)}^{j}}L_{i}^{j}\bigg)\bigg[\sum_{k=g^{j}_{t-\epsilon(t)}+1}^{g^{j}_{t}}\bigg(\prod_{i=g_{t-\epsilon(t)}^{j}+1}^{k-1}L_{i}^{j}\bigg)Q_{k}^{j}\bigg]

Observe that absolute value of second term of the product in (
14.4
) can be bounded by

\displaystyle\bigg|\sum_{k=g^{j}_{t-\epsilon(t)}+1}^{g^{j}_{t}}\bigg(\prod_{i=g_{t-\epsilon(t)}^{j}+1}^{k-1}L_{i}^{j}\bigg)Q_{k}^{j}\bigg|

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\sum_{k=g^{j}_{t-\epsilon(t)}+1}^{g_{t}^{j}}\bigg(\prod_{i=g_{t-\epsilon(t)}^{j}+1}^{k-1}L_{i}^{j}\bigg)|Q_{k}^{j}|

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\sum_{k=g^{j}_{t-\epsilon(t)}+1}^{\infty}\bigg(\prod_{i=g_{t-\epsilon(t)}^{j}+1}^{k-1}L_{i}^{j}\bigg)|Q_{k}^{j}|.

Note that
\sum_{k=g^{j}_{t-\epsilon(t)}+1}^{\infty}\bigg(\prod_{i=g_{t-\epsilon(t)}^{j}+1}^{k-1}L_{i}^{j}\bigg)|Q_{k}^{j}|\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{=}}\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}\bigg(\prod_{i=1}^{k-1}L_{i}^{j}\bigg)|Q_{k}^{j}|
(using ideas similar to (
14.2
) and (
14.3
)) which satisfies the unique solution of the SRE

X=L_{1}^{j}X+|Q_{1}^{j}|,\quad X\perp(L_{1}^{j},Q_{1}^{j})

under conditions
E\log L_{k}^{j}=-E|\mathfrak{I}^{j}_{1}|E_{\pi}a(\cdot)<0,E\log^{+}|Q_{k}^{j}|<\infty
ensured by Assumption 2. This implies that second term of the product in (
14.4
) is
O_{P}(1)
.

Observe that first term in the product of (
14.4
) is

\bigg(\prod_{i=1}^{g_{t-\epsilon(t)}^{j}}L_{i}^{j}\bigg)=\exp\Big\{(t-\epsilon(t))\Big(\frac{\sum_{i=1}^{g_{t-\epsilon(t)}}\log L^{j}_{i}}{g_{t-\epsilon(t)}}\Big)\frac{g_{t-\epsilon(t)}^{j}}{t-\epsilon(t)}\Big\}.

A consequence of Renewal Theorem and Law of large number leads to (
14.1
) with
t
replaced by
t-\epsilon(t)
and as
t-\epsilon(t)\to\infty,
one has
\prod_{i=1}^{g_{t-\epsilon(t)}^{j}}L_{i}^{j}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle P}}{{\to}}0.

\square

15
Proof of Theorem 2

15.1
Proof of
Theorem 2(a)

We begin with
I^{(a,b)}_{t}=\Phi^{(a)}_{t}\widetilde{I}^{(a,b)}_{t}
where
\widetilde{I}^{(a,b)}_{t}=\int_{0}^{t}b(Y_{s})e^{\int_{0}^{s}a(Y_{r})dr}ds.
Fix arbitrary
i,j\in S
. Observe that on
\{Y_{0}=i,Y_{t}=j\}
using renewal regenerative structure of
Y
(as a consequence of Assumption 1)

\displaystyle\Phi_{t}^{(a)}=L_{0}^{j}\Big(\prod_{i=1}^{g_{t}^{j}}L_{i}^{j}\Big)e^{-a(j)(t-\tau_{g_{t}^{j}}^{j})},\,\,\,\widetilde{I}^{(a,b)}_{t}=\bigg[\frac{Q_{0}^{j}+S^{**}_{g_{t}^{j}}}{L_{0}^{j}}+\frac{G_{j}^{(a,b)}(t-\tau_{g_{t}^{j}})}{\Phi_{t}^{(a)}}\bigg]

where for any integer
n\geq 1,\quad

S^{**}_{n}:=P_{n}\big(\frac{1}{L_{1}^{j}},\frac{Q_{1}^{j}}{L_{1}^{j}}\big)=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\frac{Q_{i}^{j}}{L_{i}^{j}}\prod_{k=1}^{i-1}\Big(\frac{1}{L_{k}^{j}}\Big).

Let
G^{(1)}_{t},G^{(2)}_{t},G^{(3)}_{t}
be defined as

\displaystyle G^{(1)}_{t}=\frac{\Big(\prod_{i=1}^{g_{t}^{j}}L_{i}^{j}\Big)^{\frac{1}{\sqrt{t}}}}{e^{-\sqrt{t}E_{\pi}a(\cdot)}},\quad G^{(2)}_{t}=\Big[L_{0}^{j}e^{-a(j)(t-\tau_{g_{t}^{j}}^{j})}\Big]^{\frac{1}{\sqrt{t}}},\,

\displaystyle G^{(3)}_{t}=\bigg[\frac{Q_{0}^{j}+S^{**}_{g_{t}^{j}}}{L_{0}^{j}}+\frac{G_{j}^{(a,b)}(t-\tau_{g_{t}^{j}})}{\Phi_{t}^{(a)}}\bigg]^{\frac{1}{\sqrt{t}}}.

Observe that

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}\Big(|\widetilde{I}^{(a,b)}_{t}|^{\frac{1}{\sqrt{t}}}\mid Y_{0}=i,Y_{t}=j\Big)=\mathcal{L}\Big(G^{(3)}_{t}\mid Y_{0}=i,Y_{t}=j\Big)

(15.1)

and

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}\bigg(\bigg(\frac{[\Phi^{(a)}_{t}]^{\frac{1}{\sqrt{t}}}}{e^{-\sqrt{t}E_{\pi}a(\cdot)}},\frac{|I^{(a,b)}_{t}|^{\frac{1}{\sqrt{t}}}}{e^{-\sqrt{t}E_{\pi}a(\cdot)}}\bigg)\mid Y_{0}=i,Y_{t}=j\bigg)

\displaystyle\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,=\mathcal{L}\Big(\big(G^{(1)}_{t}G^{(2)}_{t},G^{(1)}_{t}G^{(2)}_{t}G^{(3)}_{t}\big)\mid Y_{0}=i,Y_{t}=j\Big).

For any
A_{1},A_{2}\in\mathcal{B}(\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}),

\displaystyle P\bigg[\bigg(\frac{[\Phi^{(a)}_{t}]^{\frac{1}{\sqrt{t}}}}{e^{-\sqrt{t}E_{\pi}a(\cdot)}},\frac{|I^{(a,b)}_{t}|^{\frac{1}{\sqrt{t}}}}{e^{-\sqrt{t}E_{\pi}a(\cdot)}}\bigg)\in A_{1}\times A_{2}\mid Y_{0}=i\bigg]

\displaystyle\,\,\,\,\,\,=\quad\sum_{j\in S}P\bigg[\bigg(\frac{[\Phi^{(a)}_{t}]^{\frac{1}{\sqrt{t}}}}{e^{-\sqrt{t}E_{\pi}a(\cdot)}},\frac{|I^{(a,b)}_{t}|^{\frac{1}{\sqrt{t}}}}{e^{-\sqrt{t}E_{\pi}a(\cdot)}}\bigg)\in A_{1}\times A_{2},Y_{t}=j\mid Y_{0}=i\bigg]

\displaystyle\,\,\,\,\,\,=\quad\sum_{j\in S}P[Y_{t}=j\mid Y_{0}=i]P\Big[\big(G^{(1)}_{t}G^{(2)}_{t},G^{(1)}_{t}G^{(2)}_{t}G^{(3)}_{t}\big)\in A_{1}\times A_{2}\mid Y_{0}=i,Y_{t}=j\Big].

(15.2)

As a consequence of the Ergodic theorem and Renewal theorem,

E\log L_{1}^{j}=-E|\mathfrak{I}^{j}_{1}|E_{\pi}a(\cdot),\,\,\,\,\&\,\,\,\,\frac{g_{t}^{j}}{t}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle a.s}}{{\to}}\frac{1}{E|\mathfrak{I}^{j}_{1}|}\,\,\,\,\text{as}\,\,\,\,t\to\infty.

Applying Central Limit Theorem for the renewal reward process
\big(\sum_{k=1}^{g_{t}^{j}}\log L_{k}^{j}:t\geq 0\big)
, with the reward
\log L_{k}^{j}
for the
k
-th interval
\mathfrak{I}_{k}^{j}
(see Theorem 2.2.5 of
[
59
]
), with the continuous mappling theorem using the map
x\to e^{x},
with
N\sim N(0,1),

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}\Big(G_{t}^{(1)}|Y_{0}=i\Big)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle w}}{{\to}}\mathcal{L}\bigg(exp\Big\{\frac{\sigma_{j}}{\sqrt{E|\mathfrak{I}_{k}^{j}|}}N\Big\}\bigg)

(15.3)

Using arguments similar to step 2 of Lemma 9.2(a) in Theorem 1, with the conditions (i.e,
E_{\pi}a(\cdot)<0
& Assumption 3(c))

E\Big[\frac{1}{L_{i}^{j}}\Big]<0,\quad E\log^{+}\Big(\frac{Q_{i}^{j}}{L_{i}^{j}}\Big)<\infty,

one finds that
S^{**}_{g^{j}_{t}}\to S^{**}
as
t\to\infty,
where

\displaystyle S^{**}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{=}}\frac{1}{L_{i}^{j}}S^{**}+\frac{Q_{i}^{j}}{L_{i}^{j}},\quad S^{**}\perp\Big(\frac{1}{L_{i}^{j}},\frac{Q_{i}^{j}}{L_{i}^{j}}\Big).

(15.4)

Observe further, that in the transient regime, as
t\to\infty,

\frac{\log\Phi_{t}^{(a)}}{t}\to-E_{\pi}a(\cdot),

showing that
\Phi_{t}^{(a)}
diverges exponentially fast as
t\to\infty.
Also, under Assumption 1
G^{(a,b)}_{j}(t-\tau_{g_{t}^{j}})
is
O_{p}(1)
and using
S^{**}_{g^{j}_{t}}\to S^{**},
as
t\to\infty

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}\Big(\big(G_{t}^{(3)}\big)^{\sqrt{t}}|Y_{0}=i\Big)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle w}}{{\to}}\mathcal{L}\Big(\frac{Q_{0}^{j}+S^{**}}{L_{0}^{j}}\Big),

(15.5)

and this further implies that

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}\big(G_{t}^{(3)}|Y_{0}=i\big)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle w}}{{\to}}\delta_{\{1\}}\quad\text{as}\quad t\to\infty.

(15.6)

Since
G_{t}^{(2)}=e^{\frac{\log L_{0}^{j}}{\sqrt{t}}-a(j)\frac{(t-\tau_{g_{t}^{j}})}{\sqrt{t}}},
and both
L_{0}^{j},(t-\tau_{g_{t}^{j}})
are
O_{p}(1),
as
t\to\infty

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}\big(G_{t}^{(2)}|Y_{0}=i\big)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle w}}{{\to}}\delta_{\{1\}}.

(15.7)

Combining (
15.3
), (
15.6
), (
15.7
) and taking

L_{t}^{(1)}=(0,0)^{\prime},\quad L_{t}^{(2)}=\begin{pmatrix}G_{t}^{(2)}&0\\
0&G_{t}^{(2)}G_{t}^{(3)}\end{pmatrix},\quad H_{t}=\begin{pmatrix}G_{t}^{(1)}\\
G_{t}^{(1)}\end{pmatrix},

in Lemma 6.1 as
t\to\infty,

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}\Big(G_{t}^{(1)}G_{t}^{(2)},G_{t}^{(1)}G_{t}^{(2)}G_{t}^{(3)}|Y_{0}=i,Y_{t}=j\Big)

\displaystyle\quad\stackrel{{\scriptstyle w}}{{\to}}\mathcal{L}\bigg(exp\Big\{\frac{\sigma_{j}}{\sqrt{E|\mathfrak{I}_{k}^{j}|}}N\Big\},exp\Big\{\frac{\sigma_{j}}{\sqrt{E|\mathfrak{I}_{k}^{j}|}}N\Big\}\bigg)

(15.8)

given
H_{t}
satisfies the condition (6.1), which holds if
G^{(1)}_{t}
satisfies (6.1). This holds if
\log G^{(1)}_{t}
satisfies (6.1),

\displaystyle\log G^{(1)}_{t}-\log G^{(1)}_{t-\epsilon(t)}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle P}}{{\to}}0,\quad\text{as}\quad t\to\infty,

(15.9)

for some
\epsilon(\cdot)
such that
\frac{\epsilon(t)}{t}\to 0
which follows from Lemma
15.1
.

It follows that, using
\log(\cdot)
transformation on both sides of (
15.2
)

\displaystyle P\bigg[\bigg(\frac{\log\Phi^{(a)}_{t}+tE_{\pi}a(\cdot)}{\sqrt{t}},\frac{\log|I^{(a,b)}_{t}|+tE_{\pi}a(\cdot)}{\sqrt{t}}\bigg)\in A_{1}\times A_{2}|Y_{0}=i\bigg]

\displaystyle\quad=\sum_{j\in S}P\big[\big(\log(G^{(1)}_{t}G^{(2)}_{t}),\log(G^{(1)}_{t}G^{(2)}_{t}G^{(3)}_{t})\big)\in A_{1}\times A_{2}|Y_{0}=i,Y_{t}=j\big]

\displaystyle\quad\quad\quad\times P[Y_{t}=j|Y_{0}=i],

(15.10)

Observe that
P[Y_{t}=j|Y_{0}=i]\to\pi_{j},
and taking
\log(\cdot)
transformation in both co-ordinates of (
15.8
) desired limit of each term inside the sum will be obtained. Applying the dominated convergence theorem using the trivial upper bound
1,
for the first term in the above sum completes the proof. Finally taking the
t\to\infty
limit, the assertion will follow.

\square

Lemma 15.1
.

There exists an increasing function
t\to\epsilon(t)
such that
\epsilon(t)\to\infty
and
\frac{\epsilon(t)}{t}\to 0
as
t\to\infty,
such that (
15.9
) holds for
\log G_{t}^{(1)}.

Proof.

Take
\varepsilon(t):=\sqrt{t}
.
Define
\widetilde{G}^{(1)}_{t}:=\sum_{i=1}^{g_{t}^{j}}(Y^{*}_{i}-Z^{*}_{i})/\sqrt{t}
, where

Y^{*}_{k}:=\log L^{j}_{k}-E\log L^{j}_{k},\quad Z^{*}_{k}:=(|\mathfrak{I}^{j}_{k}|-E|\mathfrak{I}^{j}_{k}|)E_{\pi}a(\cdot),

for
k=1,\ldots,g_{t}^{j}
are two sequences of random variables with zero means. Note that it is sufficient to prove condition (
15.9
) for
\widetilde{G}^{(1)}_{t}
since

\displaystyle\log G^{(1)}_{t}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{\sum_{i=1}^{g^{j}_{t}}\log L_{i}^{j}-tE_{\pi}a(\cdot)}{\sqrt{t}}

(15.11)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\widetilde{G}^{(1)}_{t}-\frac{[\tau_{0}+(t-\tau_{g^{j}_{t}})]E_{\pi}a(\cdot)}{\sqrt{t}}

and the second term in the right-hand side above is
o_{P}(1).
Now observe

\displaystyle\widetilde{G}^{(1)}_{t}-\widetilde{G}^{(1)}_{t-\sqrt{t}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{\sum_{i=1}^{g^{j}_{t-\sqrt{t}}}Y^{*}_{i}}{\sqrt{t}}\Big[1-\Big(1-\frac{1}{\sqrt{t}}\Big)^{-1/2}\Big]+\frac{\sum_{i=g^{j}_{t-\sqrt{t}}+1}^{g^{j}_{t}}Y^{*}_{i}}{\sqrt{t}}

(15.12)

\displaystyle-\frac{\sum_{i=1}^{g^{j}_{t-\sqrt{t}}}Z^{*}_{i}}{\sqrt{t}}\Big[1-\Big(1-\frac{1}{\sqrt{t}}\Big)^{-1/2}\Big]-\frac{\sum_{i=g^{j}_{t-\sqrt{t}}+1}^{g^{j}_{t}}Z^{*}_{i}}{\sqrt{t}}

Using the asymptotic approximation

\Big(1-\frac{1}{\sqrt{t}}\Big)^{-1/2}=1+\frac{1}{2\sqrt{t}}+O(t^{-1})

shows that

\frac{\sum_{i=1}^{g^{j}_{t-\sqrt{t}}}Y^{*}_{i}}{\sqrt{t}}\Big[1-\Big(1-\frac{1}{\sqrt{t}}\Big)^{-1/2}\Big]=-\frac{\sum_{i=1}^{g^{j}_{t-\sqrt{t}}}Y^{*}_{i}}{2t}+o_{{}_{P}}(t^{-1/2})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle P}}{{\to}}-\frac{EY^{*}_{k}}{2E|\mathfrak{I}^{j}_{1}|}=0.

by the renewal version of the law of large numbers. Also
\frac{\sum_{i=1}^{g^{j}_{t-\sqrt{t}}}Z^{*}_{i}}{\sqrt{t}}\Big[1-\Big(1-\frac{1}{\sqrt{t}}\Big)^{-1/2}\Big]\stackrel{{\scriptstyle P}}{{\to}}0
holds similarly. By a similar argument as
t\to\infty,

\displaystyle\frac{\sum_{i=g^{j}_{t-\sqrt{t}}+1}^{g^{j}_{t}}Y^{*}_{i}}{\sqrt{t}}=\frac{\sum_{i=g^{j}_{t-\sqrt{t}}+1}^{g^{j}_{t}}Y^{*}_{i}}{g^{j}_{t}-g^{j}_{t-\sqrt{t}}}\frac{g^{j}_{t}-g^{j}_{t-\sqrt{t}}}{\sqrt{t}}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{a.s.}}}{{\to}}

\displaystyle\frac{1}{E|\mathfrak{I}^{j}_{1}|}EY^{*}_{k}=0,\quad\text{and}

\displaystyle\frac{\sum_{i=g^{j}_{t-\sqrt{t}}+1}^{g^{j}_{t}}Z^{*}_{i}}{\sqrt{t}}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle a.s.}}{{\to}}

\displaystyle\frac{EZ^{*}_{k}}{E|\mathfrak{I}^{j}_{1}|}=0.

The verification is complete.
∎

15.2
Proof of
Theorem 2(b)

Proof.

We will expand
I_{t}^{(a,b)}
in a different manner than in Theorem 2(a), but before that we set few ideas and notations regarding random walk with zero drift. Fix
i,j\in S
. Given
E_{\pi}a(\cdot)=0,
the random walk generated by the process
\big(\sum_{k=1}^{n}\log L_{k}^{j}:n\geq 1\big)
has zero drift as the
k
-th increment
\log L_{k}^{j}=-\int_{\tau_{i-1}^{j}}^{\tau_{i}^{j}}a(Y_{s})ds
has mean
0
and following variance

\text{Var}\big(\log L_{k}^{j}\big)=\text{Var}\Big(-\int_{\tau_{i-1}^{j}}^{\tau_{i}^{j}}a(Y_{s})ds\Big)=\sigma_{j}^{2}\quad\text{(since}\,\,E_{\pi}a(\cdot)=0).

Let

S_{n}^{\otimes j}:=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\log L_{i}^{j},\quad M_{n}^{\otimes j}:=\max_{1\leq k\leq n}\Big\{\sum_{i=1}^{k}\log L_{i}^{j}\Big\},\quad\text{ and }\quad Z_{n}^{\otimes j}:=\max_{1\leq k\leq n}\Big\{\big(\prod_{i=1}^{k-1}L_{i}^{j}\big)|Q_{k}^{j}|\Big\}.

Observe that on
\{Y_{0}=i,Y_{t}=j\},
using (9.1)

\displaystyle\big(\log\Phi_{t}^{(a)},\log|I_{t}^{(a,b)}|\big)

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathcal{L}(\cdot|Y_{0}=i,Y_{t}=j)}}{{=}}

\displaystyle\bigg(\log L^{j}_{0}+\log\bigg(\prod_{k=1}^{g^{j}_{t}}L^{j}_{k}\bigg)+\log(e^{-a(j)(t-\tau_{g_{t}^{j}}^{j})}),

(15.13)

\displaystyle\log\bigg|e^{-a(j)(t-\tau_{g_{t}^{j}}^{j})}\bigg[\bigg(\prod_{k=1}^{g_{t}^{j}}L^{j}_{k}\bigg)Q^{j}_{0}+\widetilde{P}^{j}_{g_{t}^{j}}\bigg]+G_{j}^{a,b}\big(t-\tau_{g_{t}^{j}}^{j}\big)\bigg|\bigg)

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{=}}

\displaystyle\bigg(S_{g_{t}^{j}}^{\otimes j}+[\log L^{j}_{0}-a(j)(t-\tau_{g_{t}^{j}}^{j})],

\displaystyle\quad\quad\log Z_{g_{t}^{j}}^{\otimes j}+\log\bigg|B_{t}^{(1)}+\frac{B_{t}^{(2)}}{Z_{g_{t}^{j}}^{\otimes j}}P_{g_{t}^{j}}^{j}\bigg|\bigg)

where

\displaystyle B_{t}^{(1)}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{e^{-a(j)(t-\tau_{g_{t}^{j}}^{j})}\bigg(\prod_{k=1}^{g_{t}^{j}}L^{j}_{k}\bigg)Q^{j}_{0}+G_{j}^{a,b}\big(t-\tau_{g_{t}^{j}}^{j}\big)}{Z_{g_{t}^{j}}^{\otimes j}},

\displaystyle B_{t}^{(2)}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle e^{-a(j)(t-\tau_{g_{t}^{j}}^{j})},

and denote
\bigg(B_{t}^{(1)}+\frac{B_{t}^{(2)}}{Z_{g_{t}^{j}}^{\otimes j}}P_{g_{t}^{j}}^{j}\bigg)
by
B^{(3)}_{t}.
We now consider the scaled limit of
\Big(\frac{\log\Phi_{t}^{(a)}}{\sqrt{t}},\frac{\log|I_{t}^{(a,b)}|}{\sqrt{t}}\Big)
as
t\to\infty.

Observe that both
B_{t}^{(1)},B_{t}^{(2)}
are
O_{p}(1),
and for each
1\leq k\leq n,

-Z_{n}^{\otimes j}\leq Q_{k}^{j}\Big(\prod_{i=1}^{k-1}L_{i}^{j}\Big)\leq Z_{n}^{\otimes j}.

This implies that
-nZ_{n}^{\otimes j}\leq P_{n}^{j}\leq nZ_{n}^{\otimes j},
for all
n\geq 1,
which further implies that
\Big|\frac{P_{g_{t}^{j}}^{j}}{Z_{g_{t}^{j}}^{\otimes j}}\Big|\leq g_{t}^{j}
; hence
|B^{(3)}_{t}|=O_{p}(t)
and strictly positive. Hence as
t\to\infty,

\frac{\log|B_{t}^{(3)}|}{\sqrt{t}}=\frac{\log\Big|B_{t}^{(1)}+\frac{B_{t}^{(2)}}{Z_{g_{t}^{j}}^{\otimes j}}P_{g_{t}^{j}}^{j}\Big|}{\sqrt{t}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle P}}{{\to}}0.

Consider the probability,

\displaystyle P\bigg[\Big(\frac{\log\Phi_{t}^{(a)}}{\sqrt{t}},\frac{\log|I_{t}^{(a,b)}|}{\sqrt{t}}\Big)\in\cdot|Y_{0}=i\bigg]

\displaystyle=\sum_{j\in S}P[Y_{t}=j|Y_{0}=i]P\Big[\Big(\frac{\log\Phi_{t}^{(a)}}{\sqrt{t}},\frac{\log|I_{t}^{(a,b)}|}{\sqrt{t}}\Big)\in\cdot|Y_{0}=i,Y_{t}=j\Big].

(15.14)

Let
t\to\infty,
in both sides of (
15.14
), and to get rid of
\{Y_{t}=j\}
in the conditioning part of the RHS in the limit, we apply the Lemma 6.1 with

L_{t}^{(1)}=\bigg(\frac{[\log L^{j}_{0}-a(j)(t-\tau_{g_{t}^{j}}^{j})]}{\sqrt{t}},\frac{\log|B_{t}^{(3)}|}{\sqrt{t}}\bigg)^{\prime},\quad L_{t}^{(2)}=\begin{pmatrix}1&0\\
0&1\end{pmatrix},\quad H_{t}=\begin{pmatrix}\frac{S_{g_{t}^{j}}^{\otimes j}}{\sqrt{t}}\\
\frac{\log Z_{g_{t}^{j}}^{\otimes j}}{\sqrt{t}}\end{pmatrix}.

Since both
\log L^{j}_{0}
and
a(j)(t-\tau_{g_{t}^{j}}^{j})
are
O_{P}(1),
hence
L_{t}^{(1)}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle P}}{{\to}}0,
and by Lemma
15.2
(c)
H_{t}
verifies the condition in (6.1). We will be done if we prove that
H^{\prime}_{t}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{\to}}\frac{\sigma_{j}}{\sqrt{E|\mathfrak{I}^{j}_{1}|}}(F_{1},F_{2})
as
t\to\infty.
This is because using
P\big[Y_{t}=j|Y_{0}=i\big]\to\pi_{j},
LHS of (
15.14
) would lead to

\displaystyle\lim_{t\to\infty}P\bigg[\Big(\frac{\log\Phi_{t}^{(a)}}{\sqrt{t}},\frac{\log|I_{t}^{(a,b)}|}{\sqrt{t}}\Big)\in\cdot|Y_{0}=i\bigg]
