\displaystyle=\sum_{j\in S}\pi_{j}\lim_{t\to\infty}P\bigg[\bigg(\frac{S_{g_{t}^{j}}^{\otimes j}}{\sqrt{t}},\frac{\log Z_{g_{t}^{j}}^{\otimes j}}{\sqrt{t}}\bigg)\in\cdot|Y_{0}=i\bigg]

(15.15)

proving the result. From the definitions of
M_{n}^{\otimes j},Z_{n}^{\otimes j},
it follows that

\displaystyle M_{g_{t}^{j}}^{\otimes j}-\max_{1\leq k\leq g_{t}^{j}}\log|Q_{k}^{j}|\quad\leq\quad\log Z_{g_{t}^{j}}^{\otimes j}\quad\leq\quad M_{g_{t}^{j}}^{\otimes j}+\max_{1\leq k\leq g_{t}^{j}}\log|Q_{k}^{j}|

(15.16)

(see also (4.4) of
[
37
]
). Now under the condition (4.3) Lemma
15.2
(a) holds and Lemma
15.2
(b) below verifies the weak convergence result as
t\to\infty
. Combining these in (
15.15
) the assertion follows by taking
t\to\infty
limit.

Lemma 15.2
.

Under assumptions of Theorem 2(b) as
t\to\infty,

\displaystyle(a)\quad\frac{\max_{1\leq k\leq g_{t}^{j}}\log|Q_{k}^{j}|}{\sqrt{t}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle a.s}}{{\to}}0,\quad(b)\quad\bigg(\frac{S_{g_{t}^{j}}^{\otimes j}}{\sqrt{t}},\frac{M_{g_{t}^{j}}^{\otimes j}}{\sqrt{t}}\bigg)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{\to}}\frac{\sigma_{j}}{\sqrt{E|\mathfrak{I}^{j}_{1}|}}(F_{1},F_{2})

\displaystyle(c)\quad H^{\prime}_{t}=\bigg(\frac{S_{g_{t}^{j}}^{\otimes j}}{\sqrt{t}},\frac{\log Z_{g_{t}^{j}}^{\otimes j}}{\sqrt{t}}\bigg)\quad\text{satisfies the condition in (6.1), i.e }

\displaystyle\quad\quad\quad H_{t}-H_{t-\varepsilon(t)}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle P}}{{\to}}(0,0)^{\prime}\quad\text{for some }\quad\varepsilon(\cdot)\quad\text{specified in (6.1)}.

15.2.1
Proof of
Lemma
15.2
(a) & (b)

Proof.

For ease of notations, write
\mu_{j}:=1/E|\mathfrak{I}^{j}_{1}|
. Since
\frac{g_{t}^{j}}{t}\to\mu_{j}
as
t\to\infty.

Part (a) will be proved if we show
\frac{\max_{1\leq k\leq g_{t}^{j}}\log|Q_{k}^{j}|}{\sqrt{g_{t}^{j}}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle a.s}}{{\to}}0.
As
(\log|Q^{j}_{i}|)_{i\geq 1}
is an i.i.d. sequence and (4.3) implies that
E\big[\log|Q_{1}^{j}|\big]^{2}<\infty.
By the Borel-Cantelli lemma, we will show that

\displaystyle\frac{\max_{1\leq k\leq n}\log|Q^{j}_{k}|}{\sqrt{n}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{a.s.}}}{{\to}}0\quad\text{as }n\to\infty.

(15.17)

We first show
\log|Q^{j}_{n}|/\sqrt{n}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{a.s.}}}{{\to}}0
as a consequence of Borel-Cantelli Lemma and
E\big[\log|Q_{1}^{j}|\big]^{2}<\infty.
This is evident as
E\big[\log|Q_{1}^{j}|\big]^{2}=E\big[\log|Q_{n}^{j}|\big]^{2}<\infty
implies

\displaystyle\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}P\big[\big(\log|Q_{n}^{j}|\big)^{2}>\varepsilon^{2}n\big]<\infty\quad\text{for every }\varepsilon>0,

\displaystyle\quad\Leftrightarrow P\big[\log|Q_{n}^{j}|>\varepsilon\sqrt{n}\text{ i.o.}\big]=0\quad\text{for every }\varepsilon>0.

Since only finitely many of
\{\log|Q_{k}^{j}|/\sqrt{k},1\leq k\leq n\}
can exceed
\varepsilon
for any arbitrarily small
\varepsilon>0,
(
15.17
) holds. Then from this and
g_{t}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{a.s.}}}{{\to}}\infty;
part (a) will follow by observing

\displaystyle\Big\{\frac{\max_{1\leq k\leq g_{t}^{j}}\log|Q_{k}^{j}|}{\sqrt{g_{t}^{j}}}\not\to 0\Big\}=\Big\{\frac{\max_{1\leq k\leq n}\log|Q^{j}_{k}|}{\sqrt{n}}\not\to 0\Big\}\cup\{g_{t}\not\to\infty\},

(15.18)

and using Theorem 2.1 in Gut
[
34
]
by replacing
n
by
g_{t}^{j}
(as
g_{t}^{j}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle a.s}}{{\to}}\infty
) in
(
15.17
).

15.2.2
Proof of part (b)

Since
\bigg(\frac{S_{g_{t}^{j}}^{\otimes j}}{\sqrt{t}},\frac{M_{g_{t}^{j}}^{\otimes j}}{\sqrt{t}}\bigg)=\bigg(\frac{S_{g_{t}^{j}}^{\otimes j}}{\sqrt{g_{t}^{j}}},\frac{M_{g_{t}^{j}}^{\otimes j}}{\sqrt{g_{t}^{j}}}\bigg)\sqrt{\frac{g_{t}^{j}}{t}},
and under Assumption 1,
g_{t}^{j}/t\stackrel{{\scriptstyle a.s}}{{\to}}\mu_{j},
part (b) will follow if we prove following two steps,

•

Step 1 :

\Big(\frac{S_{n}^{\otimes j}}{\sqrt{n}},\frac{M_{n}^{\otimes j}}{\sqrt{n}}\Big)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{\to}}\sigma_{j}(F_{1},F_{2})
as
n\to\infty;

•

Step 2 :

\Big(\frac{S_{n}^{\otimes j}}{\sqrt{n}},\frac{M_{n}^{\otimes j}}{\sqrt{n}}\Big)
satisfy Anscombe’s contiguity condition for
R_{n}
in (2.11);

then by replacing
n
by
g_{t}^{j}
(as
g_{t}^{j}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle a.s}}{{\to}}\infty
) the result will follow.

(A)

(
Proof of Step 1
) Let
W:=(W_{t}:0\leq t\leq 1)
be a Wiener process in
[0,1]
. Let the random curve
x_{(n)}:=(x_{(n)}(t):0\leq t\leq 1)
in
[0,1]
be defined in the following manner

\displaystyle x_{(n)}(t):=n^{-\frac{1}{2}}S_{[nt]}^{\otimes j}+(nt-[nt])\frac{\log L_{[nt]+1}^{j}}{\sqrt{n}},\quad 0\leq t\leq 1,

(15.19)

where it represents the continuous time interpolated version of the discrete partial sum process
\{S_{n}^{\otimes j}:n\in\mathbb{N}\}.
By
(\mathcal{C}[0,1],\mathcal{C})
denote the space of continuous functions in
[0,1]
with topology induced under supremum norm. Under conditions of Theorem 2(b) the weak convergence of
x_{(n)}
in
\mathcal{C}[0,1]
holds (a.k.a the functional central limit theorem (FCLT)),

\displaystyle x_{(n)}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle w}}{{\Longrightarrow}}\sigma_{j}W\quad\text{in}\quad\mathcal{C}[0,1]

(15.20)

as
n\to\infty.
We will use the traditional invariance principle. Note that for any element
x\in\mathcal{C}[0,1],
the functional
f:\mathcal{C}[0,1]\to(\mathcal{C}[0,1]\times\mathcal{C}[0,1])
defined as

f(x):=\Big(\big(x(t),\sup_{0\leq s\leq t}x(s)\big):t\in[0,1]\Big),

is a continuous under supremum norm. Using continuous mapping theorem as
n\to\infty,
one has
f(x_{(n)})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle w}}{{\to}}f(\sigma_{j}W)
in
\mathcal{C}[0,1],
and as a consequence

\displaystyle f(x_{(n)})(1)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{\to}}f(\sigma_{j}W)(1)=\sigma_{j}\big(W_{1},\sup_{0\leq s\leq 1}W_{s}\big)\quad.

(15.21)

Observe that
f(x_{(n)})(1)=n^{-\frac{1}{2}}\big(S^{\otimes j}_{n},M^{\otimes j}_{n}\big).
Hence (
15.21
) implies that

n^{-\frac{1}{2}}\Big(S^{\otimes j}_{n},M^{\otimes j}_{n}\Big)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{\to}}\sigma_{j}\big(W_{1},\sup_{0\leq s\leq 1}W_{s}\big),\quad\text{as}\quad n\to\infty.

Corollary 6.5.5 of
[
52
]
gives an explicit form of density of
\big(W_{1},\sup_{0\leq s\leq 1}W_{s}\big)
that is identical to the joint density of
(F_{1},F_{2})
in (4.5).

(B)

(
Proof of Step 2
)

We prove
Step 2
by showing Anscombe’s condition
(uniform continuity in probability, condition (A) on page 16 in
[
34
]
)
which in our context requires showing the following: Given
\gamma>0,\eta>0
, there exist
\delta>0,n_{0}>0
such that

\displaystyle P\Big[\max_{\{k:|k-n|<n\delta\}}\Big|\Big(\frac{S^{\otimes j}_{k}}{\sqrt{k}},\frac{M^{\otimes j}_{k}}{\sqrt{k}}\Big)-\Big(\frac{S^{\otimes j}_{n}}{\sqrt{n}},\frac{M^{\otimes j}_{n}}{\sqrt{n}}\Big)\Big|>\gamma\Big]<\eta\quad\text{for }n\geq n_{0}.

(15.22)

For any two
a=(a_{1},a_{2}),b=(b_{1},b_{2}),
we use the
L_{1}
norm
|a-b|=|a_{1}-a_{2}|+|b_{1}-b_{2}|
without loss of generality. (
15.22
) holds if we show that given
\gamma>0,\eta>0
, there exist
\delta>0,n_{1},n_{2}>0
such that

\displaystyle P\Big[\max_{\{k:|k-n|<n\delta\}}\Big|\frac{S^{\otimes j}_{k}}{\sqrt{k}}-\frac{S^{\otimes j}_{n}}{\sqrt{n}}\Big|>\frac{\gamma}{2}\Big]<\frac{\eta}{2}\quad\text{for }n\geq n_{1}

(15.23)

\displaystyle P\Big[\max_{\{k:|k-n|<n\delta\}}\Big|\frac{M^{\otimes j}_{k}}{\sqrt{k}}-\frac{M^{\otimes j}_{n}}{\sqrt{n}}\Big|>\frac{\gamma}{2}\Big]<\frac{\eta}{2}\quad\text{for }n\geq n_{2}

(15.24)

by setting
n_{0}=n_{1}\vee n_{2}.

Since
\{k:|k-n|<n\delta\}=\{n\leq k\leq n(1+\delta)\}\cup\{n(1-\delta)\leq k\leq n\}
an upper bound for the probability in (
15.24
) is

\displaystyle P\Big[\max_{\{k:n\leq k\leq n+n\delta\}}\Big|\frac{M^{\otimes j}_{k}}{\sqrt{k}}-\frac{M^{\otimes j}_{n}}{\sqrt{n}}\Big|>\frac{\gamma}{2}\Big]

(15.25)

\displaystyle\quad+P\Big[\max_{\{k:n(1-\delta)\leq k\leq n\}}\Big|\frac{M^{\otimes j}_{k}}{\sqrt{k}}-\frac{M^{\otimes j}_{n}}{\sqrt{n}}\Big|>\frac{\gamma}{2}\Big].

We prove that the first term is smaller than
\eta/4
. Similar arguments show that second term is smaller than
\eta/4
from which (
15.24
) follows. For notational simplicity we assume
n\delta
to be an integer (even if it’s not then abuse the notation
n\delta
to denote
[n\delta]
).
Observe that

\displaystyle P\Big[\max_{\{k:n\leq k\leq n(1+\delta)\}}\bigg|\frac{M^{\otimes j}_{k}}{\sqrt{k}}-\frac{M^{\otimes j}_{n}}{\sqrt{n}}\Big|>\frac{\gamma}{2}\bigg]

\displaystyle\quad\leq P\Big[\max_{\{k:n\leq k\leq n(1+\delta)\}}\Big(\Big|\frac{M^{\otimes j}_{k}}{\sqrt{k}}-\frac{M^{\otimes j}_{k}}{\sqrt{n}}\Big|+\Big|\frac{M^{\otimes j}_{k}}{\sqrt{n}}-\frac{M^{\otimes j}_{n}}{\sqrt{n}}\Big|\Big)>\frac{\gamma}{2}\Big]

\displaystyle\quad\leq P\Big[\frac{|M^{\otimes j}_{n(1+\delta)}|}{\sqrt{n(1+\delta)}}[(1+\delta)^{\frac{1}{2}}-1]>\frac{\gamma}{4}\Big]+P\Big[\frac{M^{\otimes j}_{n(1+\delta)}-M^{\otimes j}_{n}}{\sqrt{n}}>\frac{\gamma}{4}\Big].

(15.26)

For all
\delta\geq 0
,
(1+\delta)^{\frac{1}{2}}\leq(1+\frac{\delta}{2})
. Consequently, for all
\delta\geq 0
,

P\Big[\frac{|M^{\otimes j}_{n(1+\delta)}|}{\sqrt{n(1+\delta)}}[(1+\delta)^{\frac{1}{2}}-1]>\frac{\gamma}{4}\Big]\leq P\Big[\frac{\delta}{2}\frac{|M^{\otimes j}_{n(1+\delta)}|}{\sqrt{n(1+\delta)}}>\frac{\gamma}{4}\Big].

Note that the marginal density of
F_{2}
will be identical as of a
|N|
where
N
is a Normal
(0,1)
distributed random variable and this can be derived from the joint density of
(F_{1},F_{2})
in step 2. This implies that, as
n\to\infty

\displaystyle\frac{M^{\otimes j}_{n}}{\sqrt{n}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{\to}}\sigma_{j}|N|,

(15.27)

which is also celebrated as the Erdos-Kac theorem (see Theorem I in Erdös and Kac
[
26
]
). As a consequence of (
15.27
), for given
\gamma>0,\eta>0
one can choose a small
\delta_{\eta}>0
and a large
n_{2}^{(1)}
such that for all
\delta\leq\delta^{(1)}_{\eta}
,

P\Big[\frac{\delta}{2}\frac{|M^{\otimes j}_{n(1+\delta)}|}{\sqrt{n(1+\delta)}}>\frac{\gamma}{4}\Big]<\frac{\eta}{8},\quad\text{for }n\geq n_{2}^{(1)}.

Second term in (
15.26
) can be shown to be smaller than
\eta/8
as well. This can be shown by first writing, with

A_{1}:=\{(x,y):0\leq y<\infty,-\infty<x<y\}
,

\displaystyle P\Big[M^{\otimes j}_{n(1+\delta)}-M^{\otimes j}_{n}>\frac{\gamma}{4}\sqrt{n}\Big]

\displaystyle\quad=\int_{A_{1}}P\big[M^{\otimes j}_{n(1+\delta)}-M^{\otimes j}_{n}>\frac{\gamma}{4}\sqrt{n}\mid(S^{\otimes j}_{n},M^{\otimes j}_{n})=(s,t)\big]f_{(S^{\otimes j}_{n},M^{\otimes j}_{n})}(s,t)dsdt

\displaystyle\quad=\int_{A_{1}}P\Big[\frac{M^{\otimes j}_{n\delta}}{\sqrt{n\delta}}>\frac{t-s}{\sqrt{n\delta}}+\frac{\gamma\sqrt{n}}{4\sqrt{n\delta}}\Big]f_{(S^{\otimes j}_{n},M^{\otimes j}_{n})}(s,t)dsdt,

(15.28)

where (
15.28
) follows from the independent increment property of the mean
0,
random walk
(S_{n}^{\otimes j})_{n\geq 1}
.
For
(s,t)\in A_{1}
, one has
t-s>0,
and as a consequence

P\Big[\frac{M^{\otimes j}_{n\delta}}{\sqrt{n\delta}}>\frac{t-s}{\sqrt{n\delta}}+\frac{\gamma}{4\sqrt{\delta}}\Big]\leq P\Big[\frac{M^{\otimes j}_{n\delta}}{\sqrt{n\delta}}>\frac{\gamma}{4\sqrt{\delta}}\Big].

By choosing
\delta^{(2)}_{\eta,\gamma}>0
and
\delta<\delta^{(2)}_{\eta,\gamma}
such that
\gamma/(4\sqrt{\delta})
is sufficiently large and choosing
n_{\eta,\gamma}^{(2)}
large,
as a consequence of (
15.27
),

P\Big[\frac{M^{\otimes j}_{n\delta}}{\sqrt{n\delta}}>\frac{\gamma}{4\sqrt{\delta}}\Big]<\frac{\eta}{8},\quad\text{for }n\geq n_{\eta,\gamma}^{(2)}.

Putting this upper bound in (
15.28
), the first probability term in (
15.25
) can be shown to be less than
\eta/4,
which follows by taking
n\geq n_{0}^{(1)}\vee n_{\eta,\gamma}^{(2)}
and
\delta\leq\delta^{(1)}_{\eta}\wedge\delta^{(2)}_{\eta,\gamma}
. Hence (
15.24
) holds by setting
n_{2}=n_{0}^{(1)}\vee n_{\eta,\gamma}^{(2)}
.

To show (
15.23
), we can decompose its upper-bound like (
15.25
) and then show that given
\gamma,\eta>0
there exists
\delta,n_{1}
such that

\displaystyle P\Big[\max_{\{n\leq k\leq n(1+\delta)\}}\Big|\frac{S^{\otimes j}_{k}}{\sqrt{k}}-\frac{S^{\otimes j}_{n}}{\sqrt{n}}\Big|>\frac{\gamma}{2}\Big]<\frac{\eta}{4}\quad\text{for }n\geq n_{1}

(15.29)

The LHS of (
15.29
) can be shown to be less than

\displaystyle P\Big[\frac{|S^{\otimes j}_{n}|}{\sqrt{n(1+\delta)}}\big[(1+\delta)^{\frac{1}{2}}-1\big]>\frac{\gamma}{4}\Big]+P\Big[\frac{\max_{\{n\leq k\leq n(1+\delta)\}}\Big|S^{\otimes j}_{k}-S^{\otimes j}_{n}\Big|}{\sqrt{n}}>\frac{\gamma}{4}\Big]

\displaystyle\quad\quad\leq P\Big[\frac{\delta}{2}\frac{|S^{\otimes j}_{n}|}{\sqrt{n}}>\frac{\gamma}{4}\Big]+P\bigg[\frac{\max_{0\leq k\leq n\delta}|S_{k}^{\otimes j}|}{\sqrt{n}}>\frac{\gamma}{4}\bigg]

\displaystyle\quad\quad=P\Big[\frac{|S^{\otimes j}_{n}|}{\sqrt{n}}>\frac{\gamma}{2\delta}\Big]+P\bigg[\frac{\max_{0\leq k\leq n\delta}|S_{k}^{\otimes j}|}{\sqrt{n}}>\frac{\gamma}{4}\bigg]

(15.30)

Since marginally
\frac{S_{n}^{\otimes j}}{\sqrt{n}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{\to}}\sigma_{j}N
, as
n\to\infty
(follows from step
1
), given
\gamma,\eta>0
we can always find
\delta_{0}>0
small enough and
n_{1}
large enough such that for
n\geq n_{1},
the first term in RHS of (
15.30
) is less than
\eta/8.
Regarding the second term Choose
\delta\leq\frac{\eta\gamma^{2}}{128\sup_{j\in S}\sigma_{j}^{2}},
using Kolmogorov’s maximal inequality it follows that

\displaystyle P\bigg[\frac{\max_{0\leq k\leq n\delta}|S_{k}^{\otimes j}|}{\sqrt{n}}>\frac{\gamma}{4}\bigg]\leq\frac{16\sum_{i=1}^{n\delta}\text{Var}(\log L_{i}^{j})}{(\gamma\sqrt{n})^{2}}\leq\frac{16\delta\sup_{j\in S}\sigma^{2}_{j}}{\gamma^{2}}\leq\frac{\eta}{8}.

(15.31)

Combining both terms (
15.29
) is proved. (
15.23
) and (
15.24
) together show (
15.22
), and the verification of step 2 is complete.

∎

15.2.3
Proof of Lemma
15.2
(c)

Proof.

Part (c) follows if both
S^{\otimes j}_{g^{j}_{t}}/\sqrt{t}
and
M^{\otimes j}_{g^{j}_{t}}/\sqrt{t}
verify (
15.9
) separately as
\log G_{t}^{(1)}
(since
M^{\otimes j}_{g^{j}_{t}}/\sqrt{t}
and
\log Z^{\otimes j}_{g^{j}_{t}}/\sqrt{t}
are equivalent in probability using part (a)).
Take
t\mapsto\varepsilon(t)
to be an increasing function satisfying
\lim_{t\to\infty}\varepsilon(t)=\infty
and
\lim_{t\to\infty}\varepsilon(t)/t=0
.
Observe that

\displaystyle\frac{M^{\otimes j}_{g^{j}_{t}}}{\sqrt{t}}-\frac{M^{\otimes j}_{g^{j}_{t-\varepsilon(t)}}}{\sqrt{t-\varepsilon(t)}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{M^{\otimes j}_{g^{j}_{t}}-M^{\otimes j}_{[\mu_{j}t]}}{\sqrt{t}}

(15.32)

\displaystyle\quad+\frac{M^{\otimes j}_{[\mu_{j}t]}}{\sqrt{t}}-\frac{M^{\otimes j}_{[\mu_{j}(t-\varepsilon(t))]}}{\sqrt{t-\varepsilon(t)}}+\frac{M^{\otimes j}_{[\mu_{j}(t-\varepsilon(t))]}-M^{\otimes j}_{g^{j}_{t-\varepsilon(t)}}}{\sqrt{t-\varepsilon(t)}}.

For
\delta>0,
set
A_{(\delta,j)}:=\{n\in\mathbb{N}:|n/t-\mu_{j}|\leq\delta\}
and observe that

\lim_{t\to\infty}P[g^{j}_{t}\in A_{(\delta,j)}]=1
. For the first term on the right-hand side in (
15.32
),

\displaystyle P\Big[\frac{|M^{\otimes j}_{g^{j}_{t}}-M^{\otimes j}_{[\mu_{j}t]}|}{\sqrt{t}}>\gamma\Big]

\displaystyle\quad\leq P\Big[\frac{|M^{\otimes j}_{g^{j}_{t}}-M^{\otimes j}_{[\mu_{j}t]}|}{\sqrt{t}}>\gamma,g^{j}_{t}\in A_{(\delta,j)}\Big]+P\Big[\frac{|M^{\otimes j}_{g^{j}_{t}}-M^{\otimes j}_{[\mu_{j}t]}|}{\sqrt{t}}>\gamma,g^{j}_{t}\in A^{c}_{(\delta,j)}\Big]

\displaystyle\quad\leq P\Big[\frac{M^{\otimes j}_{[(\mu_{j}+\delta)t]}-M^{\otimes j}_{[\mu_{j}t]}}{\sqrt{t}}>\gamma\Big]+P\Big[\frac{M^{\otimes j}_{[\mu_{j}t]}-M^{\otimes j}_{[(\mu_{j}-\delta)t]}}{\sqrt{t}}>\gamma\Big]+P\Big[g^{j}_{t}\notin A^{c}_{(\delta,j)}\Big].

The arguments used to prove part (b) can be applied to prove that the first two terms on the right-hand side above goes to
0,
by making
\delta
arbitrarily small, and taking
t\to\infty
.
The third term goes to
0
as
t\to\infty
since
g^{j}_{t}/t\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{a.s.}}}{{\to}}\mu_{j}
as
t\to\infty
.
Consequently, the first term on the right-hand side in (
15.32
) goes to
0
in probability as
t\to\infty
.
Similarly, the third term on the right-hand side in (
15.32
) goes to
0
in probability as
t\to\infty
since
t-\varepsilon(t)\to\infty
as
t\to\infty
.
The second term on the right-hand side in (
15.32
) goes to
0
in probability as
t\to\infty
by using an argument similar to (
15.26
) with
\varepsilon(t)/t\to 0
as
t\to\infty
.

Regarding verification for the
S^{\otimes j}_{g^{j}_{t}}/\sqrt{t},
observe that

\displaystyle\frac{S^{\otimes j}_{g^{j}_{t}}}{\sqrt{t}}-\frac{S^{\otimes j}_{g^{j}_{t-\varepsilon(t)}}}{\sqrt{t-\varepsilon(t)}}=\frac{S^{\otimes j}_{g^{j}_{t-\varepsilon(t)}}}{\sqrt{t-\varepsilon(t)}}\bigg[\Big(1-\frac{\varepsilon(t)}{t}\Big)^{\frac{1}{2}}-1\bigg]+\frac{\sum_{i=g^{j}_{t-\varepsilon(t)}+1}^{g^{j}_{t}}\log L_{i}^{j}}{\sqrt{t}}.

(15.33)

Without loss of generality assume that
t
is large enough so that
\varepsilon(t)\leq t,
for some
t\geq t_{0}.
Observe that
(1-\delta_{1})^{\frac{1}{2}}\leq 1-\frac{\delta_{1}}{2}
for all
0\leq\delta_{1}\leq 1.
Hence, the upper bound can be expressed as

\displaystyle\Big|\frac{S^{\otimes j}_{g^{j}_{t}}}{\sqrt{t}}-\frac{S^{\otimes j}_{g^{j}_{t-\varepsilon(t)}}}{\sqrt{t-\varepsilon(t)}}\Big|\leq\frac{\varepsilon(t)}{2t}\frac{|S^{\otimes j}_{g^{j}_{t-\varepsilon(t)}}|}{\sqrt{t-\varepsilon(t)}}+\sqrt{\frac{\varepsilon(t)}{t}}\frac{|\sum_{i=g^{j}_{t-\varepsilon(t)}+1}^{g^{j}_{t}}\log L_{i}^{j}|}{\sqrt{\varepsilon(t)}},

for
t\geq t_{0}
. Since
\frac{\varepsilon(t)}{t}\to 0
as
t\to\infty
and both quantities
\frac{|S^{\otimes j}_{g^{j}_{t-\varepsilon(t)}}|}{\sqrt{t-\varepsilon(t)}},\,\,\frac{|\sum_{i=g^{j}_{t-\varepsilon(t)}+1}^{g^{j}_{t}}\log L_{i}^{j}|}{\sqrt{\varepsilon(t)}}
are
O_{P}(1)
which follows from part (b) as
(t-\varepsilon(t))
is large. Hence by Slutsky’s theorem the LHS of (
15.33
)
\stackrel{{\scriptstyle P}}{{\to}}0
as
t\to\infty,
and this concludes the proof of part (c).
∎

∎

16
Proof of supplementary results of Theorem 3

16.1
Proof of
Lemma 10.1

Proof.

Denote
\widehat{a}(\cdot)=a(\cdot)-E_{\pi}a(\cdot).
Conditioned on
\widetilde{A}_{1}^{j},
observe that

\displaystyle\int_{\tau_{0}^{j}}^{\tau_{1}^{j}}\widehat{a}(Y_{s})ds=\sum_{i\in A_{1}^{j}}\widehat{a}(J_{i})T_{i+1}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{=}}\sum_{(i_{k},i_{k+1})\in\widetilde{A}_{1}^{j}}\widehat{a}(i_{k})\widetilde{F}_{i_{k}i_{k+1}}.

Since for
(i_{k},i_{k+1})\in\widetilde{A}_{1}^{j}\cap\mathbb{S}_{\alpha}^{-},
the quantity
\widehat{a}(i_{k})<0,
hence conditioned on
\widetilde{A}_{1}^{j},
we have

\displaystyle\int_{\tau_{0}^{j}}^{\tau_{1}^{j}}\widehat{a}(Y_{s})ds

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{=}}

\displaystyle\sum_{(i_{k},i_{k+1})\in\widetilde{A}_{1}^{j}}\widehat{a}(i_{k})\widetilde{F}_{i_{k}i_{k+1}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{(i_{k},i_{k+1})\in\widetilde{A}_{1}^{j}\cap\mathbb{S}_{\alpha}^{+}}\widehat{a}(i_{k})\widetilde{F}_{i_{k}i_{k+1}}-\sum_{(i_{k},i_{k+1})\in\widetilde{A}_{1}^{j}\cap\mathbb{S}_{\alpha}^{-}}|\widehat{a}(i_{k})|\widetilde{F}_{i_{k}i_{k+1}}

\displaystyle\quad\quad+\sum_{(i_{k},i_{k+1})\in\widetilde{A}_{1}^{j}\cap\mathbb{S}^{{}^{c}}_{\alpha}}\widehat{a}(i_{k})\widetilde{F}_{i_{k}i_{k+1}}.

For any event
D

\displaystyle\quad P\Big[\int_{\tau_{0}^{j}}^{\tau_{1}^{j}}\widehat{a}(Y_{s})ds\in D\Big]=EP\Big[\sum_{(i_{k},i_{k+1})\in\widetilde{A}_{1}^{j}}\widehat{a}(i_{k})\widetilde{F}_{i_{k}i_{k+1}}\in D\mid\widetilde{A}_{1}^{j}\Big]

\displaystyle\quad=EP\bigg[\Big(\sum_{(i_{k},i_{k+1})\in\widetilde{A}_{1}^{j}\cap S_{\alpha}^{+}}\widehat{a}(i_{k})\widetilde{F}_{i_{k}i_{k+1}}+\sum_{(j_{k},j_{k+1})\in\widetilde{A}_{1}^{j}\cap S^{{}^{c,+}}_{\alpha}}\widehat{a}(j_{k})\widetilde{F}_{j_{k}j_{k+1}}\Big)

\displaystyle\quad\quad-\Big(\sum_{(i_{k},i_{k+1})\in\widetilde{A}_{1}^{j}\cap S_{\alpha}^{-}}|\widehat{a}(i_{k})|\widetilde{F}_{i_{k}i_{k+1}}+\sum_{(j_{k},j_{k+1})\in\widetilde{A}_{1}^{j}\cap S^{{}^{c,-}}_{\alpha}}|\widehat{a}(j_{k})|\widetilde{F}_{j_{k}j_{k+1}}\Big)\in D|\widetilde{A}_{1}^{j}\bigg]

(16.1)

\displaystyle\quad=:EP\Big[\widetilde{X}^{(1)}_{1}-\widetilde{X}^{(2)}_{1}\in D|\widetilde{A}_{1}^{j}\Big]

where

\displaystyle\widetilde{X}^{(1)}_{1}

\displaystyle:=

\displaystyle\sum_{(i_{k},i_{k+1})\in\widetilde{A}_{1}^{j}\cap S_{\alpha}^{+}}\widehat{a}(i_{k})\widetilde{F}_{i_{k}i_{k+1}}+\sum_{(j_{k},j_{k+1})\in\widetilde{A}_{1}^{j}\cap S^{{}^{c,+}}_{\alpha}}\widehat{a}(j_{k})\widetilde{F}_{j_{k}j_{k+1}},

\displaystyle\widetilde{X}^{(2)}_{1}

\displaystyle:=

\displaystyle\sum_{(i_{k},i_{k+1})\in\widetilde{A}_{1}^{j}\cap S_{\alpha}^{-}}|\widehat{a}(i_{k})|\widetilde{F}_{i_{k}i_{k+1}}+\sum_{(j_{k},j_{k+1})\in\widetilde{A}_{1}^{j}\cap S^{{}^{c,-}}_{\alpha}}|\widehat{a}(j_{k})|\widetilde{F}_{j_{k}j_{k+1}}.

We use the following Lemma
16.1
few times in this proof.

Lemma 16.1
.

For any arbitrary class of non-negative independent regularly varying random variables
\{\widetilde{X}_{i}:i=1,\ldots,n\}
with index
\alpha\geq 0,
one has the following asymptotic results as
x\to\infty

(i)

P[\widetilde{X}_{1}-\widetilde{X}_{2}>x]\sim P[\widetilde{X}_{1}>x],\quad
and
\quad P[\widetilde{X}_{1}-\widetilde{X}_{2}<-x]\sim P[\widetilde{X}_{2}>x].

(ii)

P[\sum_{i=1}^{n}\widetilde{X}_{i}>x]\sim\sum_{i=1}^{n}P[\widetilde{X}_{i}>x].

(iii)

If
P[\widetilde{X}_{2}>x]\sim o(P[\widetilde{X}_{1}>x]),
then
P[\widetilde{X}_{1}+\widetilde{X}_{2}>x]\sim P[\widetilde{X}_{1}>x].

Suppose both sets
\mathbb{S}_{\alpha}^{+},\mathbb{S}_{\alpha}^{-}
are non-empty. Choose
\widetilde{A}_{1}^{j}
such that
\widetilde{A}_{1}^{j}\cap\mathbb{S}_{\alpha}^{+}\neq\emptyset
and
\widetilde{A}_{1}^{j}\cap\mathbb{S}_{\alpha}^{-}\neq\emptyset
(since if one of them doesn’t hold then later we will see that the corresponding one of (10.1) or (10.2) will vacuously hold true in limit, as both sides will be
0
).

Conditioned on
\widetilde{A}^{j}_{1}
observe that
\{\widetilde{F}_{i_{k}i_{k+1}}:(i_{k},i_{k+1})\in\widetilde{A}_{1}^{j}\cap\mathbb{S}_{\alpha}^{+}\}
are all independent and regularly varying at
+\infty
with rate
x^{-\alpha}L(x).
As a result of Lemma
16.1
(ii) the following holds

\displaystyle P\Big[\sum_{(i_{k},i_{k+1})\in\widetilde{A}_{1}^{j}\cap\mathbb{S}_{\alpha}^{+}}\widehat{a}(i_{k})\widetilde{F}_{i_{k}i_{k+1}}>x|\widetilde{A}^{j}_{1}\Big]

\displaystyle\sim

\displaystyle\sum_{(i_{k},i_{k+1})\in\widetilde{A}_{1}^{j}\cap\mathbb{S}_{\alpha}^{+}}P[\widehat{a}(i_{k})\widetilde{F}_{i_{k}i_{k+1}}>x]

(16.2)

\displaystyle\sim

\displaystyle\bigg(\sum_{(i_{k},i_{k+1})\in\widetilde{A}_{1}^{j}\cap S_{\alpha}^{+}}c_{i_{k},i_{k+1}}\widehat{a}^{\alpha}(i_{k})\bigg)x^{-\alpha}L(x).

For any
(j_{k},j_{k+1})\in\widetilde{A}^{j}_{1}\cap\mathbb{S}^{{}^{c,+}}_{\alpha},
the quantity
P[\widehat{a}(j_{k})\widetilde{F}_{j_{k}j_{k+1}}>x|\widetilde{A}^{j}_{1}]=o(x^{-\alpha}L(x)),
and as a result it follows that

P[\widehat{a}(j_{k})\widetilde{F}_{j_{k}j_{k+1}}>x|\widetilde{A}^{j}_{1}]=o\Big(P\Big[\sum_{(i_{k},i_{k+1})\in\widetilde{A}_{1}^{j}\cap\mathbb{S}_{\alpha}^{+}}\widehat{a}(i_{k})\widetilde{F}_{i_{k}i_{k+1}}>x|\widetilde{A}^{j}_{1}\Big]\Big),

since for any
(j_{k},j_{k+1})\in\mathbb{S}^{{}^{c,+}}_{\alpha}
the sojourn time will have tails lighter than any sojourn time corresponding to the element
(i_{k},i_{k+1})\in\mathbb{S}^{+}_{\alpha}.
Hence as a consequence of Lemma
16.1
(iii) we have as
x\to\infty

\displaystyle P\big[\widetilde{X}^{(1)}_{1}>x|\widetilde{A}^{j}_{1}\big]

\displaystyle\sim

\displaystyle P\Big[\sum_{(i_{k},i_{k+1})\in\widetilde{A}_{1}^{j}\cap\mathbb{S}_{\alpha}^{+}}\widehat{a}(i_{k})\widetilde{F}_{i_{k}i_{k+1}}>x|\widetilde{A}^{j}_{1}\Big],

\displaystyle\quad\text{\& similarly}\quad\quad P\big[\widetilde{X}^{(2)}_{1}>x|\widetilde{A}^{j}_{1}\big]

\displaystyle\sim

\displaystyle P\Big[\sum_{(i_{k},i_{k+1})\in\widetilde{A}_{1}^{j}\cap\mathbb{S}_{\alpha}^{-}}|\widehat{a}(i_{k})|\widetilde{F}_{i_{k}i_{k+1}}>x|\widetilde{A}^{j}_{1}\Big].

(16.3)

Clearly conditioned on
\widetilde{A}_{1}^{j}
both
\widetilde{X}^{(1)}_{1},\widetilde{X}^{(2)}_{1}
are independent and regularly varying at
+\infty
with rate
x^{-\alpha}L(x).
By virtue of Lemma
16.1
(i) it follows that almost surely

\displaystyle P\big[\widetilde{X}^{(1)}_{1}-\widetilde{X}^{(2)}_{1}>x|\widetilde{A}^{j}_{1}\big]

\displaystyle\sim

\displaystyle P\big[\widetilde{X}^{(1)}_{1}>x|\widetilde{A}^{j}_{1}\big]

(16.4)

\displaystyle\sim

\displaystyle P\Big[\sum_{(i_{k},i_{k+1})\in\widetilde{A}_{1}^{j}\cap\mathbb{S}_{\alpha}^{+}}\widehat{a}(i_{k})\widetilde{F}_{i_{k}i_{k+1}}>x|\widetilde{A}^{j}_{1}\Big]

\displaystyle\sim

\displaystyle\bigg(\sum_{(i_{k},i_{k+1})\in\widetilde{A}_{1}^{j}\cap\mathbb{S}_{\alpha}^{+}}c_{i_{k},i_{k+1}}\widehat{a}^{\alpha}(i_{k})\bigg)x^{-\alpha}L(x).

We will need an upper bound of
\frac{P\big[\widetilde{X}^{(1)}_{1}-\widetilde{X}^{(2)}_{1}>x|\widetilde{A}^{j}_{1}\big]}{x^{-\alpha}L(x)}
with finite expectation for large
x,
in order to use the Dominated Convergence Theorem. Observe that

\displaystyle P\big[\widetilde{X}^{(1)}_{1}-\widetilde{X}^{(2)}_{1}>x|\widetilde{A}^{j}_{1}\big]\leq P\big[\widetilde{X}^{(1)}_{1}>x|\widetilde{A}^{j}_{1}\big]

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle P\Big[\sum_{(i_{k},i_{k+1})\in\widetilde{A}_{1}^{j}\cap S_{\alpha}^{+}}\widehat{a}(i_{k})\widetilde{F}_{i_{k}i_{k+1}}>\frac{x}{2}|\widetilde{A}^{j}_{1}\Big]+P\Big[\sum_{(j_{k},j_{k+1})\in\widetilde{A}_{1}^{j}\cap S^{{}^{c,+}}_{\alpha}}\widehat{a}(j_{k})\widetilde{F}_{j_{k}j_{k+1}}>\frac{x}{2}|\widetilde{A}^{j}_{1}\Big].

Working with the first-term yields

\displaystyle P\Big[\sum_{(i_{k},i_{k+1})\in\widetilde{A}_{1}^{j}\cap\mathbb{S}_{\alpha}^{+}}\widehat{a}(i_{k})\widetilde{F}_{i_{k}i_{k+1}}>x|\widetilde{A}^{j}_{1}\Big]

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\sum_{(i_{k},i_{k+1})\in\widetilde{A}_{1}^{j}\cap\mathbb{S}_{\alpha}^{+}}P\Big[\widehat{a}(i_{k})\widetilde{F}_{i_{k}i_{k+1}}>\frac{x}{|\widetilde{A}_{1}^{j}\cap S_{\alpha}^{+}|}|\widetilde{A}^{j}_{1}\Big],

and after scaling by
x^{-\alpha}L(x)
we get

\displaystyle\frac{P\Big[\sum_{(i_{k},i_{k+1})\in\widetilde{A}_{1}^{j}\cap\mathbb{S}_{\alpha}^{+}}\widehat{a}(i_{k})\widetilde{F}_{i_{k}i_{k+1}}>x|\widetilde{A}^{j}_{1}\Big]}{x^{-\alpha}L(x)}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\sum_{(i_{k},i_{k+1})\in\widetilde{A}_{1}^{j}\cap\mathbb{S}_{\alpha}^{+}}\frac{P\Big[\widehat{a}(i_{k})\widetilde{F}_{i_{k}i_{k+1}}>\frac{x}{|\widetilde{A}_{1}^{j}\cap\mathbb{S}_{\alpha}^{+}|}|\widetilde{A}^{j}_{1}\Big]}{x^{-\alpha}L(x)}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(a)}}{{\leq}}

\displaystyle\widetilde{c}_{\epsilon}\sum_{(i_{k},i_{k+1})\in\widetilde{A}_{1}^{j}\cap\mathbb{S}_{\alpha}^{+}}c_{i_{k}i_{k+1}}\Big(1\vee\widehat{a}(i_{k})|\widetilde{A}_{1}^{j}\cap\mathbb{S}_{\alpha}^{+}|\Big)^{\alpha+\epsilon}

where (a) holds due to (4.6) and the expectation of the final upper bound in the above RHS is finite due to (4.7).

The second term after scaled by
p(x):=x^{-\alpha}L(x)
will give the upper bound for any
x\geq 2a^{*}|\widetilde{A}_{1}^{j}|x^{*},\&\,\,\epsilon_{1}\in(0,\alpha)

\displaystyle\frac{P\Big[\sum_{(j_{k},j_{k+1})\in\widetilde{A}_{1}^{j}\cap\mathbb{S}^{{}^{c,+}}_{\alpha}}\widehat{a}(j_{k})\widetilde{F}_{j_{k}j_{k+1}}>\frac{x}{2}|\widetilde{A}^{j}_{1}\Big]}{x^{-\alpha}L(x)}

\displaystyle\quad\leq\sum_{(j_{k},j_{k+1})\in\widetilde{A}_{1}^{j}\cap\mathbb{S}^{{}^{c,+}}_{\alpha}}\frac{P\Big[\widehat{a}(j_{k})\widetilde{F}_{j_{k}j_{k+1}}>\frac{x}{2|\widetilde{A}_{1}^{j}\cap\mathbb{S}^{{}^{c,+}}_{\alpha}|}|\widetilde{A}^{j}_{1}\Big]}{x^{-\alpha}L(x)}

\displaystyle\quad=\sum_{(j_{k},j_{k+1})\in\widetilde{A}_{1}^{j}\cap\mathbb{S}^{{}^{c,+}}_{\alpha}}\frac{\overline{F}_{j_{k}j_{k+1}}\Big(\frac{x}{2\widehat{a}(j_{k})|\widetilde{A}_{1}^{j}\cap\mathbb{S}^{{}^{c,+}}_{\alpha}|}\Big)}{p\Big(\frac{x}{2\widehat{a}(j_{k})|\widetilde{A}_{1}^{j}\cap\mathbb{S}^{{}^{c,+}}_{\alpha}|}\Big)}\frac{p\Big(\frac{x}{2\widehat{a}(j_{k})|\widetilde{A}_{1}^{j}\cap\mathbb{S}^{{}^{c,+}}_{\alpha}|}\Big)}{p(x)}

\displaystyle\quad\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(a)}}{{\leq}}\sum_{(j_{k},j_{k+1})\in\widetilde{A}_{1}^{j}\cap\mathbb{S}^{{}^{c,+}}_{\alpha}}\frac{p\Big(\frac{x}{2\widehat{a}(j_{k})|\widetilde{A}_{1}^{j}\cap\mathbb{S}^{{}^{c,+}}_{\alpha}|}\Big)}{p(x)}

\displaystyle\quad\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(b)}}{{\leq}}\sum_{(j_{k},j_{k+1})\in\widetilde{A}_{1}^{j}\cap\mathbb{S}^{{}^{c,+}}_{\alpha}}(1+\epsilon_{1})\Big(2\widehat{a}(j_{k})|\widetilde{A}_{1}^{j}\cap\mathbb{S}^{{}^{c,+}}_{\alpha}|\Big)^{\alpha-\epsilon_{1}}

\displaystyle\quad\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(c)}}{{\leq}}(1+\epsilon_{1})(4a^{*})^{\alpha-\epsilon_{1}}\sum_{(j_{k},j_{k+1})\in\widetilde{A}_{1}^{j}\cap\mathbb{S}^{{}^{c,+}}_{\alpha}}|\widetilde{A}_{1}^{j}\cap\mathbb{S}^{{}^{c,+}}_{\alpha}|^{\alpha}

\displaystyle\quad\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(d)}}{{=}}(1+\epsilon_{1})(4a^{*})^{\alpha-\epsilon_{1}}|\widetilde{A}_{1}^{j}\cap\mathbb{S}^{c}_{\alpha}|^{1+\alpha}

where (a) follows by using the first condition of (4.8) with
x\geq 2a^{*}|\widetilde{A}_{1}^{j}|x^{*},
(b) follows by using the Potter’s bound using any arbitrary
\epsilon_{1}\in(0,\alpha).
The inequality at (c) holds by taking supremum of
\widehat{a}(\cdot)
over the summand, and taking it outside of the sum and having the upper bound by ignoring
\epsilon_{1};
and finally, the result follows by observing that the final upper bound in the RHS of (d) has finite expectation due to the third condition of (4.8).

Hence from (
16.4
) the assertion holds for the right tail (10.1) as a consequence of Dominated Convergence Theorem, i.e, as
x\to\infty,

\displaystyle\frac{P\big[\widetilde{X}^{(1)}_{1}-\widetilde{X}^{(2)}_{1}>x\big]}{x^{-\alpha}L(x)}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{EP\big[\widetilde{X}^{(1)}_{1}-\widetilde{X}^{(2)}_{1}>x|\widetilde{A}^{j}_{1}\big]}{x^{-\alpha}L(x)}

\displaystyle\to

\displaystyle E\bigg[\sum_{(i_{k},i_{k+1})\in\widetilde{A}_{1}^{j}\cap\mathbb{S}_{\alpha}^{+}}c_{i_{k},i_{k+1}}\widehat{a}^{\alpha}(i_{k})\bigg]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\widetilde{\alpha}_{j}^{(+)}=\frac{1+\beta_{j}}{2}\sigma^{\alpha}_{(j,\alpha)}.

Similarly (10.2) holds working with the
\{\widetilde{X}^{(1)}_{1}-\widetilde{X}^{(2)}_{1}<-x\}
type of events.

If
\widetilde{A}_{1}^{j}\cap\mathbb{S}_{\alpha}^{+}
is empty then (10.1) holds vacuously true as both sides are
0
in limit, and the same holds for (10.2) corresponding to the case
\widetilde{A}_{1}^{j}\cap\mathbb{S}_{\alpha}^{-}=\emptyset
as well.

Hence the assertion is proved for the case when both
\mathbb{S}_{\alpha}^{+},\mathbb{S}_{\alpha}^{-}
non-empty. If one of
\mathbb{S}_{\alpha}^{+},\mathbb{S}_{\alpha}^{-}
is empty, then the RHS of one of (10.1) and (10.2) will be trivially
0,
that can be easily verified after scaling the probability in the corresponding LHS by
x^{-\alpha}L(x)
(using similar arguments as above).
∎

16.1.1
Proof of
Lemma 10.2

Proof.

Observe that

\widetilde{G}^{*(1)}_{t}=\frac{\sum_{i=1}^{g_{t}^{j}}Y_{i}^{**}}{t^{\frac{1}{\alpha}}L_{0}(t)}+\frac{\big[\tau_{0}^{j}+(t-\tau_{g_{t}^{j}}^{j})\big]E_{\pi}a(\cdot)}{t^{\frac{1}{\alpha}}L_{0}(t)},

where
Y_{i}^{**}=-\int_{\tau_{i-1}^{j}}^{\tau_{i}^{j}}\Big(a(Y_{s})-E_{\pi}a(\cdot)\Big)ds.
Since the quantity
\tau_{0}^{j}+(t-\tau_{g_{t}^{j}}^{j})
is
O_{P}(1),
the second term of
\widetilde{G}^{*(1)}_{t}
is
o_{P}(1).
Denoting
t^{\frac{1}{\alpha}}L_{0}(t),(g_{t}^{j})^{1/\alpha}L_{0}(g_{t}^{j})
by
c_{t},c_{g_{t}^{j}}
respectively, it is enough to show that as
t\to\infty,

\displaystyle\frac{\sum_{i=1}^{g_{t}^{j}}Y_{i}^{**}}{c_{t}}-\frac{\sum_{i=1}^{g_{t-\varepsilon(t)}^{j}}Y_{i}^{**}}{c_{t-\varepsilon(t)}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle P}}{{\to}}0

(16.5)

for some increasing
t\to\varepsilon(t)
, such that
\varepsilon(t)\to\infty
and
\frac{\varepsilon(t)}{t}\to 0
as
t\to\infty.

Observe that

\displaystyle\frac{\sum_{i=1}^{g_{t}^{j}}Y_{i}^{**}}{c_{t}}-\frac{\sum_{i=1}^{g_{t-\varepsilon(t)}^{j}}Y_{i}^{**}}{c_{t-\varepsilon(t)}}

\displaystyle\quad=\frac{\sum_{i=g_{t-\varepsilon(t)}^{j}+1}^{g_{t}^{j}}Y_{i}^{**}}{c_{t}}+\sum_{i=1}^{g_{t-\varepsilon(t)}^{j}}Y^{**}_{i}\Big[\frac{1}{c_{t}}-\frac{1}{c_{t-\varepsilon(t)}}\Big]

\displaystyle\quad=\frac{c_{\varepsilon(t)}}{c_{t}}\frac{c_{g_{t}^{j}-g_{t-\varepsilon(t)}^{j}}}{c_{\varepsilon(t)}}\frac{\sum_{i=g_{t-\varepsilon(t)}^{j}+1}^{g_{t}^{j}}Y_{i}^{**}}{c_{g_{t}^{j}-g_{t-\varepsilon(t)}^{j}}}+\frac{c_{g_{t-\varepsilon(t)}^{j}}}{c_{t-\varepsilon(t)}}\frac{\sum_{i=1}^{g_{t-\varepsilon(t)}^{j}}Y^{**}_{i}}{c_{g_{t-\varepsilon(t)}^{j}}}\Big[\frac{c_{t-\varepsilon(t)}}{c_{t}}-1\Big].

(16.6)

Using ideas of Lemma 12.3(a) it follows that both
\frac{c_{g_{t}^{j}-g_{t-\varepsilon(t)}^{j}}}{c_{\varepsilon(t)}}
and
\frac{c_{g_{t-\varepsilon(t)}^{j}}}{c_{t-\varepsilon(t)}}
converges almost surely to
(E|\mathfrak{I}^{j}_{1}|)^{-1/\alpha},
as
t\to\infty.
Since
\{Y_{i}^{**}:i\geq 1\}
are a sequence of regularly varying random variables with mean
0,
and hence a version of stable central limit theorem can be obtained in line of
c^{-1}_{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}Y_{i}^{**}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{\to}}\sigma_{(j,\alpha)}\mathcal{S}_{\alpha}(1,\beta_{j},0)
for
n\to\infty.
Hence by Theorem 3.2 of
[
34
]
, using
g_{t-\varepsilon(t)}^{j}
and
g_{t}^{j}-g_{t-\varepsilon(t)}^{j}
as the stopped random times it follows that as
t\to\infty

\displaystyle\frac{\sum_{i=1}^{g_{t-\varepsilon(t)}^{j}}Y^{**}_{i}}{c_{g_{t-\varepsilon(t)}^{j}}}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{\to}}

\displaystyle\sigma_{(j,\alpha)}\mathcal{S}_{\alpha}(1,\beta_{j},0),

\displaystyle\frac{\sum_{i=g_{t-\varepsilon(t)}^{j}+1}^{g_{t}^{j}}Y_{i}^{**}}{c_{g_{t}^{j}-g_{t-\varepsilon(t)}^{j}}}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{=}}

\displaystyle\frac{\sum_{i=1}^{g_{t}^{j}-g_{t-\varepsilon(t)}^{j}}Y_{i}^{**}}{c_{g_{t}^{j}-g_{t-\varepsilon(t)}^{j}}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{\to}}\sigma_{(j,\alpha)}\mathcal{S}_{\alpha}(1,\beta_{j},0).

Hence by Slutsky’s theorem our assertion will follow in (
16.6
) if we prove that both
\frac{c_{\varepsilon(t)}}{c_{t}},\Big[\frac{c_{t-\varepsilon(t)}}{c_{t}}-1\Big]
go to
0
as
t\to\infty.

Observe that
\frac{c_{\varepsilon(t)}}{c_{t}}=\big(\frac{\varepsilon(t)}{t}\big)^{1/\alpha}\frac{L_{0}(\varepsilon(t))}{L_{0}(t)},
and using Lemma12.3(b) (Potter’s bound) it follows that for any
0<\epsilon<\frac{1}{\alpha},
there exists
t_{0,\epsilon}
such that for all
t\geq t_{0,\epsilon},

\frac{1}{1+\epsilon}\Big(\frac{\varepsilon(t)}{t}\Big)^{\frac{1}{\alpha}+\epsilon}\leq\frac{c_{\varepsilon(t)}}{c_{t}}\leq\frac{1}{1-\epsilon}\Big(\frac{\varepsilon(t)}{t}\Big)^{\frac{1}{\alpha}-\epsilon},

and similarly using
\frac{c_{t-\varepsilon(t)}}{c_{t}}=\big(1-\frac{\varepsilon(t)}{t}\big)^{\frac{1}{\alpha}}\frac{L_{0}(t-\varepsilon(t))}{L_{0}(t)}
one can get the following

(1-\epsilon)\big(1-\frac{\varepsilon(t)}{t}\big)^{\frac{1}{\alpha}+\epsilon}-1\leq\frac{c_{t-\varepsilon(t)}}{c_{t}}-1\leq(1+\epsilon)\big(1-\frac{\varepsilon(t)}{t}\big)^{\frac{1}{\alpha}-\epsilon}-1,

for any
0<\epsilon<\frac{1}{\alpha},
and
t\geq t_{0,\epsilon}.

Now taking
t\to\infty,\frac{\varepsilon(t)}{t}\to 0,
and taking
\epsilon
arbitrarily small

\displaystyle\Big(\frac{c_{\varepsilon(t)}}{c_{t}},\Big[\frac{c_{t-\varepsilon(t)}}{c_{t}}-1\Big]\Big)\to(0,0)

(16.7)

is proved as
t\to\infty,
showing the assertion.
∎

16.2
Proof of
Lemma 10.3 (a) & (b)

Proof.

The proof follows similar ideas (and notations) used in proving Lemma
15.2
with some differences as this is a case when variance of the increments (that is
\text{Var}(\log L_{i}^{j})
) does not exist.

Part (a) will follow if we show that
\frac{\max_{1\leq k\leq n}\log|Q_{k}^{j}|}{c_{n}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle a.s}}{{\to}}0,
as
n\to\infty.
Condition (4.14) suggests that
E\big[(\log|Q_{1}^{j}|)^{\alpha+\epsilon}\big]=E\big[(\log|Q_{n}^{j}|)^{\alpha+\epsilon}\big]<\infty,
it follows that

\displaystyle\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}P[(\log|Q_{n}^{j}|)^{\alpha+\epsilon}>n\epsilon_{1}^{\alpha+\epsilon}]<\infty,\quad\forall\epsilon_{1}>0,

\displaystyle\Longleftrightarrow P\big[\log|Q_{n}^{j}|>\epsilon_{1}n^{\frac{1}{\alpha+\epsilon}}\,\,\,\text{infinitely often}\big]=0,\quad\forall\epsilon_{1}>0.

(16.8)

Hence for the set
\big\{\frac{\log|Q_{k}^{j}|}{k^{\frac{1}{\alpha+\epsilon}}},1\leq k\leq\infty\big\}
only finitely many exceed
\epsilon_{1}>0,
for any arbitrarily small
\epsilon_{1}>0.
Hence we have
\frac{\max_{1\leq k\leq n}\log|Q_{k}^{j}|}{n^{\frac{1}{\alpha+\epsilon}}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle a.s}}{{\to}}0
as
n\to\infty
. Since
\epsilon>0,
and

\frac{\max_{1\leq k\leq n}\log|Q_{k}^{j}|}{c_{n}}=\frac{\max_{1\leq k\leq n}\log|Q_{k}^{j}|}{n^{\frac{1}{\alpha+\epsilon}}}\frac{1}{n^{\frac{1}{\alpha}-\frac{1}{\alpha+\epsilon}}L_{0}(n)},

using
n^{\frac{1}{\alpha}-\frac{1}{\alpha+\epsilon}}L_{0}(n)=n^{\Big[(\frac{1}{\alpha}-\frac{1}{\alpha+\epsilon})+\frac{\log L_{0}(n)}{\log n}\Big]},
and Lemma 12.3(c) suggests that
\frac{\log L_{0}(n)}{\log n}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle P}}{{\to}}0.
Hence
\frac{\max_{1\leq k\leq n}\log|Q_{k}^{j}|}{c_{n}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle a.s}}{{\to}}0,
as
n\to\infty.
Using argument similar to (
15.18
) the assertion of part (a) follows.

16.2.1
Proof of part (b)

Since
\bigg(\frac{S_{g_{t}^{j}}^{\otimes j}}{c_{t}},\frac{M_{g_{t}^{j}}^{\otimes j}}{c_{t}}\bigg)=\bigg(\frac{S_{g_{t}^{j}}^{\otimes j}}{c_{g_{t}^{j}}},\frac{M_{g_{t}^{j}}^{\otimes j}}{c_{g_{t}^{j}}}\bigg)\frac{c_{g_{t}^{j}}}{c_{t}},
and under Assumption 1 and Lemma 12.3(a),
c_{g_{t}^{j}}/c_{t}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle a.s}}{{\to}}\Big(\frac{1}{E|\mathfrak{I}^{j}_{1}|}\Big)^{\frac{1}{\alpha}},
part (b) will follow if we prove the following two steps,

•

Step 1 :

\Big(\frac{S_{n}^{\otimes j}}{c_{n}},\frac{M_{n}^{\otimes j}}{c_{n}}\Big)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{\to}}\sigma_{(j,\alpha)}\big(\mathcal{\bf{S}}_{\alpha,\beta_{j}}(1),\sup_{0\leq u\leq 1}\mathcal{\bf{S}}_{\alpha,\beta_{j}}(u)\big)
as
n\to\infty;

•

Step 2 :

\Big(\frac{S_{n}^{\otimes j}}{c_{n}},\frac{M_{n}^{\otimes j}}{c_{n}}\Big)
satisfies the Anscombe’s contiguity condition for
R_{n}
in (2.11);

then by replacing
n
by
g_{t}^{j}
(as
g_{t}^{j}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle a.s}}{{\to}}\infty
) the result will follow.

(A)

(
Proof of Step 1
) Let
\mathcal{\bf{S}}_{\alpha,\beta}
denote the
\alpha
-stable Levy process
\mathcal{\bf{S}}_{\alpha,\beta}:=\big(\mathcal{S}_{\alpha,\beta}(t):t\geq 0\big)
in
[0,\infty)
. Recall that
S_{n}^{\otimes j}:=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\log L_{i}^{j},\quad M_{n}^{\otimes j}:=\max_{1\leq k\leq n}\Big\{\sum_{i=1}^{k}\log L_{i}^{j}\Big\}

where under condition of Theorem 3(b),
E\log L_{i}^{j}=0
and
\{\log L_{i}^{j}:i\geq 1\}
is a sequence of regularly varying random variables with index
\alpha>0
along with (10.1) and (10.2). Let the random curve
y_{(n)}:=(y_{(n)}(t):t\geq 0)
be defined in the following manner

\displaystyle y_{(n)}(t):=c_{n}^{-1}S_{[nt]}^{\otimes j},\quad t\geq 0,

(16.9)

where it represents a stepwise functions having jumps at integers in
\{nt:t\geq 0\},
which is also the continuous time analogue of the discrete partial sum process
\{S_{n}^{\otimes j}:n\in\mathbb{N}\}.
We express a FCLT motivated by Stable CLT in Theorem 4.5.3 in
[
62
]
. Under conditions of Theorem
3
(b) one has

\displaystyle\Big(c_{n}^{-1}S_{[nt]}^{\otimes j}:t\geq 0\Big)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle w}}{{\Longrightarrow}}\sigma_{(j,\alpha)}\mathcal{\bf{S}}_{\alpha,\beta_{j}}\quad\text{in}\quad(D,J_{1})

(16.10)

as
n\to\infty,
where
(D,J_{1})
denotes the Skorohod space under
J_{1}
topology. Note that for any element
y\in D,
define the functional
f:D\to(D\times D)
such that

f(y):=\Big(\big(y(t),\sup_{0\leq s\leq t}y(s)\big):t\geq 0\Big),

and it can be shown that,
f
is a continuous functional that is continuous almost surely at every continuity point of the Levy process
\mathcal{\bf{S}}_{\alpha,\beta_{j}}
. When
E\log L_{1}^{j}=0,
using continuous mapping theorem one has as
n\to\infty,

f(y_{(n)})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle w}}{{\to}}f(\mathcal{\bf{S}}_{\alpha,\beta_{j}})
in
(D\times D,J_{1})
, and as a consequence one has

\displaystyle f(y_{(n)})(1)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{\to}}f(\mathcal{\bf{S}}_{\alpha,\beta_{j}})(1)=\sigma_{(j,\alpha)}\big(\mathcal{\bf{S}}_{\alpha,\beta_{j}}(1),\sup_{0\leq u\leq 1}\mathcal{\bf{S}}_{\alpha,\beta_{j}}(u)\big)\quad.

(16.11)

Observe that
f(y_{(n)})(1)=c_{n}^{-1}\big(S^{\otimes j}_{n},M^{\otimes j}_{n}\big).
Hence (
16.11
) implies that

c_{n}^{-1}\Big(S^{\otimes j}_{n},M^{\otimes j}_{n}\Big)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{\to}}\sigma_{(j,\alpha)}\big(\mathcal{\bf{S}}_{\alpha,\beta_{j}}(1),\sup_{0\leq u\leq 1}\mathcal{\bf{S}}_{\alpha,\beta_{j}}(u)\big),\quad\text{as}\quad n\to\infty,

which proves the assertion.

(B)

(
Proof of Step 2
) We proceed similar to Step
2
of (
15.2
). In order to show – “Given
\gamma>0,\eta>0
, there exist
\delta>0,n_{0}>0
such that

\displaystyle P\Big[\max_{\{k:|k-n|<n\delta\}}\Big|\Big(\frac{S^{\otimes j}_{k}}{c_{k}},\frac{M^{\otimes j}_{k}}{c_{k}}\Big)-\Big(\frac{S^{\otimes j}_{n}}{c_{n}},\frac{M^{\otimes j}_{n}}{c_{n}}\Big)\Big|>\gamma\Big]<\eta\quad\text{for }n\geq n_{0}."

(16.12)

it is enough if we show that given
\gamma>0,\eta>0
, there exist
\delta>0,n_{1},n_{2}>0
such that

\displaystyle P\Big[\max_{\{k:|k-n|<n\delta\}}\Big|\frac{S^{\otimes j}_{k}}{c_{k}}-\frac{S^{\otimes j}_{n}}{c_{n}}\Big|>\frac{\gamma}{2}\Big]<\frac{\eta}{2}\quad\text{for }n\geq n_{1}

(16.13)

\displaystyle P\Big[\max_{\{k:|k-n|<n\delta\}}\Big|\frac{M^{\otimes j}_{k}}{c_{k}}-\frac{M^{\otimes j}_{n}}{c_{n}}\Big|>\frac{\gamma}{2}\Big]<\frac{\eta}{2}\quad\text{for }n\geq n_{2}

(16.14)

by setting
n_{0}=n_{1}\vee n_{2}.

To prove (
16.14
) it is enough to show that there exists
\eta,\gamma>0,
and large
n_{2}
such that

\displaystyle a_{n}:=P\Big[\max_{k:n\leq k\leq n(1+\delta)}\Big|\frac{M^{\otimes j}_{k}}{c_{k}}-\frac{M^{\otimes j}_{n}}{c_{n}}\Big|>\frac{\gamma}{2}\Big]<\frac{\eta}{4}\quad\text{for }n\geq n_{2},

(16.15)

then using similar argument the other quantity
P\big[\max_{\{k:n(1-\delta)\leq k\leq n\}}\big|\cdot\big|>\frac{\gamma}{2}\big]
can be shown to be less than
\frac{\eta}{4}
proving (
16.14
). It is important to note that
c_{n}
is increasing in
n\geq 0.
Observe that

\displaystyle a_{n}

\displaystyle=P\Big[\max_{k:n\leq k\leq n(1+\delta)}\Big|\frac{M^{\otimes j}_{k}}{c_{k}}-\frac{M^{\otimes j}_{n}}{c_{n}}\Big|>\frac{\gamma}{2}\Big]

\displaystyle\leq P\Big[\max_{k:n\leq k\leq n(1+\delta)}\bigg(\Big|\frac{M^{\otimes j}_{k}}{c_{k}}-\frac{M^{\otimes j}_{k}}{c_{n}}\Big|+\Big|\frac{M^{\otimes j}_{k}}{c_{n}}-\frac{M^{\otimes j}_{n}}{c_{n}}\Big|\bigg)>\frac{\gamma}{2}\Big]

\displaystyle\leq P\Big[\max_{k:n\leq k\leq n(1+\delta)}\Big|\frac{M^{\otimes j}_{k}}{c_{k}}-\frac{M^{\otimes j}_{k}}{c_{n}}\Big|>\frac{\gamma}{4}\Big]+P\Big[\frac{M^{\otimes j}_{n(1+\delta)}-M^{\otimes j}_{n}}{c_{n}}>\frac{\gamma}{4}\Big]

\displaystyle\leq P\Big[\frac{|M^{\otimes j}_{n(1+\delta)}|}{c_{n(1+\delta)}}\Big(\frac{c_{n(1+\delta)}}{c_{n}}-1\Big)>\frac{\gamma}{4}\Big]+P\Big[\frac{M^{\otimes j}_{n(1+\delta)}-M^{\otimes j}_{n}}{c_{n}}>\frac{\gamma}{4}\Big],

(16.16)

and denote the two probabilities in (
16.16
) respectively by
a_{n}^{(1)}
and
a_{n}^{(2)}
. Using the Potter’s bound, for any
\epsilon_{1}\in(0,1-\frac{1}{\alpha}),
there exists
n_{\epsilon_{1}}
such that for all
n\geq n_{\epsilon_{1}},

(1-\epsilon_{1})(1+\delta)^{-\epsilon_{1}}\leq\frac{L_{0}(n(1+\delta))}{L_{0}(n)}\leq(1+\epsilon_{1})(1+\delta)^{\epsilon_{1}},
and as a consequence

\displaystyle\frac{c_{n(1+\delta)}}{c_{n}}=(1+\delta)^{\frac{1}{\alpha}}\frac{L_{0}(n(1+\delta))}{L_{0}(n)}\leq(1+\epsilon_{1})(1+\delta)^{\frac{1}{\alpha}+\epsilon_{1}}.

(16.17)

Hence the first term
a_{n}^{(1)}\leq P\Big[\frac{|M^{\otimes j}_{n(1+\delta)}|}{c_{n(1+\delta)}}\Big((1+\epsilon_{1})(1+\delta)^{\frac{1}{\alpha}+\epsilon_{1}}-1\Big)>\frac{\gamma}{4}\Big].
Since
\frac{1}{2}<\frac{1}{\alpha}<1,
and the chosen
\epsilon_{1}\in(0,1-\frac{1}{\alpha}),
hence
\epsilon_{1}+\frac{1}{\alpha}<1.
As a consequence of that for any
\delta>0
, we have
(1+\delta)^{\frac{1}{\alpha}+\epsilon_{1}}\leq 1+\frac{\delta}{\alpha}+\delta\epsilon_{1}
and

\displaystyle(1+\epsilon_{1})(1+\delta)^{\frac{1}{\alpha}+\epsilon_{1}}\leq(1+\epsilon_{1})\Big(1+\frac{\delta}{\alpha}+\delta\epsilon_{1}\Big)=:1+g(\epsilon_{1},\delta),

(16.18)

where
g(\epsilon_{1},\delta):=\frac{\delta}{\alpha}+(1+\delta)\epsilon_{1}+\epsilon_{1}\big(\frac{\delta}{\alpha}+\delta\epsilon_{1}\big).
Observe that
g(\epsilon_{1},\delta)
can be made arbitrarily small if we take both
\epsilon_{1},\delta
small towards
0.
It follows that for the first term

a_{n}^{(1)}\leq P\Big[\frac{|M^{\otimes j}_{n(1+\delta)}|}{c_{n(1+\delta)}}>\frac{\gamma}{4g(\epsilon_{1},\delta)}\Big].

Now given
\gamma>0,\eta>0,
we can choose
\epsilon_{1},\delta
arbitrarily small, such that
\frac{\gamma}{4g(\epsilon_{1},\delta)}
will be large. Since
\frac{M^{\otimes j}_{n}}{c_{n}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{\to}}\sigma_{(j,\alpha)}\sup_{0\leq u\leq 1}\mathcal{\bf{S}}_{\alpha,\beta_{j}}(u)
as
n\to\infty,
there exists
n(\epsilon_{1},\delta)>0
such that for all
n\geq n(\epsilon_{1},\delta)\vee n_{\epsilon_{1}}

\displaystyle a_{n}^{(1)}\leq P\Big[\frac{|M^{\otimes j}_{n(1+\delta)}|}{c_{n(1+\delta)}}>\frac{\gamma}{4g(\epsilon_{1},\delta)}\Big]\leq\frac{\eta}{8}.

(16.19)

For the second term
a_{n}^{(2)},
with the notation
A_{1}:=\{(x,y):0\leq y<\infty,-\infty<x<y\}
, it follows that

\displaystyle a_{n}^{(2)}

\displaystyle\quad=P\Big[M^{\otimes j}_{n(1+\delta)}-M^{\otimes j}_{n}>\frac{\gamma}{4}c_{n}\Big]

\displaystyle\quad=\int_{A_{1}}P\big[M^{\otimes j}_{n(1+\delta)}-M^{\otimes j}_{n}>\frac{\gamma}{4}c_{n}\mid(S^{\otimes j}_{n},M^{\otimes j}_{n})=(s,t)\big]f_{(S^{\otimes j}_{n},M^{\otimes j}_{n})}(s,t)dsdt

\displaystyle\quad\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(a)}}{{=}}\int_{A_{1}}P\Big[\frac{M^{\otimes j}_{n\delta}}{c_{n\delta}}>\frac{t-s}{c_{n\delta}}+\frac{\gamma c_{n}}{4c_{n\delta}}\Big]f_{(S^{\otimes j}_{n},M^{\otimes j}_{n})}(s,t)dsdt

\displaystyle\quad\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(b)}}{{\leq}}P\Big[\frac{M^{\otimes j}_{n\delta}}{c_{n\delta}}>\frac{\gamma c_{n}}{4c_{n\delta}}\Big]

(16.20)

where
(a)
follows from the independent increment property of the mean
0,
random walk
(S_{n}^{\otimes j})_{n\geq 1},
and the inequality
(b)
follows by observing that for any
(s,t)\in A_{1}
, one has
t-s>0.

Using Potter’s bound again for any
\epsilon_{2}>0,
there exists
n_{\epsilon_{2}}>0,
such that for all
n\geq n_{\epsilon_{2}},
one has

\frac{c_{n}}{c_{n\delta}}=\Big(\frac{1}{\delta}\Big)^{\frac{1}{\alpha}}\frac{L_{0}(n)}{L_{0}(n\delta)}\geq(1-\epsilon_{2})\Big(\frac{1}{\delta}\Big)^{\frac{1}{\alpha}-\epsilon_{2}},

which implies that
a_{n}^{(2)}\leq P\big[\frac{M^{\otimes j}_{n\delta}}{c_{n\delta}}>(1-\epsilon_{2})\frac{\gamma}{4}\big(\frac{1}{\delta}\big)^{\frac{1}{\alpha}-\epsilon_{2}}\big]
for all
n\geq n_{\epsilon_{2}}.

Select
\epsilon_{2}\in\big(0,\frac{1}{\alpha}\big)
and fix any
\eta,\gamma>0.
There exists
\delta^{(2)}_{\eta,\gamma}>0,
small enough so that for
\delta<\delta^{(2)}_{\eta,\gamma},
the quantity
\frac{\gamma}{4}\big(\frac{1}{\delta}\big)^{\frac{1}{\alpha}-\epsilon_{2}}
is sufficiently large and choosing
n^{(2)}_{\eta,\gamma,\epsilon_{2}}
large, so that for all
n\geq n^{(2)}_{\eta,\gamma,\epsilon_{2}},
we will have

\displaystyle a_{n}^{(2)}\leq P\Big[\frac{M^{\otimes j}_{n\delta}}{c_{n\delta}}>(1-\epsilon_{2})\frac{\gamma}{4}\Big(\frac{1}{\delta}\Big)^{\frac{1}{\alpha}-\epsilon_{2}}\Big]<\frac{\eta}{8}.

(16.21)

Hence putting all bounds together, combining (
16.19
),(
16.21
) it follows that
a_{n}\leq\frac{\eta}{4},
for all
n\geq n_{2}:=\big(n(\epsilon_{1},\delta)\vee n_{\epsilon_{1}}\big)\vee\big(n_{\epsilon_{2}}\vee n^{(2)}_{\eta,\gamma,\epsilon_{2}}\big)
proving (
16.15
).

In order to show (
16.13
) it is enough to show that there exists
\eta,\gamma>0
and some large
n_{1}
such that

\displaystyle b_{n}:=P\Big[\max_{k:n\leq k\leq n(1+\delta)}\Big|\frac{S^{\otimes j}_{k}}{c_{k}}-\frac{S^{\otimes j}_{n}}{c_{n}}\Big|>\frac{\gamma}{2}\Big]<\frac{\eta}{4}\quad\text{for }n\geq n_{1}.

(16.22)

Using the following inequalities,

\displaystyle b_{n}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle P\Big[\max_{k:n\leq k\leq n(1+\delta)}\Big(\Big|\frac{S^{\otimes j}_{n}}{c_{k}}-\frac{S^{\otimes j}_{n}}{c_{n}}\Big|+\Big|\frac{S^{\otimes j}_{k}}{c_{k}}-\frac{S^{\otimes j}_{n}}{c_{k}}\Big|\Big)>\frac{\gamma}{2}\Big]

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle P\Big[\max_{k:n\leq k\leq n(1+\delta)}\frac{|S^{\otimes j}_{n}|}{c_{n}}\Big|\frac{c_{n}}{c_{k}}-1\Big|>\frac{\gamma}{4}\Big]+P\Big[\max_{k:n\leq k\leq n(1+\delta)}\frac{|S^{\otimes j}_{k}-S^{\otimes j}_{n}|}{c_{n}}>\frac{\gamma}{4}\Big]

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle P\Big[\frac{|S^{\otimes j}_{n}|}{c_{n}}\Big|\frac{c_{n}}{c_{n(1+\delta)}}-1\Big|>\frac{\gamma}{4}\Big]+P\Big[\frac{\max_{\{k:0\leq k\leq n\delta\}}|S^{\otimes j}_{k}|}{c_{n}}>\frac{\gamma}{4}\Big]

and denote the above two terms in the RHS by
b_{n}^{(1)},b_{n}^{(2)}
respectively. Using (
16.17
) and (
16.18
) the first term
b_{n}^{(1)}
can be upper bounded by
P\big[\frac{|S^{\otimes j}_{n}|}{c_{n}}>\frac{\gamma}{4g(\epsilon_{1},\delta)}\big]
for any
\epsilon_{1}\in(0,1-\frac{1}{\alpha}).
Since
\frac{S_{n}^{\otimes j}}{c_{n}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{\to}}\sigma_{(j,\alpha)}\mathcal{\bf{S}}_{\alpha,\beta_{j}}(1),
for a fixed
\gamma,\eta>0
we can always find a small
\epsilon^{*}_{1}
and
\delta^{*},
such that for
(\epsilon_{1},\delta)\in(0,\epsilon^{*}_{1})\times(0,\delta^{*})
, the quantity
\frac{\gamma}{4g(\epsilon_{1},\delta)}
is large and there exists
n_{2}(\epsilon_{1},\delta)>0
such that for all
n\geq n_{2}(\epsilon_{1},\delta)\vee n_{\epsilon_{1}}

\displaystyle b_{n}^{(1)}\leq P\Big[\frac{|S^{\otimes j}_{n}|}{c_{n}}>\frac{\gamma}{4g(\epsilon_{1},\delta)}\Big]\leq\frac{\eta}{8}.

(16.23)

For bounding the second term
b_{n}^{(2)},
we cannot use the same
Kolmogorov-Maximal-inequality
based technique since the random walk
(S_{n}^{\otimes j})_{n\geq 1}
has regularly varying increment with
1<\alpha<2
, for non-existence of the variance of its increments. Instead we use
Montgomery-Smith inequality
(see Theorem 3.2 (Page 263 of
[
12
]
) also) that we state here.

Theorem 7
.

(Montgomery-Smith’s inequality)
If
\{\widetilde{X}_{i}:i\in\mathbb{N}\}
are independent and identically distributed random variables, then for
1\leq k\leq n<\infty
and all
t>0
we have

P\Big(\max_{1\leq k\leq n}\|\sum_{i=1}^{k}\widetilde{X}_{i}\|>t\Big)\leq 9P\Big(\|\sum_{i=1}^{n}\widetilde{X}_{i}\|>t/30\Big).

Since
\{\log L_{i}^{j}:i\geq 1\}
are regularly varying with
1<\alpha<2,
using Jensen’s inequality there exists
\alpha^{\prime}\in(1,\alpha)
such that
\sup_{j\in S}E|\log L_{i}^{j}|^{\alpha^{\prime}}<\infty
and for any
\delta

\Big(\frac{|S_{n\delta}^{\otimes j}|}{n\delta}\Big)^{\alpha^{\prime}}\leq\frac{\sum_{i=1}^{n\delta}|\log L_{i}^{j}|^{\alpha^{\prime}}}{n\delta}\quad\Longrightarrow\quad E\Big[|S_{n\delta}^{\otimes j}|^{\alpha^{\prime}}\Big]\leq(n\delta)^{\alpha^{\prime}}E|\log L_{i}^{j}|^{\alpha^{\prime}}.

It follows that

\displaystyle P\bigg[\frac{\max_{0\leq k\leq n\delta}|S_{k}^{\otimes j}|}{c_{n}}>\frac{\gamma}{4}\bigg]

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(a)}}{{\leq}}

\displaystyle 9P\bigg[|S_{n\delta}^{\otimes j}|>\frac{\gamma c_{n}}{120}\bigg]

(16.24)

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(b)}}{{\leq}}

\displaystyle 9\times 120^{\alpha^{\prime}}\frac{E\Big[|S_{n\delta}^{\otimes j}|^{\alpha^{\prime}}\Big]}{\gamma^{\alpha^{\prime}}c^{\alpha^{\prime}}_{n}}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(c)}}{{\leq}}

\displaystyle 9\times 120^{\alpha^{\prime}}\Big(\frac{n\delta}{\gamma c_{n}}\Big)^{\alpha^{\prime}}E|\log L_{i}^{j}|^{\alpha^{\prime}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 9\times 120^{\alpha^{\prime}}\Big(\frac{n^{1-\frac{1}{\alpha}}\delta}{\gamma L_{0}(n)}\Big)^{\alpha^{\prime}}E|\log L_{i}^{j}|^{\alpha^{\prime}},

where
(a),(b),(c)
follow respectively by using Montgomery-Smith’s inequality, Chebyshev and Jensen’s inequality. Now given
\gamma,\eta
we can choose
\delta_{0}
such that

\delta_{0}=\frac{\gamma}{120}\Big[\frac{\eta}{36\sup_{j\in S}E|\log L_{i}^{j}|^{\alpha^{\prime}}}\Big]^{1/\alpha^{\prime}}\frac{L_{0}(n)}{n^{1-\frac{1}{\alpha^{\prime}}}}
, and then for any
\delta<\min\{\delta_{0},\delta^{*}\},

P\bigg[\frac{\max_{0\leq k\leq n\delta}|S_{k}^{\otimes j}|}{c_{n}}>\frac{\gamma}{4}\bigg]\leq 9\times 120^{\alpha^{\prime}}\Big(\frac{n^{1-\frac{1}{\alpha}}\delta}{\gamma L_{0}(n)}\Big)^{\alpha^{\prime}}E|\log L_{i}^{j}|^{\alpha^{\prime}}\leq\frac{\eta}{8}.

Putting all bounds together, combining the lower limit for
\delta,
it follows that
b_{n}\leq\frac{\eta}{4},
for all
n\geq n_{1}:=n_{2}(\epsilon_{1},\delta)\vee n_{\epsilon_{1}}
proving (
16.22
).

∎

16.3
Proof of
Lemma 10.3(c)

Proof.

This follows if both
S^{\otimes j}_{g^{j}_{t}}/c_{t}
and
M^{\otimes j}_{g^{j}_{t}}/c_{t}
verify (6.1) separately as
H_{t}
(since
M^{\otimes j}_{g^{j}_{t}}/c_{t}
and
\log Z^{\otimes j}_{g^{j}_{t}}/c_{t}
are equivalent in probability using part (a)). For
\delta>0,
use the same notation
A_{(\delta,j)}:=\{n\in\mathbb{N}:|n/t-\mu_{j}|\leq\delta\}
as in Lemma
15.2
(c) and observe that
\lim_{t\to\infty}P[g^{j}_{t}\in A_{(\delta,j)}]=1.
Use the equality

\displaystyle\frac{M^{\otimes j}_{g^{j}_{t}}}{c_{t}}-\frac{M^{\otimes j}_{g^{j}_{t-\varepsilon(t)}}}{c_{t-\varepsilon(t)}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{M^{\otimes j}_{g^{j}_{t}}-M^{\otimes j}_{[\mu_{j}t]}}{c_{t}}

(16.25)

\displaystyle\quad+\frac{M^{\otimes j}_{[\mu_{j}t]}}{c_{t}}-\frac{M^{\otimes j}_{[\mu_{j}(t-\varepsilon(t))]}}{c_{t-\varepsilon(t)}}+\frac{M^{\otimes j}_{[\mu_{j}(t-\varepsilon(t))]}-M^{\otimes j}_{g^{j}_{t-\varepsilon(t)}}}{c_{t-\varepsilon(t)}}.

For the first term in the right-hand side of (
16.25
),

\displaystyle P\Big[\frac{|M^{\otimes j}_{g^{j}_{t}}-M^{\otimes j}_{[\mu_{j}t]}|}{c_{t}}>\gamma\Big]

\displaystyle\quad\leq P\Big[\frac{|M^{\otimes j}_{g^{j}_{t}}-M^{\otimes j}_{[\mu_{j}t]}|}{c_{t}}>\gamma,g^{j}_{t}\in A_{(\delta,j)}\Big]+P\Big[\frac{|M^{\otimes j}_{g^{j}_{t}}-M^{\otimes j}_{[\mu_{j}t]}|}{c_{t}}>\gamma,g^{j}_{t}\in A^{c}_{(\delta,j)}\Big]

\displaystyle\quad\leq P\Big[\frac{M^{\otimes j}_{[(\mu_{j}+\delta)t]}-M^{\otimes j}_{[\mu_{j}t]}}{c_{t}}>\gamma\Big]+P\Big[\frac{M^{\otimes j}_{[\mu_{j}t]}-M^{\otimes j}_{[(\mu_{j}-\delta)t]}}{c_{t}}>\gamma\Big]+P\Big[g^{j}_{t}\notin A^{c}_{(\delta,j)}\Big].

The arguments used to prove part (b) can be applied to prove that the first two terms on the right-hand side above goes to
0,
by making
\delta
arbitrarily small, and taking
t\to\infty
.
The third term goes to
0
as
t\to\infty
since
g^{j}_{t}/t\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{a.s.}}}{{\to}}\mu_{j}
as
t\to\infty
.
Consequently, the first term on the right-hand side in (
16.25
) goes to
0
in probability as
t\to\infty
.
Similarly, the third term on the right-hand side in (
16.25
) goes to
0
in probability as
t\to\infty
since
t-\varepsilon(t)\to\infty
as
t\to\infty
.

Regarding verification for the
S^{\otimes j}_{g^{j}_{t}}/c_{t},
observe that

\displaystyle\frac{S^{\otimes j}_{g^{j}_{t}}}{c_{t}}-\frac{S^{\otimes j}_{g^{j}_{t-\varepsilon(t)}}}{c_{t-\varepsilon(t)}}=\frac{S^{\otimes j}_{g^{j}_{t-\varepsilon(t)}}}{c_{t-\varepsilon(t)}}\Big[\frac{c_{t-\varepsilon(t)}}{c_{t}}-1\Big]+\frac{\sum_{i=g^{j}_{t-\varepsilon(t)}+1}^{g^{j}_{t}}\log L_{i}^{j}}{c_{t}}.

(16.26)

The upper bound can be expressed as

\displaystyle\Big|\frac{S^{\otimes j}_{g^{j}_{t}}}{c_{t}}-\frac{S^{\otimes j}_{g^{j}_{t-\varepsilon(t)}}}{c_{t-\varepsilon(t)}}\Big|\leq\Big|\frac{S^{\otimes j}_{g^{j}_{t-\varepsilon(t)}}}{c_{t-\varepsilon(t)}}\Big|\Big|\frac{c_{t-\varepsilon(t)}}{c_{t}}-1\Big|+\frac{c_{\varepsilon(t)}}{c_{t}}\frac{\sum_{i=g^{j}_{t-\varepsilon(t)}+1}^{g^{j}_{t}}\log L_{i}^{j}}{c_{\varepsilon(t)}},

for
t\geq t_{0}
. Since
\frac{\varepsilon(t)}{t}\to 0
as
t\to\infty
and both quantities
\frac{|S^{\otimes j}_{g^{j}_{t-\varepsilon(t)}}|}{c_{t-\varepsilon(t)}},\,\,\frac{|\sum_{i=g^{j}_{t-\varepsilon(t)}+1}^{g^{j}_{t}}\log L_{i}^{j}|}{c_{\varepsilon(t)}}
are
O_{P}(1)
which follows from part (b) as
(t-\varepsilon(t)),\varepsilon(t)
are large. As a consequence of (
16.7
)
\Big(\frac{c_{\varepsilon(t)}}{c_{t}},\Big[\frac{c_{t-\varepsilon(t)}}{c_{t}}-1\Big]\Big)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\to}}(0,0),
and by using Slutsky’s theorem the LHS of (
16.26
)
\stackrel{{\scriptstyle P}}{{\to}}0
as
t\to\infty,
and this concludes the proof of part (c).

∎

16.4
Proof of results in
Remark 4.2

Proof.

Under Assumption 1(a) the
S
-valued Markov chain
J
is irreducible, aperiodic, and positive recurrent. Hence using that for any
f:S\times S\to\mathbb{R},
for any subset
A\subset S\times S,
using Proposition 2.12.2 of
[
52
]
we have

\displaystyle\quad\,\frac{E_{j}\big[\sum_{(i_{1},i_{2})\in A}\sum_{n=0}^{|\widetilde{A}_{1}^{j}|-1}1_{\{J_{n}=i_{1},J_{n+1}=i_{2}\}}f(J_{n},J_{n+1})\big]}{E_{j}|\widetilde{A}_{1}^{j}|}=\sum_{(i_{1},i_{2})\in A}\mu_{i_{1}}P_{i_{1}i_{2}}f(i_{1}.i_{2}),

(16.27)

as under stationarity
P_{\mu}\big[J_{n}=i_{1},J_{n+1}=i_{2}\big]=\mu_{i_{1}}P_{i_{1}i_{2}}.
The right tail-index expression can be simplified under Assumptions 1 and 4. It follows from Lemma 10.1 that

\displaystyle\widetilde{\alpha}_{j}^{(+)}

\displaystyle:=

\displaystyle E\Big[\sum_{(i_{l},i_{l+1})\in\widetilde{A}_{1}^{j}\cap\mathbb{S}^{+}_{\alpha}}c_{i_{l}i_{l+1}}\big[a(i_{l})-E_{\pi}a(\cdot)\big]^{\alpha}\Big]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle E|\widetilde{A}_{1}^{j}|\frac{E\Big[\sum_{(i_{l},i_{l+1})\in\widetilde{A}_{1}^{j}\cap\mathbb{S}^{+}_{\alpha}}c_{i_{l}i_{l+1}}\widehat{a}^{\alpha}(i_{l})\Big]}{E|\widetilde{A}_{1}^{j}|}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(a)}}{{=}}

\displaystyle E|A_{1}^{j}|\frac{E\sum_{(i_{l},i_{l+1})\in\mathbb{S}^{+}_{\alpha}}\Big[\sum_{n=0}^{|\widetilde{A}_{1}^{j}|-1}1_{\{J_{n}=i_{1},J_{n+1}=i_{2}\}}c_{J_{n}J_{n+1}}\widehat{a}^{\alpha}(J_{n})\Big]}{E|\widetilde{A}_{1}^{j}|}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle E|A_{1}^{j}|\sum_{(i_{l},i_{l+1})\in\mathbb{S}^{+}_{\alpha}}\mu_{i_{1}}P_{i_{1}i_{2}}c_{i_{1}i_{2}}\widehat{a}^{\alpha}(i_{1})

where (a) follows by observing
|A_{1}^{j}|=|\widetilde{A}_{1}^{j}|
and the last equality follows by using (
16.27
). The left tail-index expression and
\sigma_{(j,\alpha)}
can be derived similarly.

Second part of the remark holds as

\displaystyle E\Big[\sum_{(i_{l},i_{l+1})\in\widetilde{A}_{1}^{j}\cap\mathbb{S}_{\alpha}}\widetilde{c}_{\epsilon}(|a(i_{l})||\widetilde{A}_{1}^{j}\cap\mathbb{S}_{\alpha}|)^{\alpha+\epsilon}\Big]

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\widetilde{c}_{\epsilon}\sup_{x\in S}|a(x)|E[|\widetilde{A}_{1}^{j}\cap\mathbb{S}_{\alpha}|^{1+\alpha+\epsilon}]

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\widetilde{c}_{\epsilon}\sup_{x\in S}|a(x)|E[|\widetilde{A}_{1}^{j}|^{1+\alpha+\epsilon}]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\widetilde{c}_{\epsilon}\sup_{x\in S}|a(x)|E[|A_{1}^{j}|^{1+\alpha+\epsilon}]<\infty.

∎

16.5
Proof of
Proposition 4.4

Proof.

Note that
\sigma_{j}^{2}={\rm Var}\Big(\int_{\tau_{0}^{j}}^{\tau_{1}^{j}}\big(a(Y_{s})-E_{\pi}a(\cdot)\big)ds\Big),
and

\sigma_{j}^{2}=0\Longleftrightarrow P\Big[\int_{\tau_{0}^{j}}^{\tau_{1}^{j}}\big(a(Y_{s})-E_{\pi}a(\cdot)\big)ds=c\Big]=1

for some constant
c
. Taking expectation of
\int_{\tau_{0}^{j}}^{\tau_{1}^{j}}\big(a(Y_{s})-E_{\pi}a(\cdot)\big)ds
observe that
c=0,
which implies that

\displaystyle P\bigg[\frac{\int_{\tau_{0}^{j}}^{\tau_{1}^{j}}a(Y_{s})ds}{\tau_{1}^{j}-\tau_{0}^{j}}=E_{\pi}a(\cdot)\bigg]=1,

(16.28)

where recall that
E_{\pi}a(\cdot)=\frac{E\big[\int_{\tau_{0}^{j}}^{\tau_{1}^{j}}a(Y_{s})ds\big]}{E[\tau_{1}^{j}-\tau_{0}^{j}]}.
Denote the set
\Big\{\omega\in\Omega:\frac{\int_{\tau_{0}^{j}}^{\tau_{1}^{j}}a(Y_{s})ds}{\tau_{1}^{j}-\tau_{0}^{j}}=E_{\pi}a(\cdot)\Big\}
by
A.

For each
i\in S,
and for each
\omega\in A
with
P[A]=1,
define

\tilde{T}_{i}(\omega)=\text{total time spent at state}\,\,\,\,i\,\,\,\text{in}\,\,\,[\tau_{0}^{j},\tau_{1}^{j}).

Note that
\int_{\tau_{0}^{j}}^{\tau_{1}^{j}}a(Y_{s})ds=\sum_{i\in S}a(i)\tilde{T}_{i}(\omega)
by definition and for each
i\in S
define
\widehat{a}(i):=a(i)-E_{\pi}a(\cdot)
, and for each
\omega\in A,

\tilde{T}_{i}(\omega)>0
with probability
1
(since under Assumption 1 sojourn time laws do not have atom at
0
). (
16.28
) can be rewritten as

\displaystyle P\Big[\frac{\sum_{i\in S}a(i)\tilde{T}_{i}}{\tau_{1}^{j}-\tau_{0}^{j}}=E_{\pi}a(\cdot)\Big]=1\quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad\forall\,\,\omega\in A,\,\,\,\sum_{i\in S}\widetilde{a}_{i}\tilde{T}_{i}(\omega)=0.

(16.29)

Since there are uncountably infinitely many
\omega\in A,
and
S
can be only at most countably finite. Then one can always choose a finite collection
B=\{\omega_{j}:j\in S\},
such that the matrix
\Big\{\tilde{T}_{i}(\omega_{j}):i\in S,\omega_{j}\in B\Big\}
will be almost surely non-singular. (
16.29
) implies that

\text{for all}\quad\omega_{j}\in B,\quad\sum_{i\in S}\widehat{a}(i)\tilde{T}_{i}(\omega_{j})=0.

Hence the only solution for
\{\widehat{a}(i):i\in S\}
holds iff
\widehat{a}(i)=0\,\,\forall i\in S,
proving the assertion.
∎

Remark 16.1
.

We conjecture that Proposition 4.4 holds even if
S
is countably infinite. This can be shown if we can prove that one can always choose a countably infinite collection
B=\{\omega_{j}:j\in S\},
such that the matrix
\Big\{\tilde{T}_{i}(\omega_{j}):i\in S,\omega_{j}\in B\Big\}
will be almost surely non-singular.

16.6
Proof of
Theorem 4

Proof.

For part (a) with
a<0,
we use following notations

\displaystyle(L^{j}_{k},Q^{j}_{k})

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{:=}}

\displaystyle\Big(e^{-a(\tau_{k}^{j}-\tau_{k-1}^{j})},\int_{\tau_{k-1}^{j}}^{\tau_{k}^{j}}b(Y_{s})e^{-a(\tau_{k}^{j}-s)}ds\Big),\quad k\geq 1,

\displaystyle(L^{j}_{0},Q^{j}_{0})

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{:=}}

\displaystyle\Big(e^{-a\tau_{0}^{j}},\int_{0}^{\tau_{0}^{j}}b(Y_{s})e^{-a(\tau_{0}^{j}-s)}ds\Big),

Recalling (8.4), denote the following

S^{**}_{n}:=P_{n}\big(\frac{1}{L_{1}^{j}},\frac{Q_{1}^{j}}{L_{1}^{j}}\big)=\sum_{k=1}^{n}\Big(\prod_{l=1}^{k-1}\frac{1}{L_{l}^{j}}\Big)\frac{Q_{k}^{j}}{L_{k}^{j}},\,\,n\geq 1.

We begin with for any function
f(\cdot,\cdot):\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}\times\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}

\displaystyle\quad\quad\quad P\big[f(t,I_{t}^{(a,b)})\in A|Y_{0}=i\big]=\sum_{j\in S}P[Y_{t}=j|Y_{0}=i]P\big[f(t,I_{t}^{(a,b)})\in A|Y_{0}=i,Y_{t}=j\big].

(16.30)

Observe that on the event
\{Y_{0}=i,\,\,Y_{t}=j\}
for
f(t_{1},x)=e^{at_{1}}x
one has

\displaystyle e^{at}I_{t}^{(a,b)}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{=}}\frac{\int_{0}^{t}b(Y_{s})e^{a(s-t)}ds}{e^{-at}}

\displaystyle\quad=\frac{G^{a,b}_{j}(t-\tau_{g_{t}^{j}}^{j})+e^{-a(t-\tau_{g_{t}^{j}}^{j})}\Big[\Big(\prod_{k=1}^{g_{t}^{j}}L_{k}^{j}\Big)Q_{0}^{j}+\sum_{k=1}^{g_{t}^{j}}\Big(\prod_{l=k+1}^{g_{t}^{j}}L_{l}^{j}\Big)Q_{k}^{j}\Big]}{e^{-a(t-\tau_{g_{t}^{j}}^{j})}\Big(\prod_{k=1}^{g_{t}^{j}}L_{k}^{j}\Big)e^{-a\tau^{j}_{0}}}

\displaystyle\quad=\frac{G^{a,b}_{j}(t-\tau_{g_{t}^{j}}^{j})}{e^{-at}}+\frac{Q_{0}^{j}+S^{**}_{g_{t}^{j}}}{L_{0}^{j}}

\displaystyle\quad\stackrel{{\scriptstyle P}}{{\to}}\frac{Q_{0}^{j}+S^{**}_{\infty}}{L_{0}^{j}}

(16.31)

as
t\to\infty.

Last step follows by using
a<0
and proceeding similarly to the derivation done in the display (
15.5
). Notice that using similar steps of Lemma 6.2 with relevant assumptions
S^{**}_{\infty}
satisfies the unique solution of the SRE

S^{**}_{\infty}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{=}}\frac{1}{L_{1}^{j}}S^{**}_{\infty}+\frac{Q_{1}^{j}}{L_{1}^{j}},\quad S^{**}_{\infty}\perp\Big(\frac{1}{L_{1}^{j}},\frac{Q_{1}^{j}}{L_{1}^{j}}\Big).

Using (
16.31
), proceeding similar to the way we proved Theorem 2(a) from (
15.2
), the assertion of part (a) follows by taking limit
t\to\infty
in (
16.30
), denoting
S^{**}_{\infty}
by
\widetilde{V}_{j}.

Under the conditions of part (b) (that is when
a=0
) observe that

I^{(0,b)}_{t}=\int_{0}^{t}b(Y_{s})ds

and then applying the law of large number for renewal process (under condition
E_{\pi}|b(\cdot)|<\infty
) yields

\frac{I^{(0,b)}_{t}}{t}=\frac{\int_{0}^{t}b(Y_{s})ds}{t}\,\,\stackrel{{\scriptstyle a.s}}{{\to}}\,\,E_{\pi}b(\cdot),

as
t\to\infty
as required.

For the rest parts, we sketch the proofs omitting the details, as they are very similar to steps of Theorem 2(a) and Theorem 3(a) and follow finally by using (
16.30
) by taking
f(t,x)=x
. Observe that on the set
\{Y_{0}=i,Y_{t}=j\}
one has

\int_{0}^{t}b(Y_{s})ds=\sum_{i=1}^{g_{t}^{j}}\int_{\tau_{i-1}^{j}}^{\tau_{i}^{j}}b(Y_{s})ds+\int_{0}^{\tau_{0}^{j}}b(Y_{s})ds+b(j)(t-\tau_{g_{t}^{j}}),

where
\sum_{i=1}^{g_{t}^{j}}\int_{\tau_{i-1}^{j}}^{\tau_{i}^{j}}b(Y_{s})ds
is the key term determining the asymptotics result while
\int_{0}^{\tau_{0}^{j}}b(Y_{s})ds+b(j)(t-\tau_{g_{t}^{j}})
is just a
O_{p}(1)
which goes to
0
asymptotically after division with any exponent of
t.
Since
\Big(\sum_{i=1}^{g_{t}^{j}}\int_{\tau_{i-1}^{j}}^{\tau_{i}^{j}}b(Y_{s})ds:t\geq 0\Big)
can be seen as a renewal reward process with
i
-th reward being
\int_{\tau_{i-1}^{j}}^{\tau_{i}^{j}}b(Y_{s})ds,
using results derived in (
15.3
) of Theorem 2(a) and Theorem 3(a), one can the subsequently derive results based on conditions if
\sigma^{2}_{j,(b)}
is finite, or
=\infty
or
=0.

We prove the result for (ii). Observe that if
\widehat{b}(\cdot):=b(\cdot)-E_{\pi}b(\cdot)
, then on
\{Y_{0}=i,Y_{t}=j\}

I_{t}^{(0,b)}-tE_{\pi}b(\cdot)=\sum_{i=1}^{g_{t}^{j}}\int_{\tau_{i-1}^{j}}^{\tau_{i}^{j}}\widehat{b}(Y_{s})ds+\int_{0}^{\tau_{0}^{j}}\widehat{b}(Y_{s})ds+\widehat{b}(j)(t-\tau_{g_{t}^{j}}).

Under Assumptions 4(a),(b) with
a(\cdot)
replaced by
b(\cdot),
Lemma 10.1 suggests that
\int_{\tau_{0}^{j}}^{\tau_{1}^{j}}\widehat{b}(Y_{s})ds=\int_{\tau_{0}^{j}}^{\tau_{1}^{j}}(b(Y_{s})-E_{\pi}b(\cdot))ds
is regularly varying with index
\alpha_{b},
along with the slowly varying function
L_{1}(\cdot),
with following right and left tail asymptotic limits

\displaystyle\widetilde{\alpha}_{j,(b)}^{(+)}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle E\Big[\sum_{(i_{l},i_{l+1})\in\widetilde{A}_{1}^{j}\cap\mathbb{S}^{+}_{\alpha_{b}}}c_{i_{l}i_{l+1}}\big[b(i_{l})-E_{\pi}b(\cdot)\big]^{\alpha_{b}}\Big],

\displaystyle\widetilde{\alpha}_{j,(b)}^{(-)}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle E\Big[\sum_{(i_{l},i_{l+1})\in\widetilde{A}_{1}^{j}\cap\mathbb{S}^{-}_{\alpha_{b}}}c_{i_{l}i_{l+1}}|b(i_{l})-E_{\pi}b(\cdot)|^{\alpha_{b}}\Big],

\displaystyle\widetilde{\beta}_{j,(b)}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{\widetilde{\alpha}_{j,(b)}^{(-)}-\widetilde{\alpha}_{j,(b)}^{(+)}}{\widetilde{\alpha}_{j,(b)}^{(+)}+\widetilde{\alpha}_{j,(b)}^{(-)}}.

Only for this part denote
t^{1/\alpha_{b}}L_{1}(t),n^{1/\alpha_{b}}L_{1}(n)
by
\tilde{c}_{t},\tilde{c}_{n}
respectively.
For each
j\in S
using the quantity
\widetilde{\beta}_{j,(b)}
above, applying the stable central limit Theorem 4.5.1 of
[
62
]
, as sample size
n\to\infty
one has

\displaystyle\tilde{c}^{-1}_{n}\Big(\sum_{i=1}^{n}\int_{\tau_{i-1}^{j}}^{\tau_{i}^{j}}\widehat{b}(Y_{s})ds\Big)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{\to}}\sigma_{(j,\alpha_{b},(b))}\mathcal{S}_{\alpha_{b}}(1,-\beta_{j,(b)},0).

(16.32)

Using (
16.32
) and Theorem 3.2 of
[
34
]
for the stopped random time
g_{t}^{j}
as
t\to\infty,

\frac{\sum_{i=1}^{g_{t}^{j}}\int_{\tau_{i-1}^{j}}^{\tau_{i}^{j}}\widehat{b}(Y_{s})ds}{(g_{t}^{j})^{\frac{1}{\alpha_{b}}}L_{1}(g_{t}^{j})}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{\to}}\sigma_{(j,\alpha_{b},(b))}\mathcal{S}_{\alpha_{b}}(1,-\beta_{j,(b)},0).

Furthermore observe
\tau_{0}^{j}+(t-\tau_{g_{t}^{j}}^{j})
is
O_{p}(1)
and
\lim_{t\to\infty}\frac{\tilde{c}_{g_{t}^{j}}}{\tilde{c}_{t}}=\Big(\frac{1}{E|\mathfrak{I}^{j}_{1}|}\Big)^{\frac{1}{\alpha_{b}}}
(see Lemma 12.3(a)). Hence as
t\to\infty
we have

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}\Big(\tilde{c}_{t}^{-1}\big[I_{t}^{(0,b)}-tE_{\pi}b(\cdot)\big]|Y_{0}=i,Y_{t}=j\Big)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle w}}{{\to}}\mathcal{L}\bigg(\big(E|\mathfrak{I}^{j}_{1}|\big)^{-\frac{1}{\alpha_{b}}}\sigma_{(j,\alpha_{b},(b))}\mathcal{S}_{\alpha_{b}}(1,-\beta_{j,(b)},0)\bigg).

Putting this estimate in (
16.30
) using
f(t,x)=x
, and taking
t\to\infty,
the second part of Theorem 4(b) will follow. Third part is trivial and the proof is omitted.
∎
