Proof of Supplementary parts of Corollary 4.5

17.0.1
Proof of
Lemma 11.1(a)

Proof.

Proceeding similar to the proof of Theorem 2(a) (and using same notation) observe that,

\displaystyle|I_{t}^{(ma,b)}|^{\frac{1}{m}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\big|\int_{0}^{t}b(Y_{s})e^{-\int_{s}^{t}ma(Y_{r})dr}ds\big|^{\frac{1}{m}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\Phi_{t}^{(a)}|\widetilde{I}_{t}^{(ma,b)}|^{\frac{1}{m}}.

Suppose
\{(L_{i,m}^{j},Q_{i,m}^{j}):i\geq 1\},

(L_{0,m}^{j},Q_{0,m}^{j})
are defined similarly as
\{(L_{i}^{j},Q_{i}^{j}):i\geq 1\}
and
(L_{0}^{j},Q_{0}^{j})
by replacing
a(\cdot)
by
ma(\cdot)
respectively.
\{S_{n,m}^{**}:n\geq 1\}
are defined as

S^{**}_{n,m}:=P_{n}\Big(\frac{1}{L_{1,m}^{j}},\frac{Q_{1,m}^{j}}{L_{1,m}^{j}}\Big)=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\frac{Q_{i,m}^{j}}{L_{i,m}^{j}}\prod_{k=1}^{i-1}\Big(\frac{1}{L_{k,m}^{j}}\Big).

Observe that on
\{Y_{0}=i,Y_{t}=j\},

\displaystyle\widetilde{I}^{(ma,b)}_{t}=\bigg[\frac{Q_{0,m}^{j}+S^{**}_{g_{t}^{j},m}}{L_{0,m}^{j}}+\frac{G_{j}^{(ma,b)}(t-\tau_{g_{t}^{j}})}{\Phi_{t}^{(ma)}}\bigg].

Using same notations as in the proof of Theorem 2(a), let
\widetilde{G}^{(3,m)}_{t}
be defined as

\displaystyle\widetilde{G}^{(3,m)}_{t}:=\bigg[\frac{Q_{0,m}^{j}+S^{**}_{g_{t}^{j},m}}{L_{0,m}^{j}}+\frac{G_{j}^{(ma,b)}(t-\tau_{g_{t}^{j}})}{\Phi_{t}^{(ma)}}\bigg]^{\frac{1}{m\sqrt{t}}}.

Observe that

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}\Big(|\widetilde{I}^{(ma,b)}_{t}|^{\frac{1}{m\sqrt{t}}}\mid Y_{0}=i,Y_{t}=j\Big)=\mathcal{L}\Big(\widetilde{G}^{(3,m)}_{t}\mid Y_{0}=i,Y_{t}=j\Big)

(17.1)

and furthermore

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}\bigg(\bigg(\frac{[\Phi^{(a)}_{t}]^{\frac{1}{\sqrt{t}}}}{e^{-\sqrt{t}E_{\pi}a(\cdot)}},\frac{|I^{(ma,b)}_{t}|^{\frac{1}{m\sqrt{t}}}}{e^{-\sqrt{t}E_{\pi}a(\cdot)}}\bigg)\mid Y_{0}=i,Y_{t}=j\bigg)

\displaystyle\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,=\mathcal{L}\Big(\big(G^{(1)}_{t}G^{(2)}_{t},G^{(1)}_{t}G^{(2)}_{t}\widetilde{G}^{(3,m)}_{t}\big)\mid Y_{0}=i,Y_{t}=j\Big).

Since
S^{**}_{g_{t}^{j},m}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{\to}}S_{m}^{**}
where
S_{m}^{**}
satisfies

S_{m}^{**}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{=}}\frac{1}{L_{1,m}^{j}}S_{m}^{**}+\frac{Q_{1,m}^{j}}{L_{1,m}^{j}},\,\,\text{with}\,\,E\log\Big[\frac{1}{L_{1,m}^{j}}\Big]<0,\,\,E\log^{+}\Big|\frac{Q_{1,m}^{j}}{L_{1,m}^{j}}\Big|<\infty,

under Assumption 3(c) with
(a,b)
replaced by
(ma,b)
.

Hence
S^{**}_{g_{t}^{j},m}
is
O_{p}(1),
along with
(L^{j}_{0,m},Q^{j}_{0,m},t-\tau_{g_{t}^{j}})
. Since
\Phi_{t}^{(ma)}\to\infty,
as
t\to\infty

\mathcal{L}\Big(\widetilde{G}^{(3,m)}_{t}\mid Y_{0}=i,Y_{t}=j\Big)=\exp\bigg[\frac{\log\Big|\frac{Q_{0,m}^{j}+S^{**}_{g_{t}^{j},m}}{L_{0,m}^{j}}+\frac{G_{j}^{(ma,b)}(t-\tau_{g_{t}^{j}})}{\Phi_{t}^{(ma)}}\Big|}{m\sqrt{t}}\bigg]\stackrel{{\scriptstyle w}}{{\to}}\delta_{\{1\}}.

Using similar analysis on the asymptotic results of
G_{t}^{(1)},G_{t}^{(2)},
proceeding as the rest of the proof of Theorem 2(a), the assertion of Lemma 11.1(a) will follow.
∎

17.0.2
Proof of
Lemma 11.1(b)

Let
\{(L_{i,m}^{j},Q_{i,m}^{j}):i\geq 0\}
be defined as in Lemma 11.1(a) and for any
n\geq 1,
denote
Z_{n,m}^{\otimes j}:=\max_{1\leq k\leq n}\Big\{\Big(\prod_{i=1}^{k-1}L_{i,m}^{j}\Big)|Q_{k,m}^{j}|\Big\},
and

P^{(j)}_{n,m}:=\sum_{k=1}^{n}\bigg(\prod_{i=1}^{k-1}L_{i,m}^{j}\bigg)Q_{k,m}^{j},\quad\widetilde{P}^{(j)}_{n,m}:=\sum_{k=1}^{n}\bigg(\prod_{i=k+1}^{n}L_{i,m}^{j}\bigg)Q_{k,m}^{j}.

Proof.

Using a similar approach and notations of the proof of Theorem 2(b), we observe that

\displaystyle\bigg(\log\Phi_{t}^{(a)},\frac{\log|I_{t}^{(ma,b)}|}{m}\bigg)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathcal{L}(\cdot|Y_{0}=i,Y_{t}=j)}}{{=}}\bigg(\log L^{j}_{0}+\log\bigg(\prod_{k=1}^{g^{j}_{t}}L^{j}_{k}\bigg)+\log(e^{-a(j)(t-\tau_{g_{t}^{j}}^{j})}),

\displaystyle\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\frac{1}{m}\log\bigg|e^{-ma(j)(t-\tau_{g_{t}^{j}}^{j})}\bigg[\bigg(\prod_{k=1}^{g_{t}^{j}}L^{j}_{k,m}\bigg)Q^{j}_{0,m}+\widetilde{P}^{(j)}_{g_{t}^{j},m}\bigg]+G_{j}^{(ma,b)}\big(t-\tau_{g_{t}^{j}}^{j}\big)\bigg|\bigg)

\displaystyle\quad\quad\quad\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{=}}\bigg(S_{g_{t}^{j}}^{\otimes j}+[\log L^{j}_{0}-a(j)(t-\tau_{g_{t}^{j}}^{j})]\,\,,\,\,\frac{\log Z_{g_{t}^{j},m}^{\otimes j}}{m}+\frac{1}{m}\log\bigg|B_{t,m}^{(1)}+\frac{B_{t,m}^{(2)}}{Z_{g_{t}^{j},m}^{\otimes j}}P_{g_{t}^{j},m}^{(j)}\bigg|\bigg)

(17.2)

where

\displaystyle B_{t,m}^{(1)}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{e^{-ma(j)(t-\tau_{g_{t}^{j}}^{j})}\bigg(\prod_{k=1}^{g_{t}^{j}}L^{j}_{k,m}\bigg)Q^{j}_{0,m}+G_{j}^{(ma,b)}\big(t-\tau_{g_{t}^{j}}^{j}\big)}{Z_{g_{t}^{j},m}^{\otimes j}},

\displaystyle B_{t,m}^{(2)}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle e^{-ma(j)(t-\tau_{g_{t}^{j}}^{j})},

and denote
\bigg(B_{t,m}^{(1)}+B_{t,m}^{(2)}\frac{P_{g_{t}^{j},m}^{(j)}}{Z_{g_{t}^{j},m}^{\otimes j}}\bigg)
by
B^{(3)}_{t,m}.
We now consider the scaled limit of
\Big(\frac{\log\Phi_{t}^{(a)}}{\sqrt{t}},\frac{\log|I_{t}^{(ma,b)}|}{m\sqrt{t}}\Big)
as
t\to\infty.
Observe that

B_{t,m}^{(1)}=\frac{e^{-ma(j)(t-\tau_{g_{t}^{j}}^{j})}\bigg(\prod_{k=1}^{g_{t}^{j}}L^{j}_{k,m}\bigg)Q^{j}_{0,m}+G_{j}^{(ma,b)}\big(t-\tau_{g_{t}^{j}}^{j}\big)}{\max_{1\leq k\leq g_{t}^{j}}\Big(\prod_{i=1}^{k-1}L_{i,m}^{j}\Big)|Q_{k,m}^{j}|},

and
B_{t,m}^{(2)}
are both
O_{p}(1)
by construction, and for each
1\leq k\leq n,

-\max_{1\leq k\leq g_{t}^{j}}\Big(\prod_{i=1}^{k-1}L_{i,m}^{j}\Big)|Q_{k,m}^{j}|\leq Q_{k,m}^{j}\Big(\prod_{i=1}^{k-1}L_{i,m}^{j}\Big)\leq\max_{1\leq k\leq g_{t}^{j}}\Big(\prod_{i=1}^{k-1}L_{i,m}^{j}\Big)|Q_{k,m}^{j}|.

Hence for all
1\leq k\leq n,

Q_{k,m}^{j}\Big(\prod_{i=1}^{k-1}L_{i,m}^{j}\Big)\in\Big(-Z_{n,m}^{\otimes j},Z_{n,m}^{\otimes j}\Big).

This implies that for all
n\geq 1,

-nZ_{n,m}^{\otimes j}\leq P_{n,m}^{(j)}\leq nZ_{n,m}^{\otimes j},

which further implies that
\Big|\frac{P_{g_{t}^{j},m}^{(j)}}{Z_{g_{t}^{j},m}^{\otimes j}}\Big|\leq g_{t}^{j}
; hence
|B^{(3)}_{t,m}|=O_{p}(t)
and strictly positive. As
t\to\infty,

\frac{\log|B_{t,m}^{(3)}|}{m\sqrt{t}}=\frac{\log\Big|B_{t,m}^{(1)}+B_{t,m}^{(2)}\frac{P_{g_{t}^{j},m}^{(j)}}{Z_{g_{t}^{j},m}^{\otimes j}}\Big|}{m\sqrt{t}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle P}}{{\to}}0.

Now we proceed identically as done in Theorem 2(b) and observe that

\displaystyle P\bigg[\Big(\frac{\log\Phi_{t}^{(a)}}{\sqrt{t}},\frac{\log|I_{t}^{(ma,b)}|}{m\sqrt{t}}\Big)\in\cdot\quad\mid Y_{0}=i\bigg]

\displaystyle=\sum_{j\in S}P[Y_{t}=j|Y_{0}=i]P\Big[\Big(\frac{\log\Phi_{t}^{(a)}}{\sqrt{t}},\frac{\log|I_{t}^{(ma,b)}|}{m\sqrt{t}}\Big)\in\cdot\quad\mid Y_{0}=i,Y_{t}=j\Big]

\displaystyle=\sum_{j\in S}\pi_{j}\lim_{t\to\infty}P\bigg[\bigg(\frac{S_{g_{t}^{j}}^{\otimes j}}{\sqrt{t}},\frac{\log Z_{g_{t}^{j},m}^{\otimes j}}{m\sqrt{t}}\bigg)\in\cdot\quad\mid Y_{0}=i\bigg],

by applying Lemma 6.1, with

L_{t}^{(1)}:=\bigg(\frac{[\log L^{j}_{0}-a(j)(t-\tau_{g_{t}^{j}}^{j})]}{\sqrt{t}},\frac{\log|B_{t,m}^{(3)}|}{m\sqrt{t}}\bigg)^{\prime},H_{t}:=\bigg(\frac{S_{g_{t}^{j}}^{\otimes j}}{\sqrt{t}},\frac{\log Z_{g_{t}^{j},m}^{\otimes j}}{m\sqrt{t}}\bigg)^{\prime},

and
L_{t}^{(2)}
defined as in Theorem 2(b) (with the verification lemma that
H_{t}
satisfies (6.1), using ideas similar to Lemma
15.2
(c)).

For any two sequences
\{u_{k}\}_{k\geq 1},\{v_{k}\}_{k\geq 1},
one has

\max\{u_{k}\}-\max\{v_{k}\}\leq\max\{u_{k}+v_{k}\}\leq\max\{u_{k}\}+\max\{v_{k}\}.

As a result we can bound

\displaystyle\log Z_{g_{t}^{j},m}^{\otimes j}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\max_{1\leq k\leq g_{t}^{j}}\Big\{\log\Big(\prod_{i=1}^{K-1}L_{i,m}^{j}\Big)|Q_{k,m}^{j}|\Big\}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\max_{1\leq k\leq g_{t}^{j}}\{\sum_{i=1}^{k-1}\log L_{i,m}^{j}+\log|Q_{k,m}^{j}|\}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\max_{1\leq k\leq g_{t}^{j}}\{m\sum_{i=1}^{k-1}\log L_{i}^{j}+\log|Q_{k,m}^{j}|\},

in the following manner

\displaystyle\,\,\,\,\frac{M_{g_{t}^{j}}^{\otimes j}}{\sqrt{t}}-\frac{\max_{1\leq k\leq g_{t}^{j}}\log|Q_{k,m}^{j}|}{m\sqrt{t}}\leq\frac{\log Z_{g_{t}^{j},m}^{\otimes j}}{m\sqrt{t}}\leq\frac{M_{g_{t}^{j}}^{\otimes j}}{\sqrt{t}}+\frac{\max_{1\leq k\leq g_{t}^{j}}\log|Q_{k,m}^{j}|}{m\sqrt{t}}.

(17.3)

Since (4.3) holds with
(a,b)
replaced by
(ma,b),
it follows that
\frac{\max_{1\leq k\leq g_{t}^{j}}\log|Q_{k,m}^{j}|}{m\sqrt{t}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle a.s}}{{\to}}0

(following arguments similar to Lemma
15.2
(a)). We get the final result by applying the weak limit of
\Big(\frac{S_{g_{t}^{j}}^{\otimes j}}{\sqrt{t}},\frac{M_{g_{t}^{j}}^{\otimes j}}{\sqrt{t}}\Big)
by using Lemma
15.2
(b).
∎

17.1
Proof of
Corollary 5.2

Proof.

Proceeding similar to the proof of Theorem 5.9, integrating in the range
[0,t]
yields,

\displaystyle X_{t}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle X_{0}e^{-\int_{0}^{t}a(Y_{s})ds}+\int_{0}^{t}b(Y_{s})e^{-\int_{s}^{t}a(Y_{r})dr}d\mathcal{\bf{S}}_{\alpha^{*},0}(s)

(17.4)

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{=}}

\displaystyle X_{0}\Phi^{(a)}_{t}+|I^{(\alpha^{*}a,b^{{}^{\alpha^{*}}})}_{t}|^{1/\alpha^{*}}\mathcal{S}_{\alpha^{*}}(1,0,0)

Using the continuous mapping theorem on Theorem 1, part (A) will follow.

For the rest of part (B), (C), (D) we observe that marginal evolution of the process at (
17.4
) resembles with
Z_{t}^{(2)}
in Corollary 4.5 of
[
Majumder2024semi
]
with
a_{2}=X_{0},b_{2}=\mathcal{S}_{\alpha^{*}}(1,0,0),m=\alpha^{*};
hence all the results will follow by replacing
(a,b)
by
(a,b^{{}^{\alpha^{*}}})
in Corollary 4.5 for
|Z_{t}^{(2)}|
.
∎

18
Proofs of the lemmas in the Appendix with some additional results

Proofs of Lemma 12.1, Proposition 2.2, Lemma 12.2, Lemma 12.3(a),(b),(c), and one supplementary result are given here.

18.1
Proof of
Lemma 12.1

Proof.

We use the notations similar to section 8 of
[
20
]
. Define the matrix of functions
A(t):=(A_{ij}(t):i,j\in S)
such that
A_{ij}(t):=Q_{ij}(t)=P\big[J_{1}=j,T_{1}\leq t\mid J_{0}=j\big]=P_{ij}F_{ij}(t).

A
represents the semi-Markov matrix. For any two matrix valued functions
B(t):=(B_{ij}(t):i,j\in S),C(t):=(C_{ij}(t):i,j\in S),
by the convolution
B\star C
we denote the matrix valued functions where for each
i,j\in S,\quad(B\star C)_{ij}(t):=\int_{0}^{t}\sum_{k\in S}B_{ik}(du)C_{kj}(t-u)=\int_{0}^{t}\sum_{k\in S}B_{ik}(t-u)C_{kj}(du).
This manner recuresively for
n\geq 1,
we have
A^{(n)}=A\star A^{(n-1)}
and the interpretation of
A_{ij}^{(n)}(t)=P[Y_{n}=j,T_{n}\leq t\mid Y_{0}=i].
Define the renewal function at state
j
starting from
i
as
R_{ij}(t):=E\big[(g_{t}^{j}+1)1_{\{t\geq\tau_{0}^{j}\}}\mid Y_{0}=i\big]=\sum_{n\geq 0}A_{ij}^{(n)}(t)
as the expected number of hitting at state
j
before time
t.
Using similar notations of
[
20
]
by
V^{-}(t)
we denote
t-S_{N_{t}}.
Note that on
\{Y_{t}=j\},

\tau_{g_{t}^{j}}=S_{N_{t}},
hence we can safely write
P_{{}_{i}}[t-\tau_{g_{t}^{j}}^{j}>x,Y_{t}=j]=P_{{}_{i}}[t-S_{N_{t}}>x,Y_{t}=j]=P_{{}_{i}}[V^{-}(t)>x,Y_{t}=j].

Observe that

\displaystyle P_{{}_{i}}[V^{-}(t)>x,Y_{t}=j]=P_{{}_{i}}[V^{-}(t)>x,Y_{t}=j,T_{1}>t]+P_{{}_{i}}[V^{-}(t)>x,Y_{t}=j,T_{1}<t]

\displaystyle\quad\quad=\delta_{\{i=j\}}1_{\{t>x\}}P[T_{1}>t\mid Y_{0}=j]+\sum_{k\in S}\int_{0}^{t}P_{ik}F_{ik}(du)P_{k}\big[V^{-}(t-u)>x,Y_{t-u}=j\big]

\displaystyle\,\,=\delta_{\{i=j\}}1_{\{t>x\}}\sum_{k\in S}P_{jk}\bar{F}_{jk}(t)+\sum_{k\in S}\int_{0}^{t}A_{ik}(du)P_{{}_{k}}\big[V^{-}(t-u)>x,Y_{t-u}=j\big].

Now for fixed
i,x,
writing
U_{j}(t):=P_{{}_{i}}[V^{-}(t)>x,Y_{t}=j],
and

G_{j}(t):=\delta_{\{i=j\}}1_{\{t>x\}}\sum_{k\in S}P_{jk}\bar{F}_{jk}(t),

from aforementioned relation we have the renewal equation

\displaystyle U=G+A\star U.

(18.1)

Observe that
G_{j}(t)=\delta_{\{i=j\}}1_{\{t>x\}}\sum_{k\in S}P_{jk}\bar{F}_{jk}(t)=\delta_{\{i=j\}}1_{\{t>x\}}(1-F_{j}(t))\leq(1-F_{j}(t))=1-A_{j}(t)1,
which implies that
G\leq 1-A.1
co-ordinate wise, and as a consequence of Theorem 3.17 of
[
20
]
there exists a solution of the equation (
18.1
), and from Theorem 3.11 and display 4.9 of
[
20
]
the solution is unique if all states are conservative (which is granted under Assumption 1). The minimal solution satisfies

\displaystyle P_{{}_{i}}[V^{-}(t)>x,Y_{t}=j]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{t}R_{ij}(du)1_{\{t-u>x\}}(1-F_{j}(t-u))

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{t}R_{ij}(du)1_{\{t-u>x\}}\sum_{k\in S}P_{jk}\bar{F}_{jk}(t-u).

Now using analog of Key-Renewal Theorem 6.3 of
[
20
]
gives

\displaystyle\lim_{t\to\infty}P_{{}_{i}}[V^{-}(t)>x,Y_{t}=j]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\lim_{t\to\infty}\int_{0}^{t}R_{ij}(du)1_{\{t-u>x\}}\big(1-F_{j}(t-u)\big)

(18.2)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{\mu_{j}\int_{x}^{\infty}(1-F_{j}(y))dy}{\sum_{k\in S}\mu_{k}m_{k}},

given the fact that state
j
is recurrent (follows from Assumption 1) and the function
t\to 1_{\{t>x\}}(1-F_{j}(t))
is directly Riemann integrable (in short d.r.i). Definition and the necessary-sufficient conditions of ‘d.r.i functions’ can be found in Section 4 (Page 153 of chapter V (Renewal theory) of
[
5
]
) and Proposition 4.1 of
[
5
]
respectively. Since the distribution function
F_{j}(t)=\sum_{k\in S}P_{jk}F_{jk}(t)
has at most countable number of jumps so it is continuous almost everywhere w.r.t Lebesgue measure and the function
1_{\{t>x\}}(1-F_{j}(t))
is bounded as well. So using Proposition 4.1(i) of
[
5
]
the function
1_{\{t>x\}}(1-F_{j}(t))
is d.r.i hence the above assertion (
18.2
) holds.
∎

18.2
Proof of
Proposition 2.2

Proof.

Note that the distribution of
\tau_{g^{j}_{t}+2}^{j}-\tau_{g^{j}_{t}+1}^{j}
is identical as
\tau^{j}_{2}-\tau_{1}^{j},
since

\displaystyle P[\tau_{g^{j}_{t}+2}^{j}-\tau_{g^{j}_{t}+1}^{j}>x]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}P[\tau_{g^{j}_{t}+2}^{j}-\tau_{g^{j}_{t}+1}^{j}>x\mid g^{n}_{t}=n]P[g^{n}_{t}=n]

(18.3)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}P[\tau_{n+2}^{j}-\tau_{n+1}^{j}>x\mid g^{j}_{t}=n]P[g^{j}_{t}=n]

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(a)}}{{=}}

\displaystyle\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}P[\tau_{1}^{j}-\tau_{0}^{j}>x]P[g^{j}_{t}=n]=P[\tau_{1}^{j}-\tau_{0}^{j}>x]

where (a) holds from the fact that the event
\{g_{t}^{j}=n\}=\{\tau_{n}^{j}\leq t<\tau_{n+1}^{j}\}
is independent with the event
\{\tau_{n+2}^{j}-\tau_{n+1}^{j}>x\}
due to the regeneration property, and the latter is identical in probability with
\{\tau_{1}^{j}-\tau_{0}^{j}>x\},
proving the assertion of part (i).

Similar to the steps done in (
18.3
) one can show that any functional of
Y
in
\{s:s\geq\tau^{j}_{g_{t}^{j}+1}\}
is independent of
g_{t}^{j}
and identically distributed as that functional on
\{s:s\geq\tau_{0}^{j}\}
for any
t\geq\tau_{0}^{j}
for part (ii).

For part (iii), observe that
\{g^{j}_{t}=n,Y_{t}=j^{\prime}\}=\{\tau^{j}_{n}\leq t<\tau^{j}_{n+1},Y_{t}=j^{\prime}\}.
For any two sets
A,B

\displaystyle P[Y^{(1)}_{t}\in A,Y^{(2)}_{t}\in B\mid K_{t},Y_{t}=j^{\prime}]

\displaystyle\quad\quad=

\displaystyle\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}P\big[Y^{(1)}_{t}\in A,Y^{(2)}_{t}\in B\mid K_{t},Y_{t}=j^{\prime},g^{j}_{t}=n\big]P[g^{j}_{t}=n\mid K_{t},Y_{t}=j^{\prime}]

\displaystyle\quad\quad\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(a)}}{{=}}

\displaystyle\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}P\big[Y^{(1)}_{t}\in A\mid K_{t},g^{j}_{t}=n,Y_{t}=j^{\prime}\big]P\big[Y^{(2)}_{t}\in B\mid K_{t},g^{j}_{t}=n,Y_{t}=j^{\prime}\big]

\displaystyle\quad\times P[g^{j}_{t}=n\mid K_{t},Y_{t}=j^{\prime}]

\displaystyle\quad\quad\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(b)}}{{=}}

\displaystyle\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}P\big[Y^{(1)}_{t}\in A\mid K_{t},g^{j}_{t}=n,Y_{t}=j^{\prime}\big]P\big[Y^{(2)}_{t}\in B\mid K_{t}\big]P[g^{j}_{t}=n\mid K_{t},Y_{t}=j^{\prime}]

\displaystyle\quad\quad=

\displaystyle P[Y^{(1)}_{t}\in A\mid K_{t},Y_{t}=j^{\prime}\big]P\big[Y^{(2)}_{t}\in B\mid K_{t}].

Equality in
(a)
follows from the fact that conditioned on
\{K_{t},g^{j}_{t}=n,Y_{t}=j^{\prime}\}
the processes
Y^{(1)}_{t},Y^{(2)}_{t}
are respectively
\sigma\{\mathcal{H}_{i}:i\leq n\}
and
\sigma\{\mathcal{H}_{i}:i\geq n+2\}
measurable which are independent sigma fields. Equality in
(b)
follows as conditioned on
\{K_{t}\}
and
\{g_{t}^{j}=n,Y_{t}=j^{\prime}\}

Y_{t}^{(2)}
is
\sigma\{\mathcal{H}_{i}:i\geq n+2\}
measurable and independent with
\{g_{t}^{j}=n,Y_{t}=j^{\prime}\}=\{\tau_{n}^{j}\leq t<\tau_{n+1}^{j},Y_{t}=j^{\prime}\}
as the latter is
\sigma\{\mathcal{H}_{i}:i\leq n+1\}
measurable.

∎

18.3
Proof of
Lemma 12.2

Proof.

We prove that
\log|L^{j}_{0}|=O_{P}(1)
and
|Q^{j}_{0}|=O_{P}(1)
. Let, for arbitrary
i,j\in S
,
A^{(k)}_{ij}
denote the event that
Y
visits the state
j
in
k
-th excursion from state
i
to itself (i.e in time interval
[\tau_{k-1}^{i},\tau_{k}^{i})
). Clearly
0<P\big(A^{(k)}_{ij}\big)<1
for any
k\in\mathbb{N}
since
Y
is irreducible in
S
. Observe that on
\{Y_{0}=i\}
for any arbitrary
i\in S
,

\log|L^{j}_{0}|=-\int_{0}^{\tau_{0}^{j}}a(Y_{s})ds\leq\sum_{l=1}^{k^{*}-1}\log L_{k}^{i}+\int_{\tau_{k^{*}-1}^{i}}^{\tau_{k^{*}}^{i}}|a(Y_{s})|ds,\quad k^{*}=\inf\Big\{k\geq 1:1_{A^{(k)}_{ij}}=1\Big\}.

Observe that above upper bound is a geometric sum of random variables having finite expectation which proves the first assertion that
\log|L^{j}_{0}|=O_{P}(1)
.
Similarly, on
\{Y_{0}=i\}
,

\displaystyle Q^{j}_{0}

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{\tau_{0}^{j}}b(Y_{s})e^{-\int_{s}^{\tau_{0}^{j}}a(Y_{r})dr}ds

\displaystyle=e^{(\tau_{0}^{j}-\tau_{k^{*}}^{i})}\sum_{l=1}^{k^{*}-1}\Big(\prod_{n=l+1}^{k^{*}}L_{n}^{i}\Big)Q_{l}^{i}+\int_{\tau_{k*-1}^{i}}^{\tau_{0}^{j}}b(Y_{s})e^{-\int_{s}^{\tau_{0}^{j}}a(Y_{r})dr}ds.

Note that
[\tau_{k*-1}^{i},\tau_{0}^{j})\subset[\tau_{k*-1}^{i},\tau_{k*}^{i})
and that

\sum_{l=1}^{\infty}\Big(\prod_{n=l+1}^{\infty}L_{n}^{i}\Big)Q_{l}^{i}

is absolutely convergent due to Assumption 2. Hence,
|Q^{j}_{0}|=O_{P}(1)
.
∎

18.4
Statement of
Lemma 12.3

(a)

Suppose Assumption 1 holds and
c_{\cdot}
is a function defined on
\mathbb{N}
as (2.15) and its definition is extended to the whole
\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
for any arbitrary slowly varying function
L(\cdot)
. Let
g^{j}_{t}
be defined as the number of total renewals to the state
j,
before time
t.
Then for any fixed
s>0,

\lim_{t\to\infty}c^{-1}_{g_{st}^{j}}/c^{-1}_{t}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle a.s}}{{\to}}\Big(\frac{s}{E|\mathfrak{I}^{j}_{1}|}\Big)^{-\frac{1}{\alpha}}.

(b)

For any function
L(\cdot)
slowly varying at
+\infty,
and any two functions
a_{1}(\cdot),b_{1}(\cdot):\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}\to\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
such that both
a_{1}(t),b_{1}(t)\to\infty
as
t\to\infty
and
a_{1}\leq b_{1};
for any
\epsilon>0
there exists
t_{0,\epsilon}\geq 0,
such that for all
t\geq t_{0,\epsilon},

\displaystyle(1-\epsilon)\Big(\frac{b_{1}(t)}{a_{1}(t)}\Big)^{-\epsilon}\leq\frac{L(b_{1}(t))}{L(a_{1}(t))}\leq(1+\epsilon)\Big(\frac{b_{1}(t)}{a_{1}(t)}\Big)^{\epsilon}

(18.4)

(c)

For any function
L(\cdot)
slowly varying at
t\to\infty,

\lim_{t\to\infty}\frac{\log L(t)}{\log t}\to 0
.

18.4.1
Proof of
Lemma 12.3(a)

Proof.

Observe that,

c^{-1}_{g_{st}^{j}}/c^{-1}_{t}=\frac{(g_{st}^{j})^{-\frac{1}{\alpha}}L_{0}^{-1}(g_{st})}{t^{-\frac{1}{\alpha}}L^{-1}_{0}(t)},

where
L_{0}(\cdot)
depends on
L(\cdot)
through (2.15) as it is defined as
c_{n}\sim n^{-1/\alpha}L_{0}(n).

The result follows from the fact
\lim_{t\to\infty}\frac{g_{st}}{\frac{s}{E|\mathfrak{I}^{j}_{1}|}t}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle a.s}}{{\to}}1,
and
\lim_{t\to\infty}\frac{L_{0}(t)}{L_{0}(g^{j}_{st})}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle a.s}}{{\to}}1
as an application of Potter’s bound. To see the second result observe that given a small
\epsilon_{1}\in\Big(0,\frac{s}{E|\mathfrak{I}_{1}^{j}|}\Big),
for each
\omega\in A
with
P[A]=1
there exists
t_{1}(\omega,\epsilon_{1})
such that
\frac{g_{st}}{\frac{s}{E|\mathfrak{I}^{j}_{1}|}t}\geq 1-\frac{\epsilon_{1}}{\frac{s}{E|\mathfrak{I}^{j}_{1}|}},
for all
t\geq t_{1}(\omega,\epsilon_{1}).
Since

\frac{L_{0}(t)}{L_{0}(g_{st})}=\frac{L_{0}(t)}{L_{0}\big(t\big(\frac{s}{E|\mathfrak{I}^{j}_{1}|}-\epsilon_{1}\big)\big)}\frac{L_{0}\big(t\big(\frac{s}{E|\mathfrak{I}^{j}_{1}|}-\epsilon_{1}\big)\big)}{L_{0}(g_{st})},

the first term goes to
1
as
t\to\infty
by definition. The second term can be bounded by using Potter’s bound for any slowly varying function
L_{0}(\cdot)
which suggests that for all
\epsilon>0,
there exists
t_{0}>0,
such that

(1-\epsilon)\bigg(\frac{\frac{g_{st}^{j}}{t}}{\frac{s}{E|\mathfrak{I}^{j}_{1}|}-\epsilon_{1}}\bigg)^{-\epsilon}\leq\frac{L_{0}(g^{j}_{st})}{L_{0}\big(t\big(\frac{s}{E|\mathfrak{I}^{j}_{1}|}-\epsilon_{1}\big)\big)}\leq(1+\epsilon)\bigg(\frac{\frac{g_{st}^{j}}{\frac{s}{E|\mathfrak{I}^{j}_{1}|}t}}{1-\frac{\epsilon_{1}}{\frac{s}{E|\mathfrak{I}^{j}_{1}|}}}\bigg)^{\epsilon}

for all
t\geq t_{0}\vee t_{1}(\omega,\epsilon_{1}).
As both
\epsilon_{1},\epsilon
can be chosen arbitrarily small, both sides have an almost sure limit to
1,
for large
t
proving the part (b) of the Lemma.
∎

18.4.2
Proof of
Lemma 12.3(b)

Proof.

Using Karamata’s representation theorem for a slowly varying function
L(\cdot)
at
\infty,
there exist
x_{0}\geq 0
, functions
c_{1}(\cdot),\eta(\cdot)
such that there exists a constant
c_{0},
for which
\lim_{x\to\infty}c_{1}(x)=c_{0},\lim_{x\to\infty}\eta(x)=0,
and
L(\cdot)
can be represented as
L(x)=c_{1}(x)e^{\int_{x_{0}}^{x}\frac{\eta(s)}{s}ds}.

Using this representation for any
t\geq 0,

\frac{L(b_{1}(t))}{L(a_{1}(t))}=\frac{c_{1}(b_{1}(t))}{c_{1}(a_{1}(t))}\frac{e^{\int_{x_{0}}^{b_{1}(t)}\frac{\eta(s)}{s}ds}}{e^{\int_{x_{0}}^{a_{1}(t)}\frac{\eta(s)}{s}ds}}=\frac{c_{1}(b_{1}(t))}{c_{1}(a_{1}(t))}e^{\int_{1}^{\frac{b_{1}(t)}{a_{1}(t)}}\frac{\eta(a_{1}(t)s)}{s}ds}.

Since both
(a_{1}(t),b_{1}(t))\to(\infty,\infty)
as
t\to\infty,
for any
\epsilon>0,
there exists
t_{0,\epsilon}\geq 0
such that

\Big|\frac{c_{1}(b_{1}(t))}{c_{1}(a_{1}(t))}-1\Big|<\epsilon\quad\&\quad|\eta(a_{1}(t)s)|\leq\epsilon\quad\forall\,\,t\geq t_{0,\epsilon}.

If
|\eta(a_{1}(t)s)|\leq\epsilon,
it implies that

-\epsilon\log\Big(\frac{b_{1}(t)}{a_{1}(t)}\Big)\leq\int_{1}^{\frac{b_{1}(t)}{a_{1}(t)}}\frac{\eta(a_{1}(t)s)}{s}ds\leq\epsilon\log\Big(\frac{b_{1}(t)}{a_{1}(t)}\Big),

which together with
\Big|\frac{c_{1}(b_{1}(t))}{c_{1}(a_{1}(t))}-1\Big|<\epsilon,
prove the assertion in (
18.4
).
∎

18.4.3
Proof of
Lemma 12.3(c)

Proof.

For any
L(t)
slowly varying at
t\to\infty,
we show that
\lim_{t\to\infty}\frac{\log L(t)}{\log t}\to 0.
Suppose given any small
\delta>0,
there exists
t_{\delta}
such that for all
t\geq t_{\delta},

|\eta(t)|\leq\delta
and
|c_{1}(t)-c_{0}|\leq\delta.

Using Karamata’s presentation

\frac{\log L(t)}{\log t}=\frac{\log c_{1}(t)}{\log(t)}+\frac{1}{\log(t)}\Big[\int_{x_{0}}^{t_{\delta}}\frac{\eta(s)}{s}ds+\int_{t_{\delta}}^{t}\frac{\eta(s)}{s}ds\Big].

It follows that for large
t,
the first and second term in the RHS go to
0,
and the third term

\frac{1}{\log(t)}\int_{t_{\delta}}^{t}\frac{\eta(s)}{s}ds\leq\delta\frac{\log(t)-\log(t_{\delta})}{\log(t)}\leq\delta,

for any arbitrary
\delta>0.
Hence the assertion follows as
t\to\infty.

∎

18.5
Proof of Lemma
16.1

Proof.

To prove first assertion observe that for any
\delta>0,

\{\widetilde{X}_{1}>(1+\delta)x,\widetilde{X}_{2}<\delta x\}\subset\{\widetilde{X}_{1}-\widetilde{X}_{2}>x\}\subset\{\widetilde{X}_{1}>x\}.

These inequalities together imply that

\displaystyle 1

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle\frac{P\big[\widetilde{X}_{1}-\widetilde{X}_{2}>x\big]}{P[\widetilde{X}_{1}>x]}

(18.5)

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle\frac{P\big[\widetilde{X}_{1}>(1+\delta)x,\widetilde{X}_{2}<\delta x\big]}{P[\widetilde{X}_{1}>x]}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{P\big[\widetilde{X}_{1}>(1+\delta)x\big]}{P[\widetilde{X}_{1}>x]}\Big[1-P[\widetilde{X}_{2}\geq\delta x]\Big]=(1+\delta)^{-\alpha}[1-\delta^{-\alpha}O(x^{-\alpha})]+o(1),

where equality in (
18.5
) follows from the independence of
\{\widetilde{X}_{i}:i=1,\ldots,n\}
. Taking limit as
x\to\infty
and having
\delta>0
arbitrarily small proves the first assertion

P[\widetilde{X}_{1}-\widetilde{X}_{2}>x]\sim P[\widetilde{X}_{1}>x].

The assertion
P[\widetilde{X}_{1}-\widetilde{X}_{2}<-x]\sim P[\widetilde{X}_{2}>x]
follows similarly by changing signs. Second and third assertion follow as a corollary of Lemma 1.3.4 and Remark 1.3.5 of
[
49
]
respectively.
∎

18.6
Proof of
Lemma 12.4

Proof.

Define two functions
G_{1},G_{2}:\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}^{2}\to\mathbb{R},
such that

G_{1}(x,y)=\max\{\log x,\log y\},G_{2}(x,y)=\log\max\{x,y\}.

It is easy to see that

\displaystyle P[G_{1}(X,Y)=G_{2}(X,Y)]=P[G_{1}(X,Y)=G_{2}(X,Y),X>Y]

\displaystyle\quad+P[G_{1}(X,Y)=G_{2}(X,Y),X=Y]+P[G_{1}(X,Y)=G_{2}(X,Y),X<Y]

and each of these probabilities in the RHS are

\displaystyle P[G_{1}(X,Y)=G_{2}(X,Y),X>Y]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle P[X>Y],

\displaystyle P[G_{1}(X,Y)=G_{2}(X,Y),X<Y]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle P[X<Y],

\displaystyle P[G_{1}(X,Y)=G_{2}(X,Y),X=Y]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle P[X=Y]

and after summing one can see that
P[G_{1}(X,Y)=G_{2}(X,Y)]=1.

∎
